A Lagrangian shape and topology optimization framework based on semi-discrete optimal transport
C. Dapogny 1 , B. Levy 2 and E. Oudet 1

Abstract
. This article revolves around shape and topology optimization, in the applicative context where the objective and constraint functionals depend on the solution to a physical boundary value problem posed on the optimized domain. We introduce a novel numerical framework based on modern concepts from computational geometry, optimal transport and numerical analysis. Its pivotal feature is a representation of the optimized shape by the cells of an adapted version of a Laguerre diagram. Although such objects are originally described by a collection of seed points and weights, recent results from optimal transport theory suggest a more intuitive parametrization in terms of the seed points and measures of the associated cells. The discrete polygonal mesh of the shape induced by this diagram is a convenient support for the deployment of the Virtual Element Method for the numerical solution of the boundary value problem characterizing the physics at play and for the evaluation of the functionals of the domain involved in the optimization problem. The sensitivities of the latter are derived next; at first, we calculate their derivatives with respect to the positions of the vertices of the Laguerre diagram by shape calculus techniques; a suitable adjoint methodology is then developed to express them in terms of the seed points and cell measures of the diagram. The evolution of the shape through the optimization process is realized by first updating the design variables according to these sensitivities and then reconstructing the diagram thanks to efficient algorithms from computational geometry. Our shape and topology optimization strategy is versatile: it can be applied to a whole gammut of physical situations (such as thermal and structural mechanics) and optimal design settings (including single- and multi-phase problems). It is Lagrangian and body-fitted by essence, and it thereby benefits from all the assets of an explicit, meshed representation of the shape at each stage of the process. Yet, it naturally handles dramatic motions of the optimized shape, including topological changes, in a very robust fashion. These features, among others, are illustrated by a series of 2d numerical examples.



1
Univ. Grenoble Alpes, CNRS, Grenoble INP
1
1

1

Institute of Engineering Univ. Grenoble Alpes
, LJK, 38000 Grenoble, France

2
Centre Inria de Saclay, Université Paris Saclay, Laboratoire de Mathématiques d’Orsay
.

1.
Introduction and related works

Since the early implementations of optimal design,
the identification of a means to represent the shape
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
(
d=2
or
3
in practice)
which is amenable to all the operations of the numerical resolution has been a recurring issue – especially when physical applications are addressed, where the behavior of the optimized shape is modeled by boundary value problems, such as the conductivity or the linear elasticity equations.
It is indeed notoriously difficult to reconcile accurate simulations of these boundary value problems with a robust description of the evolution of
\Omega
through the optimization process.
The historical “Lagrangian” approaches for shape optimization hinge on a (simplicial) mesh of
\Omega
which is updated between the iterations of the workflow;
while this representation naturally offers a convenient support for mechanical computations via the Finite Element Method,
it makes it difficult to track the deformations of
\Omega
in a robust way, especially when they include topological changes
[
85
,
100
]
.
More recent solutions to carry out this thorny evolution operation resort to implicit, Eulerian representations of the shape
\Omega
. The most popular practice in this category is certainly to use the level set method
[
8
,
96
,
111
,
125
]
. The latter brings into play a fixed mesh
{\mathcal{T}}
of a large “hold-all” domain
D
: the optimized design
\Omega\subset D
is encoded into a so-called “level set function”
\phi:D\to{\mathbb{R}}
, discretized on
{\mathcal{T}}
in practice, which is negative (resp. positive) inside
\Omega
(resp. inside
D\setminus\overline{\Omega}
). Arbitrary updates of
\Omega
are accounted for via “simple” updates of
\phi
– for instance, small deformations of the boundary
\partial\Omega
translate into an advection or a Hamilton-Jacobi equation for
\phi
.
Unfortunately, this flexibility has a price: since no mesh of the shape
\Omega
is available, physical boundary value problems posed on
\Omega
have to be replaced
by approximate counterparts, posed on the total domain
D
, which are solved on the fixed mesh
{\mathcal{T}}
.
This practice often leverages a fictitious domain method, such as the ersatz material approximation in structural mechanics, see e.g.
[
3
]
; in any event, it results in an inaccurate numerical solution.
The main competing Eulerian strategy for optimal design, the so-called phase-field method, shares fairly similar spirit, assets and drawbacks
[
22
,
26
,
121
]
.
On the extreme side of this line of thinking, density-based topology optimization methods
[
17
]
, such as the popular Solid Isotropic Material with Penalization (SIMP) method in structural mechanics (see also
[
25
]
about artificial porosity methods in the context of fluid mechanics), downright relax the optimal design problem in terms of a “blurred” version
\rho
of the characteristic function of the optimized design, defined on (the mesh
{\mathcal{T}}
of)
D
and taking values in the interval
[0,1]
. Intuitively,
\rho
encodes the “local density” of material: regions where
\rho(\mathbf{x})\equiv 1
(resp.
\rho(\mathbf{x})\equiv 0
) are full (resp. devoid) of material and the intermediate values
\rho(\mathbf{x})\in(0,1)
represent regions filled by a microscopic arrangement of material and void in proportions
\rho(\mathbf{x})
and
(1-\rho(\mathbf{x}))
. The physical problem under scrutiny is approximated and reformulated to accommodate such a description of the design, notably by endowing regions where
\rho
takes intermediate values with fictitious material properties.
Last but not least, and without entering into details, let us eventually mention that several of the aforementioned approaches have been combined, see e.g.
[
4
,
5
,
6
]
for a concurrent use of the level set method with simplicial remeshing algorithms, or
[
35
]
for a quite sophisticated Lagrangian method using remeshing.

The present article introduces a novel framework for shape and topology optimization which is based on concepts and techniques from computational geometry and optimal transport.
This construction elaborates on the previous work
[
80
]
of one of the authors, taking place in the context of computational fluid dynamics,
and on its recent applications
[
74
,
91
,
92
]
in the field of cosmology, see also
[
43
]
about related ideas.
The cornerstone of our strategy is a representation of the optimized shape
\Omega
by the cells of a (modified version of a) Laguerre diagram,
a generalization of the well-known notion of Voronoi diagram in computational geometry
[
14
,
24
]
.
Although the natural definition of such objects involves a collection
\mathbf{s}=\left\{\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{N}\right\}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
of seed points and associated weights
{\bm{\psi}}=\left\{\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{N}\right\}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
,
we leverage recent results from optimal transport theory to parametrize them by the seed points
\mathbf{s}
and the measures
{\bm{\nu}}=\left\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{N}\right\}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
of the associated cells.
In the present applications, the shape
\Omega
is iteratively optimized with respect to an objective and constraint functions
J(\Omega)
and
\mathbf{G}(\Omega)
. Their evaluation and that of their derivatives, which in turn determine the velocity field governing the evolution of
\Omega
through the process, raise the need to solve one or several boundary value problems posed on
\Omega
; this is a major additional difficulty with respect to the aforementioned works
[
80
,
74
,
91
,
92
]
,
where heuristic simplifications made it possible to express the velocity field for
\Omega
in terms of geometric quantities of the diagram only.
In order to solve these boundary value problems, we take advantage of the tessellation of
\Omega
into convex polytopes induced by its diagram representation; this offers a convenient support for the use of the Virtual Element Method – a recent variant of the classical Finite Element Method adapted to polygonal meshes, see e.g.
[
12
]
.
The calculation of the sensitivities of the optimization criteria
J(\Omega)
and
\mathbf{G}(\Omega)
with respect to the seed points
\mathbf{s}
and cell measures
{\bm{\nu}}
of the diagram is realized in two steps:
we first apply shape calculus techniques to compute their sensitivities with respect to the vertices of the diagram and we next employ a suitable adjoint method to reformulate them in terms of
\mathbf{s}
and
{\bm{\nu}}
.
A descent direction for the optimization problem is then inferred from this information,
and the parameters
\mathbf{s}
and
{\bm{\nu}}
of the Laguerre diagram for
\Omega
are updated; the new diagram associated to the resulting seed points and weights is generated, revealing the next iterate of the shape
\Omega
.
This strategy is Lagrangian by nature since the evolution of
\Omega
is realized by tracking the motion of the defining seed points; it consistently features an exact, body-fitted description of the shape
\Omega
.
In the meantime, it leaves the room for a robust account of dramatic deformations between iterations, including topological changes: the deformations of (the mesh of)
\Omega
are never tracked explicitly; rather, the shape is rediscovered after each iteration, once the defining Laguerre diagram has been calculated anew.

As reflected by the previous discussion, one originality of our approach is that it leverages recent concepts and techniques pertaining to quite different fields:
shapes are represented via Laguerre diagrams, a notion from computational geometry and optimal transport; the Virtual Element Method from numerical analysis is used for the solution of the physical boundary value problems at play on polygonal meshes; eventually, fine techniques from shape and topology optimization are used to orchestrate the optimal design process.

To the best of our knowledge, the present work is the first one using all these ingredients together even though, admittedly some of them have already been combined in the literature. Voronoi-like diagrams, polygonal meshes, and numerical methods dedicated to this type of structures
have been considered in the perspective of optimal design or more generally interface motion tracking, as we now briefly overview, without exhaustivity.
In
[
58
]
, the cells of a Voronoi diagram are used to represent the many individual entities of a granular medium. The collection is subjected to the viscous, elastic and contact forces between cells, which are modeled by a finite-dimensional system of ordinary differential equations.
The contributions
[
118
,
119
]
arise in the field of fluid dynamics;
the 2d polygonal mesh induced by a Voronoi diagram is used as computational support for the finite volume calculations involved in the simulation of the flow, notably for the reconstruction of the fluxes at the interface between cells. Although this discretization is not fitted with the surface of the fluid, the defining seed points evolve according to the flow velocity.
A high-order version of this strategy was proposed in
[
56
]
, see also
[
49
]
for another contribution in this setting.
Still in the context of fluid mechanics, the article
[
43
]
associates a Laguerre diagram to a collection of fluid particles;
its weights are adjusted to take into account the compressibility or incompressibility of the fluid.
A simple, approximate numerical solver based on discrete differential operators allows to compute the velocity of the fluid.
This framework has been recently revisited in
[
103
]
, where entropy-regularized transport plans are used to ease the calculation of the Laguerre cell attached to each particle.
It can be adapted to the case of free surfaces, owing to a modified version of this entropy-regularized optimal transport formulation and of the Sinkhorn algorithm used to calculate the transport plan.
Voronoi diagrams appear more systematically as the pivotal ingredient of the so-called Voronoi Implicit Interface Method introduced in
[
108
,
109
]
,
see also
[
129
]
for a mathematical analysis in the context of multiphase motion driven by curvature.
Briefly, a partition of a fixed domain into multiple phases is represented via the Voronoi diagram generated by a set of seed objects (and not mere seed points).
The evolution of these phases is achieved by first calculating the domains induced by the
\varepsilon
-offset of the collection of edges (in 2d) or faces (in 3d) of the diagram,
then by realizing their evolution via the “classical” level set method.
The updated collection of phases is retrieved as the Voronoi diagram of the resulting objects.
Beyond the field of computational physics, Voronoi diagrams have inspired popular formats for shapes in applicative disciplines such as computer graphics, see e.g. the article
[
2
]
where the motion of a shape is tracked by introducing a clipped Voronoi tessellation of a template shape, whose cells are rigidly deformed to match a target.

Closer to the setting of the present article, the specific features of Voronoi diagrams have inspired various original representations of the shape in optimal design.
The recent contribution
[
51
]
is dedicated to trusses, i.e. networks of bars.
The considered structures are represented by the edges of a Voronoi diagram contained in a fixed “hold-all” domain
D\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
;
when it comes to calculating their elastic behavior, a density function
\rho:D\to[0,1]
is generated, featuring a continuous interpolation between the value
1
inside the bars and that
0
in void.
This function is parametrized by the seed points of the diagram, and the optimal design problem is solved in terms of these variables.
A quite similar strategy is used in the article
[
77
]
, dealing with the optimization of foams.

The use of a polygonal computational mesh of the shape
\Omega
or a larger hold-all domain
D
is not extraneous to the field of shape and topology optimization, either.
For instance, the contributions
[
122
,
11
,
33
]
rely on a density topology optimization method, where the hold-all domain
D
is equipped with a fixed polygonal mesh serving as the support for all the mechanical computations of the workflow, which are performed by means of the Virtual Element Method.
The added value of the use of the Virtual Element Method on a general polygonal mesh with respect to the more classical practice of the Finite Element Method on a simplicial mesh hinges on its reported superior stability properties.
To the best of our knowledge, only a few recent contributions leverage the greater flexibility of general polygonal meshes over simplicial meshes to propose shape and topology optimization methods featuring a body-fitted discretization of the optimized shape. In
[
89
,
90
]
, the authors rely on the level set method; a level set function
\phi:D\to{\mathbb{R}}
for the shape
\Omega
is defined on a fixed hexahedral mesh of the computational domain
D
and at each iteration, the
0
isosurface of
\phi
is explicitly discretized in this mesh, thus revealing a polygonal mesh of the shape. Mechanical computations are performed on this computational support for
\Omega
thanks to a moving least-square approximation, which allows to calculate the shape derivative of the minimized function of the domain.
A similar strategy is used in
[
54
]
, where a polygonal mesh of the computational domain
D
is used and the level set function
\phi:D\to{\mathbb{R}}
for the shape
\Omega
is represented as a discontinuous Finite Element function.
Again, at each iteration of the process, the
0
level set of
\phi
for the shape
\Omega
is explicitly discretized in the mesh of
D
, which results in a new, non conforming polygonal mesh of the latter,
on which Finite Element analyses can be conducted by a discontinuous Galerkin solver. The topological derivative of the minimized function of the domain is calculated and the level set function
\phi
is updated from this information. Let us eventually mention the related, very recent work
[
53
]
where a very fine simplicial mesh of
D
is used to update the level set function, and a coarse polygonal mesh is generated when it comes to mechanical computations, whose elements are agglomerations of the simplices of the fine mesh.

To conclude this brief overview of related works, let us point out that, from the conceptual viewpoint,
our shape and topology optimization framework shares similarities with that developed in
[
71
,
20
]
.
In there, a decomposition of the computational domain
D
into
K
phases is described by a so-called minimization diagram. The latter is attached to a collection
\left\{\phi_{k}\right\}_{k=1,\ldots,K}
of scalar functions
\phi_{k}:D\to{\mathbb{R}}
, in such a way that
each phase
k=1,\ldots,K
is made of those points
\mathbf{x}\in D
where
\phi_{k}(\mathbf{x})
is minimum among
\phi_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\ldots,\phi_{k}(\mathbf{x})
.
The motion of the boundary between each phase is accounted for by a quite similar mechanism as in the context of the level set method.
We also mention the recent contribution
[
21
]
about the reconstruction of a potential in an inverse problem, which is piecewise constant with respect to the cells of a Voronoi diagram.

The present article focuses on the exposition of our shape and topology optimization framework in two space dimensions,
where its main ingredients can be conveniently described with a minimum level of technicality.
There is no conceptual obstruction to the three-dimensional extension of this work, but only an increase in the implementation burden.
Indeed, the chief tools involved – notably, the calculation of Laguerre diagrams and the Virtual Element Method – are already available in this situation.
To emphasize this fact, our presentation takes place in the general,
d
dimensional case whenever it is possible without jeopardizing with clarity.
On a different note, as we have already noted, our strategy leverages concepts and algorithms pertaining to disciplines which seldom appear together in the literature:
computational geometry, via the handling of Laguerre diagrams,
numerical analysis for the simulation of physical phenomena (and notably, the Virtual Element Method), and optimal design for the notions of shape and topological derivatives, the adjoint method, etc.
In order to ensure a relatively self-contained presentation, we provide a reasonable amount of background material about these ingredients, including proofs when they help intuition or when they are not readily available in the precise context of interest.
Our implementation essentially relies on open-source libraries, and we aim to make it available in a short future, together with associated tutorials.
We believe that the methods introduced in this work will find useful applications beyond shape optimization, e.g. in computational physics.

The remainder of this article is organized as follows.
The next
Section

2
sets the scene of the shape optimization problems analyzed in this article.
In particular, we introduce the main notation used throughout and recall a few basic notions about shape optimization.

Section

3
is devoted to the numerical representation of our shapes in terms of the seed points and cell measures of a Laguerre diagram;
the general shape optimization workflow adopted in this context is then presented.
The subsequent
Sections

4
,
5
and
6
are more in-depth presentations of the main operations involved therein.

Section

4
deals with the geometric computations attached to Laguerre diagrams involved in our framework, and notably their construction, cell regularization, resampling, etc.
Section

5
sketches the implementation of the Virtual Element Method for the solution of the conductivity equation and the linear elasticity system. Eventually,
Section

6
details the calculation of the objective and constraint functionals featured in our shape optimization problem and their sensitivities with respect to the parameters of our shape representation, namely the seed points and cell measures of the representing diagram.
A series of numerical examples is presented and discussed in
Section

7
to showcase the main features of our approaches.
In
Section

8
, we draw a few conclusions about this strategy and we outline natural perspectives for future work.
The article ends with a series of appendices, devoted to the proofs of some technical results which are not completely new, but are adaptations of existing results,
or known facts which are not easily found under the needed form in the literature. It also contains several useful implementation details.

2.
Presentation of the shape optimization problem

Our main purpose in this article is to optimize shapes, that is, bounded, Lipschitz domains
\Omega
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, where
d=2,3
in practice.
We consider optimization problems of the general form:

(
P
)

\min\limits_{\Omega}\>J(\Omega)\>\>\text{ s.t. } \mathbf{G}(\Omega)=0,

where
J(\Omega)
is an objective criterion,
and
\mathbf{G}(\Omega):=(G_{1}(\Omega),\ldots,G_{p}(\Omega))
is a collection of
p
scalar-valued equality constraint functionals.

Elementary examples of shape functionals are the volume
\text{\rm Vol}(\Omega)
and the perimeter
\text{\rm Per}(\Omega)
, defined by:

(2.1)

\text{\rm Vol}(\Omega)=\int_{\Omega}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x},\text{ and } \text{\rm Per}(\Omega)=\int_{\partial\Omega}\>\text{\rm d}s.

More involved instances about
J(\Omega)
and
\mathbf{G}(\Omega)
depend on the physical behavior of
\Omega
via a so-called state function
u_{\Omega}
,
which is the solution to a boundary value problem posed on
\Omega
.

After setting a few notations about calculus in
Section

2.1
, we describe in
Section

2.2
the applicative situations considered in the present article, namely those of conductive media and of elastic structures.
Eventually, in
Section

2.3
, we recall a few facts about differentiation with respect to the domain.

2.1.
Notations

Throughout this article, vectors
\mathbf{x}
in a Euclidean space
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
appear in bold face, and their components are denoted by
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
. Moreover, we rely on the following conventions:

•

For
N\geq 1
, the Euclidean space
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
is equipped with its canonical basis
\left\{\mathbf{e}_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}
.
The attached Euclidean inner product is denoted by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
, and sometimes only with a
\cdot
, and the norm is denoted by
\lvert\cdot\lvert
.
We also introduce the supremum norm:

\lvert\mathbf{x}\lvert_{\infty}:=\sup\limits_{i=1,\ldots,N}\lvert x_{i}\lvert,\quad\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}.

•

The open ball in
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
with center
\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
and radius
r>0
is denoted by
B(\mathbf{x},r)
.

•

Let
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a (smooth enough) scalar function and let
\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
;
we denote by
\frac{\partial f}{\partial\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{x})
the derivative of
f
at
\mathbf{x}
, i.e.
\frac{\partial f}{\partial\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{x}):{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is the linear mapping giving rise to the expansion:

f(\mathbf{x}+\widehat{\mathbf{x}})=f(\mathbf{x})+\frac{\partial f}{\partial\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{x})(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})+\text{\rm o}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}}),\text{ where }\frac{\text{\rm o}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})}{|\widehat{\mathbf{x}}|}\to 0\text{ as } \widehat{\mathbf{x}}\to 0.

The gradient
\nabla f(\mathbf{x})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
of
f
at
\mathbf{x}
is the Riesz representative of this linear mapping, that is:

\forall\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N},\quad\langle\nabla f(\mathbf{x}),\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\rangle=\frac{\partial f}{\partial\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{x})(\widehat{\mathbf{x}}).

When needed, we shall explicitly indicate in the operator
\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}
the variables
\mathbf{x}
involved in the derivative.

•

Still considering a (smooth enough) scalar function
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, the Hessian matrix
\left[\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x})\right]
of
f
at
\mathbf{x}
is the
N\times N
matrix with entries:

\left[\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{x})\right]_{ij}=\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}}(\mathbf{x}),\quad i,j=1,\ldots,N.

•

For
M,N\geq 1
, the derivative of a smooth enough vector-valued function
{\bm{\psi}}=(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{M}):{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
is the linear mapping
\frac{\partial{\bm{\psi}}}{\partial\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{x}):{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
featured in the expansion

{\bm{\psi}}(\mathbf{x}+\widehat{\mathbf{x}})={\bm{\psi}}(\mathbf{x})+\frac{\partial{\bm{\psi}}}{\partial\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{x})(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})+\text{\rm o}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}}),\text{ where }\frac{\text{\rm o}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})}{|\widehat{\mathbf{x}}|}\to 0\text{ as } \widehat{\mathbf{x}}\to 0.

The
M\times N
matrix
\nabla{\bm{\psi}}(\mathbf{x})
associated to this derivative – sometimes also denoted by
\left[\nabla{\bm{\psi}}(\mathbf{x})\right]
for clarity – is that with entries:

\left[\nabla{\bm{\psi}}(\mathbf{x})\right]_{ij}=\frac{\partial\psi_{i}}{\partial x_{j}}(\mathbf{x}),\quad i=1,\ldots,M,\>\>j=1,\ldots,N.

As a result, the following relation holds true:

\forall\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N},\quad\frac{\partial{\bm{\psi}}}{\partial\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{x})(\widehat{\mathbf{x}})=\left[\nabla{\bm{\psi}}(\mathbf{x})\right]\widehat{\mathbf{x}},

where the right-hand side features the matrix-vector product between the
M\times N
matrix
\left[\nabla{\bm{\psi}}(\mathbf{x})\right]
and the vector
\widehat{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
.
Note the (classical and hopefully harmless) ambiguity of these notations in the case
M=1
where
\nabla\psi(\mathbf{x})
is a vector with size
N
and
\left[\nabla{\bm{\psi}}(\mathbf{x})\right]
is the transpose
1\times N
matrix.

More specific notations will be introduced when needed, later in the article.

2.2.
Mathematical models for conductive media and elastic structures

In this section, we introduce the two physical applications of our shape optimization problems. We refer to classical treaties about continuum mechanics such as
[
123
]
for further details, see also
[
63
]
about linear elasticity.

2.2.1.
Optimal design of conductive media

Let the shape
\Omega
stand for an electric conductor in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
(
d=2
or
3
).
It is filled by a material with smooth conductivity
\gamma\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,
satisfying the following ellipticity condition:

\exists\>\>0<\underline{\gamma}\leq\overline{\gamma}<\infty\>\>\text{ s.t. }\forall\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\quad\underline{\gamma}\leq\gamma(\mathbf{x})\leq\overline{\gamma}.

The boundary
\partial\Omega
is maintained at fixed potential
0
.
Introducing a source
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, the voltage potential
u_{\Omega}
inside
\Omega

is the unique solution in
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
to the following boundary value problem:

(
Cond
)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}-\text{\rm div}(\gamma\nabla u_{\Omega})=f&\text{in } \Omega,\\
u_{\Omega}=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega.\end{array}\right.

The associated variational formulation reads:

(2.2)

\text{Search for }u_{\Omega}\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)\>\>\text{ s.t. }\forall v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega),\quad\int_{\Omega}\gamma\nabla u_{\Omega}\cdot\nabla v\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}=\int_{\Omega}fv\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}.

In this context, a typical objective function measuring the performance of
\Omega
is the compliance

(2.3)

C(\Omega)=\int_{\Omega}\gamma\lvert\nabla u_{\Omega}\lvert^{2}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}=\int_{\Omega}fu_{\Omega}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x},

which appraises the energy stored inside the shape
\Omega
, or equivalently, the work done by the source.
One may also be interested in minimizing the first eigenvalue
\lambda_{\Omega}^{1}
(resp. the
k^{\text{th}}
eigenvalue
\lambda_{\Omega}^{k}
) of the operator
-\text{\rm div}(\gamma\nabla\cdot)
on
\Omega
equipped with Dirichlet boundary conditions:
\lambda_{\Omega}^{1}
is the smallest positive number
\lambda
(resp. the
k^{\text{th}}
smallest positive number) for which there exists a non trivial function
u\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
satisfying

(2.4)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}-\text{\rm div}(\gamma\nabla u)=\lambda u&\text{in } \Omega,\\
u=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega.\end{array}\right.

From the physical viewpoint,
\lambda_{\Omega}^{1}
accounts for the minimum energy of a potential
u
with unit squared amplitude, as reflected by the classical Rayleigh quotient formula:

(2.5)

\lambda_{\Omega}^{1}=\min\limits_{u\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega),\atop u\neq 0}\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\gamma\lvert\nabla u\lvert^{2}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}}{\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}u^{2}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}}.

Remark 2.1
.

We shall also consider a two-phase variant of this setting,
where the shape
\Omega
represents one phase made of a material with conductivity
\gamma_{1}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
within a fixed domain
D
made of a material with different conductivity
\gamma_{0}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.
One then aims to optimize the repartition
D=\Omega\cup(D\setminus\overline{\Omega})
of both materials within
D
with respect to, e.g. the compliance of the total device
D
; see
Section

7.3
for a numerical example in this setting.

2.2.2.
Optimization of the shape of elastic structures

In this section,

\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
stands for a mechanical structure, whose boundary is divided into three disjoint pieces:

\partial\Omega=\overline{\Gamma_{D}} \cup\overline{\Gamma_{N}}\cup\overline{\Gamma}.

In this decomposition,

•

The shape
\Omega
is clamped on
\Gamma_{D}
;

•

Surface loads
\mathbf{g}:\Gamma_{N}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
are applied on
\Gamma_{N}
;

•

The region
\Gamma
is free of applied efforts.

In practice,
\Gamma_{D}
and
\Gamma_{N}
are often imposed by the context,
so that only the free boundary
\Gamma
is subject to optimization.
Introducing (smooth) body forces
\mathbf{f}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
representing e.g. gravity, the displacement
\mathbf{u}_{\Omega}
of
\Omega
belongs to the space

H^{1}_{\Gamma_{D}}(\Omega)^{d}:=\left\{\mathbf{u}\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{d},\>\>\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{0}\text{ on }\Gamma_{D}\right\}.

It is the unique solution in the latter to the linearized elasticity system:

(
Elas
)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}-\text{\rm div}(Ae(\mathbf{u}_{\Omega}))=\mathbf{f}&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\mathbf{u}_{\Omega}=\mathbf{0}&\text{on }\Gamma_{D},\\
Ae(\mathbf{u}_{\Omega})\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{g}&\text{on }\Gamma_{N},\\
Ae(\mathbf{u}_{\Omega})\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{0}&\text{on }\Gamma.\end{array}\right.

Here,
e(\mathbf{u}):=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\mathbf{u}+\nabla\mathbf{u}^{T})
is the linearized strain tensor induced y a vector field
\mathbf{u}:\Omega\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
The material properties of the elastic medium are encoded in the Hooke’s tensor
A
, which reads:

\text{For all symmetric matrix }\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d},\quad A\xi=2\mu\xi+\lambda\text{\rm tr}(\xi)\text{\rm I},

where
\lambda>0
and
\mu>0
are the Lamé coefficients of the material.
The boundary value problem
Elas
rewrites, under variational form:

(2.6)

\text{Search for }\mathbf{u}_{\Omega}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{D}}(\Omega)^{d}\>\>\text{ s.t. }\forall\mathbf{v}\in H^{1}_{\Gamma_{D}}(\Omega)^{d},\>\>\int_{\Omega}Ae(\mathbf{u}_{\Omega}):e(\mathbf{v})\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}=\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathbf{v}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}+\int_{\Gamma_{N}}\mathbf{g}\cdot\mathbf{v}\>\text{\rm d}s.

The shape optimization problem
P
under scrutiny in the present context of mechanical engineering may for instance consist in minimizing the compliance
J(\Omega)=C(\Omega)
of the structure as a means to maximize its rigidity,

(2.7)

C(\Omega):=\int_{\Omega}Ae(\mathbf{u}_{\Omega}):e(\mathbf{u}_{\Omega})\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}=\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathbf{u}_{\Omega}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}+\int_{\Gamma_{N}}\mathbf{g}\cdot\mathbf{u}_{\Omega}\>\text{\rm d}s,

under a volume constraint
G(\Omega)=\text{\rm Vol}(\Omega)-V_{T}
, where
V_{T}
is a volume target.

Remark 2.2
.

For simplicity of the presentation, we have assumed that all the data of the models of the previous and present sections (the domain
\Omega
, the forces
\mathbf{f}
,
\mathbf{g}
, etc.) are smooth. This framework is by no means the minimal one guaranteeing the well-posedness of these models, and the validity of our developments.

2.3.
Shape optimization in a nutshell

The mathematical analysis and numerical resolution of a shape optimization problem of the form
P
hinges on the sensitivities of the objective and constraint functions
J(\Omega)
and
\mathbf{G}(\Omega)
with respect to the domain
\Omega
–
a notion which can be appraised in various ways.
In this work, we mainly rely on the boundary variation method of Hadamard, see for instance
[
9
,
66
,
86
,
114
]
.
The latter is based on variations of a given shape
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
of the form:

\Omega_{\bm{\theta}}:=(\text{\rm Id}+{\bm{\theta}})(\Omega),\>\>{\bm{\theta}}\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d}),\>\>||{\bm{\theta}}||_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})}<1.

Loosely speaking,
\Omega_{\bm{\theta}}
is obtained by moving the points of
\Omega
according to the “small” vector field
{\bm{\theta}}
, see
Fig.

1
.

Figure 1
.
Variation
\Omega_{\bm{\theta}}
of a shape
\Omega
in the sense of Hadamard’s method.

One function
F(\Omega)
of the domain is called shape differentiable at a particular shape
\Omega
when the underlying mapping
{\bm{\theta}}\mapsto F(\Omega_{\bm{\theta}})
,
defined from a neighborhood of
\mathbf{0}
in
W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
into
{\mathbb{R}}
, is Fréchet differentiable at
\mathbf{0}
. Its derivative
{\bm{\theta}}\mapsto F^{\prime}(\Omega)({\bm{\theta}})
is called the shape derivative of
F
at
\Omega

and it satisfies the following expansion:

F(\Omega_{\bm{\theta}})=F(\Omega)+F^{\prime}(\Omega)({\bm{\theta}})+\text{\rm o}({\bm{\theta}}),\text{ where }\frac{\text{\rm o}({\bm{\theta}})}{||{\bm{\theta}}||_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})}}\xrightarrow{{\bm{\theta}}\to\mathbf{0}}0.

Intuitively, a “small” perturbation of
\Omega
following a “descent direction”
{\bm{\theta}}
, i.e. a deformation such that
F^{\prime}(\Omega)({\bm{\theta}})<0
, for a short pseudo-time step
\tau>0
produces a shape
\Omega_{\tau{\bm{\theta}}}
with a “better” performance (i.e. a lower value), as measured in terms of
F
:

F(\Omega_{\tau{\bm{\theta}}})\approx F(\Omega)+\tau F^{\prime}(\Omega)({\bm{\theta}})<F(\Omega).

Simple examples of shape derivatives are those of the volume and perimeter functionals defined in
2.1
.
Under mild assumptions that we omit for brevity, these read:

(2.8)

\text{\rm Vol}^{\prime}(\Omega)({\bm{\theta}})=\int_{\Omega}\text{\rm div}({\bm{\theta}})\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}=\int_{\partial\Omega}{\bm{\theta}}\cdot\mathbf{n}\>\text{\rm d}s,\text{ and }\text{\rm Per}^{\prime}(\Omega)({\bm{\theta}})=\int_{\partial\Omega}\kappa\>{\bm{\theta}}\cdot\mathbf{n}\>\text{\rm d}s,

where
\kappa:\partial\Omega\to{\mathbb{R}}
is the mean curvature of
\partial\Omega
.

The analysis of the “physical” functionals of interest in this article is more intricate, as they involve the solution
u_{\Omega}
to a boundary value problem posed on
\Omega
, see e.g.
2.3
and
2.7
.
Nevertheless, the classical adjoint method allows to calculate their shape derivatives in closed form, in terms of
u_{\Omega}
and possibly of an adjoint state
p_{\Omega}
, solution to a boundary value problem similar to that for
u_{\Omega}
.
Although it is non trivial, this technical issue is well-known in the literature,
and we do not enter into details, referring to e.g.
[
7
,
78
]
, and also
[
101
]
for a comprehensive introduction.

Remark 2.3
.

The adjoint method will be deployed (and detailed) in
Section

6.2
below to achieve another purpose, that of converting a derivative with respect to the vertices of a Laguerre diagram into derivatives with respect to its seed points and cell measures.

For further reference, let us note that the shape derivative of a generic shape functional
F(\Omega)
has often two different, albeit equivalent structures:

•

A volume form

(2.9)

F^{\prime}(\Omega)({\bm{\theta}})=\int_{\Omega}(\mathbf{t}_{\Omega}\cdot{\bm{\theta}}+S_{\Omega}:\nabla{\bm{\theta}})\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x},

featuring an integral over the domain
\Omega
of the deformation
{\bm{\theta}}
and its derivative, and some vector- and matrix-valued functions
\mathbf{t}_{\Omega}:\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
S_{\Omega}:\Omega\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d}
depending on the function
F(\Omega)
.

•

A surface form

(2.10)

F^{\prime}(\Omega)({\bm{\theta}})=\int_{\partial\Omega}v_{\Omega}\>{\bm{\theta}}\cdot\mathbf{n}\>\text{\rm d}s,

involving a scalar field
v_{\Omega}:\partial\Omega\to{\mathbb{R}}
which depends on
F(\Omega)
.

The surface expression
2.10
is often deemed more appealing: it indeed reflects the natural fact that the shape derivative of a “regular enough” objective function depends only on the normal component of the deformation
{\bm{\theta}}
on
\partial\Omega
, see for instance
[
47
,
66
]
about the so-called Structure theorem for shape derivatives. Besides, it easily lends itself to the identification of a descent direction for
F(\Omega)
, as letting
{\bm{\theta}}=-v_{\Omega}\mathbf{n}
on
\partial\Omega
straightforwardly ensures that
F^{\prime}(\Omega)({\bm{\theta}})<0
.
However, the volume form
2.9
is usually more suitable for mathematical analysis, see e.g.
[
68
,
61
]
.

Remark 2.4
.

The deformations
{\bm{\theta}}
considered in the practice of the method of Hadamard are often restricted to a subset of
W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which is for instance made of vector fields with higher regularity, or vector fields vanishing on a fixed, non optimizable region of space.

Remark 2.5
.

The notion of shape derivative is not the only means to understand differentiation with respect to the domain.
The alternative concept of topological derivative appraises the sensitivity of a function
F(\Omega)
with respect to the nucleation of a “small” hole inside
\Omega
.
It relies on variations of
\Omega
of the form:

\Omega_{\mathbf{x},r}:=\Omega\setminus\overline{B(\mathbf{x},r)},\text{ where }\mathbf{x}\in\Omega\text{ and }r\ll 1.

The function
F(\Omega)
is then said to have a topological derivative
\text{\rm d}_{T}F(\Omega)(\mathbf{x})
at
\mathbf{x}\in\Omega
if the following expansion holds:

F(\Omega_{\mathbf{x},r})=F(\Omega)+r^{d}\text{\rm d}_{T}F(\Omega)(\mathbf{x})+\text{\rm o}(r^{d}),\text{ where }\frac{\text{\rm o}(r^{d})}{r^{d}}\xrightarrow{r\to 0}0.

We refer to
[
60
,
113
]
for the seminal contributions about topological derivatives and to
[
10
,
94
]
for recent overviews.

3.
Discretization and evolution of shapes using modified Laguerre diagrams

This section details the representation of shapes adopted in our framework.
The first
Section

3.1
introduces basic notions about Laguerre diagrams, in their original acceptation and in the modified version introduced in
[
80
]
.
In the next
Section

3.2
, we explain how such objects can be conveniently parametrized by the seed points and the measures of their cells.
Finally,
Section

3.3
sketches our optimization strategy, whose most critical operations are more extensively described in the next parts of the article.

3.1.
Representation of shapes via Laguerre diagrams

Let
D\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be a fixed bounded and Lipschitz domain containing all the shapes of interest.
We start with the classical definitions of Voronoi and Laguerre diagrams, the latter being more general, weighted versions of the former; we refer to
[
14
,
24
]
about these classical notions from computational geometry.

Definition 3.1
.

•

Let
\mathbf{s}=\left\{\mathbf{s}_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
be a collection of seed points.
The Voronoi diagram
\mathbf{Vor}(\mathbf{s})
induced by
\mathbf{s}
is the following decomposition of
\overline{D}
:

(3.1)

\overline{D}=\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{N}\text{\rm Vor}_{i}(\mathbf{s}),

where for
i=1,\ldots,N
, the Voronoi cell
\text{\rm Vor}_{i}(\mathbf{s})
is defined by:

(3.2)

\text{\rm Vor}_{i}(\mathbf{s}):=\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\overline{D},\>\>|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|^{2}\leq|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{j}|^{2},\>\>\forall j\neq i\right\}.

•

Let
\mathbf{s}=\left\{\mathbf{s}_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
be a collection of seed points and
{\bm{\psi}}=\left\{\psi_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
be a set of associated weights.
The (classical) Laguerre diagram
\mathbf{Lag}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
attached to
\mathbf{s}
and
{\bm{\psi}}
is the following decomposition of
\overline{D}
:

(3.3)

\overline{D}=\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{N}\text{\rm Lag}_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}),

where for
i=1,\ldots,N
, the Laguerre cell
\text{\rm Lag}_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
reads:

(3.4)

\text{\rm Lag}_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}):=\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\overline{D},\>\>|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|^{2}-\psi_{i}\leq|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{j}|^{2}-\psi_{j},\>\>\forall j\neq i\right\}.

The concept of Laguerre diagram paves the way to a way of representing a shape
\Omega\subset D
, as a subset of the cells of a diagram of the form
3.3
and
3.4
, associated to suitably chosen seed points
\mathbf{s}
and weights
{\bm{\psi}}
:

(3.5)

\overline{\Omega}=\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{K}\text{\rm Lag}_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}),\text{ where }K<N,

and the cells
\text{\rm Lag}_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
,
i=K+1,\ldots,N
form a decomposition of the void phase
D\setminus\overline{\Omega}
.
In addition to this option, we shall also rely on another point of view, introduced in
[
80
]
, which leverages a slightly modified version of the notion of Laguerre diagram.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
\Omega\subset D
be a shape; two sets
\mathbf{s}=\left\{\mathbf{s}_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
and
{\bm{\psi}}=\left\{\psi_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
of seed points and weights
generate a (modified) Laguerre diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
for
\Omega
if the following decomposition holds:

(3.6)

\overline{\Omega}=\bigcup\limits_{i=1}^{N}V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}),

where each closed cell
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
is obtained by intersection of the corresponding Laguerre cell
\text{\rm Lag}_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
in
3.4
with the ball centered at
\mathbf{s}_{i}
with radius
\psi_{i}^{1/2}
:

(3.7)

V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})=\text{\rm Lag}_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})\cap\overline{B(\mathbf{s}_{i},\psi_{i}^{1/2})}.

Both types of decomposition
3.5
and
3.6
will be used in the present work to represent a shape
\Omega\subset D
. The analyses of their mathematical properties are very similar,
and since “classical” diagrams of the form
3.3
and
3.4
are more familiar in the literature (although not in the present shape and topology optimization context), we essentially focus our presentation on the modified diagrams of
Definition

3.2
, pointing out the differences with their classical counterparts when relevant.

The next statement draws the main geometric features of the representation
3.6
and sets related notations used throughout the sequel, see
Fig.

2
.
For simplicity, it is only provided in the case of two space dimensions.
Its elementary proof is completely similar to that of the corresponding statement for “classical” Laguerre diagrams
3.3
, about which we refer to e.g.
[
14
]
,
and it is omitted for brevity.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
d=2
, and let
\Omega\subset D
be a shape defined via the diagram
3.6
associated to seed points
\mathbf{s}=\left\{\mathbf{s}_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}
and weights
{\bm{\psi}}=\left\{\psi_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}
.
The following facts hold true:

(i)

The cells
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
in
3.7
are closed, bounded and convex subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

(ii)

The intersection
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})\cap V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
between two different cells
i\neq j
is either empty, or a point or a (closed) straight segment
\mathbf{e}_{ij}
orthogonal to
\mathbf{s}_{i}\mathbf{s}_{j}
.

(iii)

When the intersection
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})\cap V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
is a segment with positive length,
V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
is called a neighbor of
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
.
The index set of the neighbors of the
i^{\text{th}}
cell is denoted by
{\mathcal{N}}_{i}\subset\left\{1,\ldots,i-1,i+1,\ldots,N\right\}
.

(iv)

We denote by
{\mathcal{E}}\subset\left\{1,\ldots,N\right\}^{2}
the set of neighboring pairs of cells in the diagram
3.6
, that is,
(i,j)\in{\mathcal{E}}
if
j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{i}
(and so
i\in{\mathcal{N}}_{j}
).

(v)

For each
i=1,\ldots,N
, the boundary
\partial V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
can be decomposed as:

\partial V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})=\left(\bigcup\limits_{j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{i}}\mathbf{e}_{ij}\right)\cup{\mathcal{C}}_{i}\cup{\mathcal{L}}_{i},

where

•

For
j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{i}
,
\mathbf{e}_{ij}:=\partial V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})\cap\partial V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
is a line segment;

•

{\mathcal{C}}_{i}=\partial\Omega\cap\partial V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
is a (possibly empty) collection of circular arcs;

•

{\mathcal{L}}_{i}=\partial D\cap\partial V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
is a (possibly empty) collection of line segments.

(vi)

The endpoints of the (straight or curvilinear) pieces of this representation are called the vertices of the diagram
3.6
.
We denote by
\mathbf{q}:=\left\{\mathbf{q}_{j}\right\}_{j=1,\ldots,M}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2M}
the collection of these
M
vertices.

Figure 2
.
Representation
3.6
of the shape
\Omega\subset D
as the modified Laguerre diagram
3.6
associated to the seed points
\mathbf{s}=\left\{\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{N}\right\}
and weights
{\bm{\psi}}=\left\{\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{N}\right\}
.

Remark 3.1
.

Although the seed points of a Voronoi diagram obviously belong to the interior of their respective cells, the defining seed points of a Laguerre diagram
may lie far outside;
actually, they may even lie outside the computational domain
D
although, for implementation stability purposes, we shall impose that they remain inside the latter, see
Section

6.3
.

To illustrate this point, let us consider a collection of seed points
\mathbf{s}=\left\{\mathbf{s}_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
.
Let
\mathbf{h}
be an arbitrary vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
; we introduce the collection of seed points
\widetilde{\mathbf{s}}=\left\{\mathbf{s}_{i}+\mathbf{h}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}
resulting from a common translation of
\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{N}
by
\mathbf{h}
and the weight vector
\widetilde{{\bm{\psi}}}
given by
\widetilde{\psi}_{i}=2\mathbf{s}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{h}
,
i=1,\ldots,N
. Then, for any index
i=1,\ldots,N
and any point
\mathbf{x}\in D
, a simple calculation reveals that:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbf{x}\in\text{\rm Lag}_{i}(\widetilde{\mathbf{s}},\widetilde{{\bm{\psi}}})&\Leftrightarrow&\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert^{2}-2\mathbf{s}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{h}\>\leq\>\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{s}_{j}\lvert^{2}-2\mathbf{s}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{h}\quad\forall j\neq i\\[5.69054pt]
&\Leftrightarrow&\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert^{2}-2\mathbf{h}\cdot(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i})+\lvert\mathbf{h}\lvert^{2}-2\mathbf{s}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{h}\>\leq\>\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{j}\lvert^{2}-2\mathbf{h}\cdot(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{j})+\lvert\mathbf{h}\lvert^{2}-2\mathbf{s}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{h}\quad\forall j\neq i\\[5.69054pt]
&\Leftrightarrow&\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert^{2}\>\leq\>\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{j}\lvert^{2}\quad\forall j\neq i\\[5.69054pt]
&\Leftrightarrow&\mathbf{x}\in\text{\rm Vor}_{i}(\mathbf{s}).\end{array}

In other terms, the Voronoi tessellation
\mathbf{Vor}(\mathbf{s})
of
D
, whose seed points
\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{N}
belong to the interior of their respective cells, has the same cells as the Laguerre diagram
\mathbf{Lag}(\widetilde{\mathbf{s}},\widetilde{{\bm{\psi}}})
whose seed points lie arbitrarily far away.

Remark 3.2
.

Although, strictly speaking, a decomposition of the form
3.6
is not a classical Laguerre diagram, it can be understood as the diagram associated to the collection of “seed objects”

\mathbf{s}\cup\left\{\mathbf{s}_{0}\right\}
and weights
{\bm{\psi}}\cup\left\{\psi_{0}\right\}
, where the additional object
\mathbf{s}_{0}
is the whole domain
D
and
\psi_{0}=0
, see
[
80
]
.
A straightforward adaptation of the
Definition

3.1
of a Laguerre diagram to allow seed objects (and not just seed points) shows indeed that the cells of this new diagram are exactly those
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
in
3.7
for
i=1,\ldots,N
, with the following additional “void cell”, associated to
\mathbf{s}_{0}
:

(3.8)

V_{0}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}):=\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\overline{D},\>\>\forall i=1,\ldots,N,\quad 0\leq|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|^{2}-\psi_{i}\right\}.

Hence, up to a small abuse of terminology, we shall refer to
3.6
as a (modified) Laguerre diagram for
\Omega
.

a

b

c

d

e

f

Figure 3
.
(a) Two-dimensional Voronoi diagrams (and “classical” Laguerre diagrams) can be described by collections of three-dimensional paraboloids with axes aligned with the third coordinate vector; (d) The 2d diagram is obtained by “seeing this collection from below”; (b) Modified Laguerre diagrams are obtained by inserting the 3d lower half-space in the diagram, and (d) The intersections between the paraboloids and this half-space create circular arcs at the boundaries of the modified cells; (c) The weights
\psi_{i}
of a
(classical or modified) Laguerre diagram correspond to shifts of the parabolas along the vertical axis; (f) This results in a change of the area of the cells.

An insightful geometric viewpoint about classical and modified Laguerre diagrams
3.3
and
3.4
and
3.6
and
3.7
is to consider them as minimization diagrams of a family of functions, as we now briefly explain in the case
d=2
for simplicity, see
Fig.

3
for an illustration.
The Voronoi diagram
\mathbf{Vor}(\mathbf{s})
of a set of seed points
\mathbf{s}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2N}
is generated by the 3d paraboloids given by the graphs of the functions
\mathbf{x}\mapsto p_{i}(\mathbf{x})=\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert^{2}
, in the sense that:

\text{For each }i=1,\ldots,N,\quad\text{\rm Vor}_{i}(\mathbf{s})=\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\overline{D}\text{ s.t. }i\in\argmin\limits_{j=1,\ldots,N}p_{j}(\mathbf{x})\right\},

i.e.
\text{\rm Vor}_{i}(\mathbf{s})
is the set of points
\mathbf{x}\in\overline{D}
where
p_{i}(\mathbf{x})
is minimal among
p_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\ldots,p_{N}(\mathbf{x})
.
The “classical” Laguerre diagram
\mathbf{Lag}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
involving weights
{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
is obtained by “shifting” each of the aforementioned 3d paraboloids by the corresponding weight
\psi_{i}
in the negative
\mathbf{e}_{3}
direction.
Eventually, a corresponding representation of the modified diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
is obtained by adding the function
p_{0}(\mathbf{x})=0
to the minimization diagram, i.e. by intersecting each of the 3d paraboloids with the lower half-space
\left\{\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\text{ s.t. }x_{3}\leq 0\right\}
.

3.2.
Parametrization of the diagram representation via the seed points and cell measures

The effect of the weights
{\bm{\psi}}
on the shapes of the cells
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
as encoded in
3.7
is implicit and difficult to handle.
It turns out that the decomposition
3.6
of
\Omega
can be equivalently characterized by the seed points
\mathbf{s}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
and the vector

{\bm{\nu}}:=\left\{\nu_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N},\quad\nu_{i}=\lvert V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})\lvert

gathering the measures of the cells.
This section discusses this convenient alternative viewpoint which will be used throughout the following.
It relies on optimal transport; for the convenience of the reader, we recall a few useful facts about this theory,
referring to
[
72
,
76
,
99
,
107
,
124
]
for more exhaustive treatments.

Let
X
stand for the compact set
\overline{D}
.
For
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in X
, we denote by
c(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\lvert^{2}
the quadratic ground cost on
X
,
which intuitively represents the cost of moving one unit of mass from the position
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
.
When
\mu
and
\nu
are positive measures on
X
with equal mass
\mu(X)=\nu(X)
, the Monge formulation of the optimal transport problem of
\mu
onto
\nu
consists in the search for a mapping
T:X\to X
realizing the transfer of the mass of
\mu
onto
\nu
at minimal cost, that is:

(
OT-M
)

\min\left\{\int_{X}c(\mathbf{x},T(\mathbf{x}))\>\text{\rm d}\mu(\mathbf{x}),\>\>T:X\to X\text{ is }(\mu,\nu)\text{-measurable with }T_{\#}\mu=\nu\right\},

where
T_{\#}\mu
is the push-forward of the measure
\mu
by
T
, i.e.

\text{For all }\nu\text{-measurable subset }B\subset X,\quad T_{\#}\mu(B)=\mu(T^{-1}(B)).

Unfortunately, the problem
OT-M
is often ill-posed;
for instance, an admissible mapping
T
in
OT-M
may easily fail to exist.
One therefore usually considers instead the so-called Kantorovic relaxation of
OT-M
:

(
OT-K
)

\min\left\{\int_{X\times X}c(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\>\text{\rm d}\pi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\>\>\pi\text{ is a positive measure on }X\times X\text{ s.t. }\pi_{1}=\mu,\>\pi_{2}=\nu\right\}.

Here,
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
denote the first and second marginals of the measure
\pi
on
X\times X
:

\text{For all }\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\text{-measurable subsets }A\subset X,\>B\subset X,\quad\pi_{1}(A)=\pi(A\times X)\>\>\text{and }\pi_{2}(B)=\pi(X\times B).

In
OT-K
, the minimization is realized over positive measures
\pi
on
X\times X
(also called transport plans or couplings) with marginals
\mu
and
\nu
. Intuitively, when
A
and
B
are
\mu
- and
\nu
- measurable subsets of
X
,
\pi(A\times B)
is the “quantity of mass” transferred from
A
to
B
.
The problem
OT-K
is often better behaved than
OT-M
from the mathematical viewpoint; for instance, it is immediate to see that it is convex.

Let us specialize this abstract setting to our purpose.
Let
\mathbf{s}=\left\{\mathbf{s}_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
be a collection of seed points, and let
{\bm{\psi}}=\left\{\psi_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}
be a weight vector.
According to
Remark

3.2
we let
\psi_{0}:=0
be the weight for the seed object
\mathbf{s}_{0}=D
.
Likewise, when
{\bm{\nu}}=\left\{\nu_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}
is a collection of volume targets such that

\forall i=1,\ldots,N,\quad\nu_{i}\geq 0,\quad\text{ and }\quad\sum_{i=0}^{N}\nu_{i}\leq|D|,

we denote by
\nu_{0}:=|D|-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nu_{i}
the volume of the corresponding “void phase”
V_{0}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
in
3.8
.
We then aim to apply the aforementioned optimal transport theory to the situation where
\mu:=\mathds{1}_{D}\>\text{\rm d}x
is the restriction to
X
of the
d
-dimensional Lebesgue measure and

(3.9)

\nu:=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nu_{i}\delta_{\mathbf{s}_{i}}+\nu_{0}\frac{1}{|D|}\mathds{1}_{D}\>\text{\rm d}x

is the discrete measure induced by the objects
\left\{\mathbf{s}_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}\cup\left\{\mathbf{s}_{0}\right\}
. In this perspective,
we shall often invoke the following assumptions about
\mathbf{s}
and
{\bm{\psi}}
to rule out very degenerate situations as far as the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
is concerned:

(
G1
)

\text{All the cells }V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})\text{ have positive Lebesgue measure, $i=0,1,\ldots,N$ (in particular, they are non empty).}

(
G2
)

\text{No three distinct seed points }\mathbf{s}_{i},\>\mathbf{s}_{j},\>\mathbf{s}_{k}\text{ are aligned.}

(3.10)

\text{For each vertex }\mathbf{q}\text{ at the intersection between 2 neighboring cells }V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}),\>V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}),\>\>i,j=1,\ldots,N,\\
\text{ and the void phase }V_{0}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}),\>\>\mathbf{q},\>\mathbf{s}_{i}\text{ and }\mathbf{s}_{j}\text{ are not aligned.}

(3.11)

\text{Each vertex }\mathbf{q}\text{ is at the intersection of at most 3 distinct cells }\\
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}),\>V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}),V_{k}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}),\>\>i,j,k=0,1,\ldots,N.

(3.12)

\text{The boundary }\partial D\text{ of the computational domain is smooth at each vertex }\mathbf{q}\text{ lying on }\partial D.\\
\text{Such a point is moreover at the intersection of at most 2 distinct cells }V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}),\>V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}),\>\>i,j=0,\ldots,N.

(3.13)

\text{For each vertex }\mathbf{q}\text{ at the intersection of }\partial D\text{ and 2 distinct cells }V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}),\>V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}),\>i,j=1,\ldots,N,\\
\text{the line } \partial V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})\cap\partial V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})\text{ is not orthogonal to }\mathbf{n}(\mathbf{q}).

(3.14)

\text{For each vertex }\mathbf{q}\text{ at the intersection between }\partial D,\text{ one cell }V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})\text{ and the void }V_{0}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}),\\
\text{the line }\mathbf{q}\mathbf{s}_{i}\text{ is not orthogonal to }\mathbf{n}(\mathbf{q}).

The desired description of shapes
\Omega
in terms of the seed points
\mathbf{s}
and cell measures
{\bm{\nu}}
of an associated diagram
3.6
is enabled by the following result. Its proof, which is an adaptation of those of Theorems 37 and 40 in
[
84
]
,
is postponed to
Appendix

C
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
D\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be a bounded, Lipschitz domain and
X:=\overline{D}
.
Let
\mathbf{s}=\left\{\mathbf{s}_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
be a set of seed points in
D
,
and let
{\bm{\nu}}=\left\{\nu_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
be a vector of associated cell measures, satisfying:

(3.15)

\forall i=1,\ldots,N,\quad\nu_{i}>0,\quad\text{ and }\quad\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nu_{i}<|D|.

The following statements hold true:

(i)

There exists a unique weight vector
{\bm{\psi}}^{*}\equiv{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
such that:

(3.16)

\forall i=1,\ldots,N,\quad|V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*})|=\nu_{i},\text{ and so }\left\lvert V_{0}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*})\right\lvert=\nu_{0}.

(ii)

The vector
{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
is one solution to the maximization problem

(3.17)

\max\limits_{{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}}K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}),

where the so-called Kantorovic functional
K:{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}_{\mathbf{s}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}_{{\bm{\nu}}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}_{{\bm{\psi}}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is defined by:

(3.18)

K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}):=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\int_{V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})}\left(|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|^{2}-\psi_{i}\right)\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\nu_{i}\psi_{i}.

(iii)

Assuming that the genericity conditions
G1
,
G2
,
3.10
,
3.11
,
3.12
,
3.13
and
3.14
hold true, this solution is unique.
Moreover,
K
is smooth on an open neighborhood of
(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}_{\mathbf{s}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}_{{\bm{\nu}}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}_{{\bm{\psi}}}
, and
{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
is the unique solution to the equation:

(3.19)

\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))=\bm{0},\text{ where }\mathbf{F}:{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}_{\mathbf{s}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}_{\bm{\nu}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}_{\bm{\psi}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\text{ is defined by }\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}):=\nabla_{\bm{\psi}}K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}).

(iv)

There exists a unique optimal transport mapping
T_{{\bm{\psi}}}
between the measures
\mu
and
\nu
in the Monge formulation
OT-M
, which is given by:

(3.20)

\text{For a.e. }\mathbf{x}\in X,\quad T_{{\bm{\psi}}}(\mathbf{x})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbf{s}_{i}&\text{if } \mathbf{x}\in V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})),\>\>i=1,\ldots,N,\\
\mathbf{x}&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Remark 3.3
.

The uniqueness statement
(iii)
in the above theorem actually holds true under weaker assumptions than the collection
G1
,
G2
,
3.10
,
3.11
,
3.12
,
3.13
and
3.14
. It only requires that the mapping
{\bm{\psi}}\mapsto K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})
be differentiable, i.e. loosely speaking that the area
\lvert V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})\lvert
of each cell be differentiable with respect to
{\bm{\psi}}
, while
G1
,
G2
,
3.10
,
3.11
,
3.12
,
3.13
and
3.14
imply that all the individual vertices of the cells of
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
are differentiable with respect to
{\bm{\psi}}
, see
Remark

B.1
below about this point.

Remark 3.4
.

The situation where
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nu_{i}=|D|
corresponds to the “classical” setting where the cell
V_{0}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
is empty and the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
coincides with the Laguerre diagram
\mathbf{Lag}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
.
Then, there still exists a collection of weights
{\bm{\psi}}
fulfilling the conclusions of
Theorem

3.1
,
but the latter is only unique up to the addition of a common factor to all the weights
\psi_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,N

[
84
]
.

Remark 3.5
.

Our representation
3.6
of shapes and their deformations echoes to the framework of “linearized optimal transport”, recently proposed in
[
46
,
83
,
126
]
. Briefly, the latter advocates to parametrize measures
\nu
on
X
by the unique optimal transport mapping between a fixed reference measure
\mu
on
X
and
\nu
, i.e. the solution to
OT-M
. By comparison, our parametrization
3.6
of shapes
\Omega\subset D
boils down to considering the particular class
3.20
of optimal transport mapping between the uniform measure
\mu=\frac{1}{\lvert D\lvert}\mathds{1}_{D}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}
on
X
and measures
\nu
of the form
3.9
.

3.3.
General sketch of the Laguerre diagram-based shape and topology optimization method

This section outlines our algorithmic strategy for the resolution of a generic shape and topology optimization problem of the form
P
,
in which the objective and constraint functionals
J(\Omega)
and
\mathbf{G}(\Omega)
depend on the optimized shape
\Omega
via the solution
u_{\Omega}
to a boundary value problem posed on
\Omega
, as in the examples of
Section

2.2
.
The method is summarized in
Algorithm

1
; its main steps are illustrated on
Fig.

4
and they are more thoroughly described in the next sections of the article.

Algorithm 1

Laguerre diagram based shape and topology optimization algorithm for
P
.

Input:
Initial shape
\Omega^{0}
characterized via
3.6
and
3.7
by the datum of:

•

The collection of seed points
\mathbf{s}^{0}=\left\{\mathbf{s}^{0}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{N}^{0}\right\}
;

•

The vector
{\bm{\nu}}^{0}:=\left\{\nu_{1}^{0},\ldots,\nu_{N}^{0}\right\}
gathering the measures of the cells.

for

n=0,...,
until convergence
do

(1)

Calculate the unique weight vector
{\bm{\psi}}^{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
such that:

\forall i=1,\ldots,N,\quad\lvert V_{i}(\mathbf{s}^{n},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})\lvert=\nu_{i}^{n}.

(2)

(Optionally) Modify this diagram by resampling and Lloyd regularization.

(3)

Construct a polygonal mesh
{\mathcal{T}}^{n}
for
\Omega^{n}
from the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}^{n},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
.

(4)

Calculate the solutions
u_{\Omega^{n}}
,
p_{\Omega^{n}}
to the state and adjoint equations on the mesh
{\mathcal{T}}^{n}
.

(5)

Calculate a descent direction
(\mathbf{h}^{n},\widehat{\bm{\nu}}^{n})
for the problem
P
, that is:

•

A collection
\mathbf{h}^{n}=\left\{\mathbf{h}_{i}^{n}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
of vectors attached to the seed points
\mathbf{s}^{n}
;

•

An update vector
\widehat{{\bm{\nu}}}^{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
for the measures.

(6)

Update the seed points and measure vectors
\mathbf{s}^{n}
and
{\bm{\nu}}^{n}
as:

\mathbf{s}_{i}^{n+1}=\mathbf{s}_{i}^{n}+\tau^{n}\mathbf{h}_{i}^{n},\text{ and } {\bm{\nu}}^{n+1}={\bm{\nu}}^{n}+\rho^{n}\widehat{{\bm{\nu}}}^{n},

where
\tau^{n}>0
and
\rho^{n}>0
are suitable descent steps.

end

for

return
Seed points
\mathbf{s}^{n}
and measures
{\bm{\nu}}^{n}
representing the optimized shape
\Omega^{n}
via
3.6
and
3.7
.

a

b

c

d

e

Figure 4
.
Illustration of the main steps of
Algorithm

1
; (a) At the beginning of each iteration, the weight vector
{\bm{\psi}}^{n}
is computed, and the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}^{n},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
for
\Omega^{n}
is constructed (the seed points are depicted in red); (b) The state equation for
u_{\Omega^{n}}
is solved by the Virtual Element Method on the polygonal mesh
{\mathcal{T}}^{n}
of
\Omega^{n}
induced by
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}^{n},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
; (c) A descent direction is calculated as a vector field
\mathbf{h}^{n}
for the seed points and an update
{\bm{\nu}}^{n}
for the cell measures; (d) The seed points
\mathbf{s}^{n+1}
of the new diagram for
\Omega^{n+1}
are obtained by moving those in
\mathbf{s}^{n}
along
\mathbf{h}^{n}
; the cell measures
{\bm{\nu}}^{n+1}
are obtained likewise; (e) The new weight vector
{\bm{\psi}}^{n+1}
and the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}^{n+1},{\bm{\psi}}^{n+1})
for
\Omega^{n+1}
are computed.

At each iteration
n=0,\ldots
, we label with an
n
superscript the actual instances of the various objects at stake.
The shape
\Omega^{n}\subset D
is consistently described by the seed points
\mathbf{s}^{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
and the vector
{\bm{\nu}}^{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
of the cell measures of a diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}^{n},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
of the form
3.6
and
3.7
.
The iteration starts with the calculation of the unique weight vector
{\bm{\psi}}^{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
such that the measure of each cell
V_{i}(\mathbf{s}^{n},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
equals
\nu_{i}
, see
Section

4.1
.
The diagram associated to these data is computed, as described in
Section

4.1
.
A number of post-processing operations are conducted on the latter, such as regularization, resampling, etc., see
Sections

4.2
and
4.3
.
Note that these operations may alter the number
N
of seed points – a detail which is omitted in the present sketch for simplicity.
A polygonal mesh
{\mathcal{T}}^{n}
of
\Omega^{n}
is then constructed from this diagram, see again
Section

4
.
Then, the solution
u_{\Omega^{n}}
to the mechanical system of interest (and that
p_{\Omega^{n}}
to the adjoint system) is calculated on the mesh
{\mathcal{T}}^{n}
; this task relies on the Virtuel Element Method, and its treatment is described in
Section

5
.
The derivatives of the objective and constraint functions
J(\Omega)
and
\mathbf{G}(\Omega)
are evaluated, at first with respect to the vertices
\mathbf{q}^{n}
of the polygonal mesh
{\mathcal{T}}^{n}
– see
Section

6.1
– then with respect to the seed points and cell measures
\mathbf{s}^{n}
and
{\bm{\nu}}^{n}
defining the representation
3.6
of
\Omega^{n}
, see
Section

6.2
.
A descent direction for the considered optimization problem is inferred thanks to a constrained optimization algorithm under the form of update vectors for seeds and cell measures, see
Sections

6.3
and
6.4
.
This procedure is iterated until convergence.

4.
Geometric computations on
\Omega

This section provides a few details about the non trivial operations from algorithmic geometry involved in
Algorithm

1
.
In
Section

4.1
, we discuss the calculation of the unique weight vector
{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
supplied by
Theorem

3.1
, guaranteeing that the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))
complies with the measure constraints
3.16
; we notably describe the computation of this diagram. In
Section

4.2
, we present a variant of the well-known Lloyd’s algorithm aimed at improving the aspect of the cells of the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
, in the perspective of realizing accurate mechanical computations. Eventually, in
Section

4.3
, we sketch simple numerical recipes to adjust the local density of cells and remove “small” components disconnected from the main structure in the course of the iterative process.

4.1.
Calculation of the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{\nu})
associated to seed points
\mathbf{s}
and cell measures
\mathbf{\nu}

Throughout this section,
\mathbf{s}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
and
{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
are collections of seed points and weights satisfying the assumptions of
Theorem

3.1
; notably, they satisfy
G1
,
G2
,
3.10
,
3.11
,
3.12
,
3.13
and
3.14
. We describe the calculation of the unique weight vector
{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
guaranteeing that each cell
i=1,\ldots,N
of the modified diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))
has measure
\nu_{i}
, together with the practical construction of this diagram.
Our strategy is based on
Theorem

3.1
, whereby
{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
is characterized as the unique solution to the non linear equation

3.19
, that we rewrite below for convenience:

(4.1)

\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))=0,\text{ where }\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}):=\nabla_{\bm{\psi}}K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}).

This feature paves the way to a Newton-Raphson algorithm
[
70
]
, based on the differentiability of
\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},\cdot)
, and thus on the second order differentiability of the Kantorovic functional
K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},\cdot)
with respect to weights
{\bm{\psi}}
, which hold true since
\mathbf{s}
and
{\bm{\nu}}
are generic in the sense of
G1
,
G2
,
3.10
,
3.11
,
3.12
,
3.13
and
3.14
.

This numerical strategy is sketched in
Algorithm

2
and it is illustrated in
Fig.

5
. Briefly, the algorithm starts with a suitable initial guess
{\bm{\psi}}^{0}
. At each iteration
n=0,\ldots
, whose corresponding objects are labelled with an
n
superscript, we first construct the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
. The entries of the gradient
\nabla_{{\bm{\psi}}}K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
and Hessian matrix
[\nabla^{2}_{{\bm{\psi}}}K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})]\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N\times N}
of the Kantorovic functional are then calculated from this geometric support. The update step
\mathbf{p}^{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
for the weight vector is then obtained by the solution of the following Newton linear system with size
N
:

(4.2)

\left[\nabla_{{\bm{\psi}}}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})\right]\mathbf{p}^{n}=-\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n}).

Eventually, a descent parameter
\alpha^{n}>0
is chosen to update the weight vector
{\bm{\psi}}^{n}
into the next iterate
{\bm{\psi}}^{n+1}
. We provide a little more details about the main steps of this process in the next subsections.

Algorithm 2

Computation of the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
associated to given seed points
\mathbf{s}
and cell measures
{\bm{\nu}}

Inputs:

•

Fixed collection
\mathbf{s}=\left\{\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{N}\right\}
of seed points.

•

Fixed collection
{\bm{\nu}}=\left\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{N}\right\}
of cell measures.

•

Initial weight vector
{\bm{\psi}}^{0}=\{\psi_{1}^{0},\ldots,\psi_{N}^{0}\}
such that for all
i=1,\ldots,N
,
V_{i}({\bm{\mathbf{s}}},{\bm{\psi}}^{0})\neq\emptyset
.

•

Tolerance parameter
\varepsilon_{\text{Newt}}
about the fulfillment of
4.1
.

for

n=0,...,
until convergence
do

(1)

Compute the Laguerre diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})

(2)

Calculate the gradient
\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})=\nabla_{{\bm{\psi}}}K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
of the functional
K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},\cdot)
at
{\bm{\psi}}^{n}
.

(3)

if

\lvert\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})\lvert_{\infty}<\varepsilon_{\text{Newt}}

then exit loop

(4)

Calculate the
N\times N
Hessian matrix
[\nabla_{{\bm{\psi}}}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})]=[\nabla^{2}_{{\bm{\psi}}}K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})]
of
K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},\cdot)
at
{\bm{\psi}}^{n}
.

(5)

Calculate the solution
\mathbf{p}^{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
to the linear system
4.2
.

(6)

Select a suitable descent parameter
\alpha^{n}>0
.

(7)

Update the weight vector as:
{\bm{\psi}}^{n+1}={\bm{\psi}}^{n}+\alpha^{n}\mathbf{p}^{n}
.

end

for

return
Diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
satisfying
\lvert V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})\lvert=\nu_{i}
, for all
i=1,\ldots,N
, up to precision
\varepsilon_{\text{Newt}}
.

n=0
n=3
n=5

n=0
n=4
n=7

Figure 5
.
Three different Newton iterations in the calculation of the modified diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
in
Section

4.1
. The weight vector
{\bm{\psi}}
is initialized with a value
{\bm{\psi}}^{0}
ensuring that none of the cells
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{0})
is empty, and it is iteratively updated by Newton iterations. This procedure appraises contacts between neighboring cells, and at convergence,
{\bm{\psi}}^{n}
is such that the measure of each cell
\lvert V_{i}({\bm{\mathbf{s}}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})\lvert
corresponds to the prescribed value
\nu_{i}
, up to the precision parameter
\varepsilon_{\text{Newt}}
.

4.1.1.
Initialization

According to
Theorem

3.1
, the initial weight vector
{\bm{\psi}}^{0}
must be such that all the cells
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{0})
,
i=0,1,\ldots,N
(i.e. including the “void” phase
V_{0}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{0})
given by
3.8
) are non empty. In particular, this property is meant to ensure that the Kantorovic mapping
K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},\cdot)
is differentiable at
{\bm{\psi}}^{0}
, see
Remark

3.3
.

One sufficient condition for this to hold is to select a common positive value
\overline{\psi}>0
for all the
\psi^{0}_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,N
. Indeed, the classical Laguerre diagram
\mathbf{Lag}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{0})
then coincides with the Voronoi diagram
\mathbf{Vor}(\mathbf{s})
associated to the seed points
\mathbf{s}
, where each cell
\text{\rm Vor}_{i}(\mathbf{s})
contains at least its associated seed point
\mathbf{s}_{i}
. In turn, each modified cell
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{0})=\text{\rm Lag}_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{0})\cap\overline{B(\mathbf{s}_{i},\psi_{i}^{1/2})}
is non empty,
i=1,\ldots,N
, and since the common value
\overline{\psi}
is arbitrary, it is easy to guarantee that
V_{0}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{0})
is also non empty. In practice, we choose
{\bm{\psi}}^{0}=\left\{1,1,\ldots,1\right\}
as initial weight vector.

If all the target measures
\nu_{i}
are equal to a common value
\overline{\nu}
, a simple heuristic suggests an initialization procedure with “better” practical behavior. Intuitively, if a seed point
\mathbf{s}_{i}
lies “sufficiently far away” from all the other seeds
\mathbf{s}_{j}
,
j\neq i
,
then the classical Laguerre cell
\text{\rm Lag}_{i}({\bm{\mathbf{s}}},{\bm{\psi}})
contains the ball
\overline{B(\mathbf{s}_{i},\psi_{i}^{1/2})}
; thus the modified cell
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
coincides with this ball. Going further, it is easy to show that if the seed points
\mathbf{s}_{i}
are “well separated” from one another in the sense that:

(4.3)

\lvert\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert>\sqrt{\frac{\nu_{i}}{\pi}}+\sqrt{\frac{\nu_{j}}{\pi}}\>\>\text{ in 2d, and }\>\>\lvert\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert>\left(\frac{3\nu_{i}}{4\pi}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}+\left(\frac{3\nu_{j}}{4\pi}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}\>\>\text{ in 3d,}

then the optimal transport problem
3.19
is trivial, its solution being given by:

(4.4)

\psi_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{\overline{\nu}}{\pi}&\text{if }d=2,\\[1.99997pt]
\left(\frac{3\overline{\nu}}{4\pi}\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}&\text{if }d=3,\end{array}\right.\quad i=1,\ldots,N,

and the balls
\overline{B(\mathbf{s}_{i},\psi_{i}^{1/2})}
are disjoint from one another.

In general, the data
\mathbf{s}
and
{\bm{\nu}}
do not satisfy the separation property
4.3
, and the cells of the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
“interact” through their common boundaries, see
Fig.

5
. Then, several iterations of
Algorithm

2
are needed to achieve convergence (typically 5 to 10), but in practice, the heuristic initialization
4.4
saves a couple of Newton iterations.

Last but not least, when the target measures
\nu_{i}
are different, the choice
4.4
still proves quite efficient when used with the average value
\overline{\nu}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nu_{i}
.

4.1.2.
Computation of Laguerre diagrams

This section briefly describes the construction of the modified diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
associated to given seed points
\mathbf{s}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
and weights
{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
. We consider the 2d case
d=2
for simplicity, and refer to
[
80
]
and the bibliography therein for further details, including the treatment of the 3d case. The task under scrutiny is decomposed into two steps.

Step 1: We compute the classical Laguerre diagram
\mathbf{Lag}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
associated to
\mathbf{s}
and
{\bm{\psi}}
.

Several algorithms are available to achieve this task, whose outcome is illustrated on
Fig.

6
(a). In the present article, we rely on the iterative insertion algorithm of Bowyer and Watson proposed in
[
28
,
127
]
, see also
[
55
]
– a strategy originally intended to construct Voronoi diagrams (or their dual, Delaunay triangulations), which can handle more general Laguerre diagrams up to simple adaptations, see
[
14
]
.

The Bowyer-Watson algorithm proceeds by iterative insertion of the seed points
\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{N}
. Each stage
n=1,\ldots,N
of the process starts with the datum of
the Laguerre diagram
\mathbf{Lag}(\widehat{\mathbf{s}}^{n-1},\widehat{{\bm{\psi}}}^{n-1})
associated to the subcollections
\widehat{\mathbf{s}}^{n-1}:=\left\{\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{n-1}\right\}
and
\widehat{{\bm{\psi}}}^{n-1}:=\left\{\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{n-1}\right\}
of seed points and weights, with an obvious adaptation of this setting in the case
n=1
. This diagram is stored under the dual form of a so-called regular triangulation
{\mathcal{T}}^{n-1}
of
D
, see
Fig.

6
(b): the vertices of
{\mathcal{T}}^{n-1}
are exactly
\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{n-1}
and its triangles are those connecting the 3-uples of seed points
\left\{\mathbf{s}_{i},\mathbf{s}_{j},\mathbf{s}_{k}\right\}\subset\widehat{\mathbf{s}}^{n-1}
(with disjoint indices
i,j,k
) whose associated cells share a common vertex
\mathbf{q}
. Working with this dual structure is convenient since all dual cells (i.e. the triangles of
{\mathcal{T}}^{n-1}
) have the same number of vertices, whereas storing directly the Laguerre cells of
\mathbf{Lag}(\widehat{\mathbf{s}}^{n-1},\widehat{{\bm{\psi}}}^{n-1})
would require a more complicated data structure, since the latter have different numbers of vertices.

a
b
c

Figure 6
.
Computation of the modified Laguerre diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
in
Section

4.1.2
: (a) “Classical” Laguerre diagram
\mathbf{Lag}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
; (b) Associated dual regular triangulation used to store
\mathbf{Lag}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
internally; (c) Modified diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
obtained from
\mathbf{Lag}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
after clipping each cell
\text{\rm Lag}_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
by the ball with center
\mathbf{s}_{i}
and radius
\sqrt{\psi_{i}}
.

The algorithm then inserts the seed
\mathbf{s}_{n}
into
\mathbf{Lag}(\widehat{\mathbf{s}}^{n-1},\widehat{{\bm{\psi}}}^{n-1})
, see
Fig.

7
.

a

b
c

Figure 7
.
Iterative construction of the classical Laguerre diagram
\mathbf{Lag}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
with the Bowyer-Watson algorithm in
Section

4.1.2
