; (a) Insertion of a new seed
\mathbf{s}_{n}
(in red) into the diagram
\mathbf{Lag}(\widehat{\mathbf{s}}^{n-1},\widehat{{\bm{\psi}}}^{n-1})
under construction; (b) Some of the vertices of the current diagram (in red) cannot exist in the new diagram because they lie inside the Laguerre cell of the new seed
\mathbf{s}_{n}
; (c) The cavity formed by the associated dual triangles (in red) is removed and the vertices of the new Laguerre cell are dual to triangles connecting the new seed to those at the border of the cavity (in green).

To achieve this, one first identifies the vertices
\mathbf{q}
of
\mathbf{Lag}(\widehat{\mathbf{s}}^{n-1},\widehat{{\bm{\psi}}}^{n-1})
that are in conflict with the presence of
\mathbf{s}_{n}
, in the sense that they cannot exist in the updated diagram
\mathbf{Lag}(\widehat{\mathbf{s}}^{n},\widehat{{\bm{\psi}}}^{n})
because they lie inside the Laguerre cell
\text{\rm Lag}_{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{s}}^{n},\widehat{{\bm{\psi}}}^{n})
of the new seed point
\mathbf{s}_{n}
in the latter.
Such a conflicting vertex
\mathbf{q}
, defined by the intersection of the three Laguerre cells attached to the seeds
\mathbf{s}_{i}
,
\mathbf{s}_{j}
,
\mathbf{s}_{k}
, is characterized by the following relation:

(4.5)

|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{n}|-\psi_{n}<R_{ijk},\text{ where }R_{ijk}:=|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|^{2}-\psi_{i}=|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{j}|^{2}-\psi_{j}=|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{k}|^{2}-\psi_{k}.

The equalities featured in
4.5
yield a linear system characterizing the coordinates of
\mathbf{q}
, see also
Appendix

A
. By solving the latter with the classical Cramer’s formulas, substituting the resulting values into the above condition and symmetrizing the determinant by row manipulations,
4.5
rewrites:

(4.6)

C(\mathbf{s}_{i},\mathbf{s}_{j},\mathbf{s}_{k},\mathbf{s}_{n},\psi_{i},\psi_{j},\psi_{k},\psi_{n}):=\left|\begin{array}[]{cccc}s_{i,1}&s_{i,2}&s_{i,1}^{2}+s_{i,2}^{2}-\psi_{i}&1\\[5.69054pt]
s_{j,1}&s_{j,2}&s_{j,1}^{2}+s_{j,2}^{2}-\psi_{j}&1\\[5.69054pt]
s_{k,1}&s_{k,2}&s_{k,1}^{2}+s_{k,2}^{2}-\psi_{k}&1\\[5.69054pt]
s_{n,1}&s_{n,2}&s_{n,1}^{2}+s_{n,2}^{2}-\psi_{n}&1\end{array}\right|>0.

One then constructs the cavity
{\mathcal{C}}^{n-1}
, defined as the collection of the dual triangles in
{\mathcal{T}}^{n-1}
associated to the vertices in conflict with
\mathbf{s}_{n}
, see the red triangles in
Fig.

7
(b). With a small abuse of notations, we use the same notation for this collection of triangles and the closed subset of
\overline{D}
formed by their reunion.
It can be shown that
{\mathcal{C}}^{n-1}
is a connected set containing
\mathbf{s}_{n}
. Hence the identification of
{\mathcal{C}}^{n-1}
starts by finding one triangle
T\in{\mathcal{T}}^{n-1}
containing
\mathbf{s}_{n}
, and then proceeds by propagation through the neighbors of
T
while they are in conflict with
\mathbf{s}_{n}
, in the sense that
4.6
holds, see
Fig.

7
(b).
Finally, the triangles in the cavity
{\mathcal{C}}^{n-1}
are removed from the dual triangulation
{\mathcal{T}}^{n-1}
, and the new triangles dual to the vertices of the new Laguerre cell
\text{\rm Lag}_{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{s}}^{n},\widehat{{\bm{\psi}}}^{n})
are constructed. Each of them connects
\mathbf{s}_{n}
with an edge of the boundary of
{\mathcal{C}}^{n-1}
, see
Fig.

7
(c). This procedure is iterated until all the seed points in
\mathbf{s}
have been inserted.

The Bowyer-Watson algorithm crucially hinges on geometric predicates, that is, procedures taking combinatorial decisions based on the fulfillment of inequalities between the coordinates of some geometric objects. Notably, the conflict test
4.6
is based on a predicate; moreover, the aforementioned search for a dual triangle in
{\mathcal{C}}^{n-1}
containing the inserted seed point
\mathbf{s}_{n}
requires another predicate, measuring the relative orientation of three points.
The implementation of both predicates requires a particular attention, in order to overcome the lack of precision of standard computer arithmetics. They amount to evaluate the sign of a polynomial function evaluated at the coordinates of the seed points
\mathbf{s}_{i}
and weights
\psi_{i}
. Such a quantity can be exactly computed at a reasonable computational cost thanks to a combination of different techniques, see
[
112
,
73
]
and references therein.

The above Bowyer-Watson procedure also rests on the assumption that the seed points
\mathbf{s}
and the weights
{\bm{\psi}}
are in a generic configuration (see again
Theorem

3.1
and
Remark

3.3
). In practice, this assumption guarantees that the determinant in the characterization
4.6
of the dual triangles in the cavity
{\mathcal{C}}^{n-1}
does not vanish – a situation where
\mathbf{s}_{n}
would lie on the boundary of this cavity, making the procedure invalid. However, “pathologic” configurations where this determinant vanishes happen in practice, and they require a specific treatment. For instance, if
{\bm{\psi}}=\mathbf{0}
(and then
\mathbf{Lag}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
is the Voronoi diagram
\mathbf{Vor}(\mathbf{s})
), the predicate
4.6
evaluates whether the added seed point
\mathbf{s}_{n}
lies inside the disk circumscribed to the triangle with vertices

\mathbf{s}_{i}
,
\mathbf{s}_{j}
,
\mathbf{s}_{k}
; the determinant in
4.6
then equals zero if the four seed points

\mathbf{s}_{i},\mathbf{s}_{j},\mathbf{s}_{k},\mathbf{s}_{n}
are cocyclic. In this case, two triangulations of the cavity
{\mathcal{C}}^{n-1}
based on
\mathbf{s}_{n}
are possible, and one would need to consistently choose among them.

One possibility to treat situations where the determinant
C(\mathbf{s}_{i},\mathbf{s}_{j},\mathbf{s}_{k},\mathbf{s}_{n},\psi_{i},\psi_{j},\psi_{k},\psi_{n})
vanishes is to evade from singular configurations thanks to the symbolic perturbation approach
[
50
]
: we conceptually perturb each weight
\psi_{i}
as
\psi_{i}+\varepsilon^{i}
, where
\varepsilon\ll 1
is a very small parameter, and we consider the expansion of the associated determinant in terms of the powers of
\varepsilon
:

(4.7)

C(\mathbf{s}_{i},\mathbf{s}_{j},\mathbf{s}_{k},\mathbf{s}_{l},\psi_{i}+\varepsilon^{i},\psi_{j}+\varepsilon^{j},\psi_{k}+\varepsilon^{k},\psi_{n}+\varepsilon^{n})=\left|\begin{array}[]{cccc}s_{i,1}&s_{i,2}&s_{i,1}^{2}+s_{i,2}^{2}-\psi_{i}-\varepsilon^{i}&1\\[5.69054pt]
s_{j,1}&s_{j,2}&s_{j,1}^{2}+s_{j,2}^{2}-\psi_{j}-\varepsilon^{j}&1\\[5.69054pt]
s_{k,1}&s_{k,2}&s_{k,1}^{2}+s_{k,2}^{2}-\psi_{k}-\varepsilon^{k}&1\\[5.69054pt]
s_{n,1}&s_{n,2}&s_{n,1}^{2}+s_{n,2}^{2}-\psi_{n}-\varepsilon^{n}&1\end{array}\right|\\[10.00002pt]
\quad=C(\mathbf{s}_{i},\mathbf{s}_{j},\mathbf{s}_{k},\mathbf{s}_{l},\psi_{i},\psi_{j},\psi_{k},\psi_{n})-\varepsilon^{i}\left|\begin{array}[]{ccc}s_{j,1}&s_{j,2}&1\\
s_{k,1}&s_{k,2}&1\\
s_{n,1}&s_{n,2}&1\\
\end{array}\right|+\varepsilon^{j}\left|\begin{array}[]{ccc}s_{i,1}&s_{i,2}&1\\
s_{k,1}&s_{k,2}&1\\
s_{n,1}&s_{n,2}&1\\
\end{array}\right|\\[10.00002pt]
-\varepsilon^{k}\left|\begin{array}[]{ccc}s_{i,1}&s_{i,2}&1\\
s_{j,1}&s_{j,2}&1\\
s_{n,1}&s_{n,2}&1\\
\end{array}\right|+\varepsilon^{n}\left|\begin{array}[]{ccc}s_{i,1}&s_{i,2}&1\\
s_{j,1}&s_{j,2}&1\\
s_{k,1}&s_{k,2}&1\\
\end{array}\right|,

where the second equality follows from the multilinearity of the determinant. In the last expression of the above right-hand side, the factor of
\varepsilon^{n}
does not vanish since according to
G2
the seed points
\mathbf{s}_{i},\mathbf{s}_{j},\mathbf{s}_{k}
are not aligned; hence, using the sign of the leading term in the above expansion in powers of
\varepsilon
results in a modified predicate which never returns
0
and takes consistent combinatorial decisions in the presence of degeneracies.

Step 2: Computation of
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
by truncation of
\mathbf{Lag}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
.

For each
i=1,\ldots,N
, the
i^{\text{th}}
cell
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
of the modified diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
is obtained by computing the intersection between its classical counterpart
\text{\rm Lag}_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
with the ball centered at
\mathbf{s}_{i}
with radius
\sqrt{\psi_{i}}
, as depicted in
Fig.

6
(c). According to
Definition

3.3
, the boundary of
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
is composed of line segments – some of them pertaining to the boundary of the computational domain
D
, possibly bearing particular references inherited from the latter – and, occasionally, circular arcs, see
Figs.

2
and
5
.

As we shall see more precisely in the next
Section

5
, the mechanical computations
involved in the evaluation of the shape functionals of our optimization problem
P
rely on a discretization of
\Omega
made of convex polytopes.
The latter is constructed via the procedure described in
[
75
]
:
a number
n_{\text{\rm arc}}
is chosen, and each circular arc is discretized into a polygonal line made of
n_{\text{\rm arc}}
line segments and
(n_{\text{\rm arc}}+1)
vertices.
The calculation of the positions of these vertices from the datum of the seed points
\mathbf{s}
is detailed in
Appendix

A
.

4.1.3.
Computation of the gradient and of the Hessian matrix of the Kantorovic functional

In Steps 2 and 4 of
Algorithm

2
, the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
is used to evaluate the entries of the gradient
\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})=\nabla_{{\bm{\psi}}}K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
and of the Hessian matrix
\nabla_{{\bm{\psi}}}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})=\nabla^{2}_{{\bm{\psi}}}K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
of the Kantorovich functional
K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},\cdot)
at
{\bm{\psi}}^{n}
.

These components depend on the measures of
the cells
|V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})|
of the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
and on the lengths of their edges
\mathbf{e}_{ij}
.
Their expressions have been calculated in e.g.
[
70
,
76
]
;
for the sake of completeness, they are recalled in
Appendix

B
, where an intuitive proof is provided under simplifying assumptions.

4.1.4.
Solution mechanism for the linear systems

The calculation of the Newton step
\mathbf{p}^{n}
in Step 5 of
Algorithm

2
is based on the solution of the following linear system with size
N\times N
:

(4.8)

\left[\nabla_{{\bm{\psi}}}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})\right]\mathbf{p}^{n}=-\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n}).

When two-dimensional applications are concerned, featuring a relatively small number
N
of seed points (say,
N<10^{5}
),
direct methods such as Gaussian elimination or
LDL^{T}
factorization are available.

Larger configurations raise the need to use an iterative solver, that takes advantage of the sparse nature of the matrix involved in
4.8
, see again its expression in
Appendix

B
. The Conjugate Gradient algorithm
[
67
]
combined with the simple Jacobi preconditionner (which involves division by the diagonal coefficients) is an efficient candidate in this perspective.

When very large problems are considered (where the number
N
of seed points exceeds
10^{6}
), yet another strategy is handful. The latter is based on the fact that the linear system
4.8
corresponds to the discretization of a Poisson equation with
\mathbb{P}_{1}
finite elements. This calls for a multigrid method, that can solve such a Poisson system in linear time. In our case, the mesh characterizing this discretization is irregular and we recommend the use of an algebraic multigrid method
[
48
]
. Roughly speaking, such a multi-grid method creates a hierarchy of operators by merging nodes in the graph that corresponds to the non-zero entries of the sparse matrix.
This technique made it possible to solve 3d problems featuring up to
10^{8}
points in our previous works
[
74
,
81
]
.

4.1.5.
Stopping criterion

The components of the gradient
\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})=\nabla_{{\bm{\psi}}}K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
of the Kantorovic functional express the difference between the target cell measures
\nu_{i}
and the measures
|V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})|
of the cells of the actual version
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
of the diagram.
Hence, the supremum norm of this gradient appraises the largest error on the fulfillment of the cell measure constraint. This geometric interpretation of
\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
suggests a natural convergence criterion for
Algorithm

2
: the Newton procedure is terminated as soon as the largest measure error is smaller than 1 % of the smallest measure prescription, i.e. when the following inequality is satisfied:

\lvert\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})\lvert_{\infty}<\varepsilon_{\text{Newt}},\text{ where }\varepsilon_{\text{Newt}}=0.01\left(\min\limits_{i=1,\ldots,N}\nu_{i}\right).

4.1.6.
Determination of the descent parameter

The selection of a suitable descent parameter
\alpha^{n}
for the update of the weight vector
{\bm{\psi}}
in Step 6 of
Algorithm

2
deserves a particular attention. Indeed, the invertibility of the Hessian matrix
\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
of the Kantorovic functional involved in the linear system
4.8
for the Newton step
\mathbf{p}^{n}
crucially requires that none of the cells
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
is empty,
n=0,\ldots,N
, see again the expressions in
Appendix

B
. Hence, the choice of
\alpha^{n}
must guarantee that no cell
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{n+1})
in the updated diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{n+1})
is empty.

To ensure this property, we rely on the Kitagawa-Mérigot-Thibert strategy, described in
Algorithm

3
and analyzed in
[
70
]
: starting from the Newton step
\alpha^{n,0}=1
, the descent parameter is halved until
the magnitude of the gradient
\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
has sufficiently decreased while
the size of the smallest cell in
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{n+1})
is larger than a certain threshold
\nu_{\text{min}}
; in practice, we take:

(4.9)

\nu_{\text{min}}=\frac{1}{2}\min\left(\min\limits_{i=0,1,\ldots,N}|V_{i}(\mathbf{s},\bm{0})|,\min\limits_{i=0,1,\ldots,N}\nu_{i}\right).

Using the so-computed descent parameter, the convergence of the Newton algorithm
Algorithm

2
is proven, see
[
70
]
for the details.

Algorithm 3

Determination of a suitable descent parameter for the update of the weight vector

Input:
The current Newton iterate
n
in
Algorithm

2
, characterized by the datum of:

•

The collection of seed points
{\bm{\mathbf{s}}}=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{N}\}
;

•

The current weight vector
{\bm{\psi}}^{n}
;

•

The current Newton step vector
\mathbf{p}^{n}
;

•

The initial guess about the descent parameter
\alpha^{n,0}=1
.

for

k=0,...,
until convergence
do

(1)

if

\min\limits_{i=0,\ldots,N}|V_{i}({\bm{\psi}}^{n}+\alpha^{n,k}\mathbf{p}^{n})|>\nu_{\text{min}}

and

\lvert\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n}+\alpha^{n,k}\mathbf{p}^{n})\lvert\leq(1-\frac{\alpha^{n,k}}{2})\lvert\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})\lvert

exit loop

(2)

Update the descent parameter as:
\alpha^{n,k+1}=\alpha^{n,k}/2
.

(3)

Compute the new diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{n}+\alpha^{n,k+1}\mathbf{p}^{n})

end

for

return
Descent parameter
\alpha^{n}:=\alpha^{n,k}
.

4.2.
Smoothing of a diagram by a variant of Lloyd’s algorithm

As we shall describe more extensively in
Section

5
, the polygonal mesh induced by the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
representing the shape
\Omega
in our optimal design
Algorithm

1
is used, in particular, as the numerical support for the solution of boundary value problems posed on
\Omega
, by means of the Virtual Element Method. The accuracy of the latter, like that of any Galerkin-based solution strategy, is tightly related to the “quality” of this polygonal mesh; intuitively, this notion appraises how close its polygons are from being regular
[
117
,
116
]
. Unfortunately,
Algorithm

1
does not offer any guarantee about the quality of the cells of the diagrams
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}^{n},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
at play, and it is necessary to periodically stop the process, say every
3
,
4
iterations, to improve their quality.

One possibility to achieve this goal borrows from the famous Lloyd’s algorithm for Voronoi diagrams
[
79
]
. Roughly speaking, the latter produces a “well-shaped” centroidal Voronoi tessellation (i.e. where the seed point of each cell coincides with its centroid) from an arbitrary initial diagram
\mathbf{Vor}(\mathbf{s})
; it proceeds within a series of iterations, by replacing the seed point
\mathbf{s}_{i}
of each cell
i=1,\ldots,N
by the centroid
\mathbf{c}_{i}
of the latter, and then computing the new diagram
\mathbf{Vor}(\mathbf{c})
.
Often, a relaxed version of this procedure is used, whereby each seed point
\mathbf{s}_{i}
is moved towards
\mathbf{c}_{i}
for a short pseudo-time step
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

In our strategy, we rely on a natural extension of this procedure to the present context of Laguerre diagrams in which the measure of each cell is imposed.
This was recently proposed in
[
42
,
43
,
128
]
, see also
[
27
]
; it is sketched in
Algorithm

4
.

Algorithm 4

Smoothing of a diagram with constrained cell measures by a variant of Lloyd’s algorithm

Inputs:

•

Diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}^{0},{\bm{\psi}}^{0})
induced by:

-

An initial collection of seed points
\mathbf{s}^{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
;

-

A given vector
{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
of cell measures;

-

The unique weight vector
{\bm{\psi}}^{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
such that each cell
V_{i}(\mathbf{s}^{0},{\bm{\psi}}^{0})
has measure
\nu_{i}
.

•

Relaxation parameter
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

for

n=0,...,
until convergence
do

(1)

Calculate the collection
\mathbf{c}^{n}:=\left\{\mathbf{c}^{n}_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}
of the centroids
\mathbf{c}^{n}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
of the cells
V_{i}(\mathbf{s}^{n},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
.

(2)

Update the seed points as:
\mathbf{s}^{n+1}=(1-\alpha)\mathbf{s}^{n}+\alpha\mathbf{c}^{n}
.

(3)

Calculate the weight vector
{\bm{\psi}}^{n+1}
guaranteeing that
\lvert V_{i}(\mathbf{s}^{n+1},{\bm{\psi}}^{n+1})\lvert=\nu_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,N
.

(4)

Compute the new diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}^{n+1},{\bm{\psi}}^{n+1})
.

end

for

return
Diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}^{n},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
where each cell
V_{i}(\mathbf{s}^{n},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
is “well-shaped” and has measure
\nu_{i}

4.3.
Additional operations on diagrams

In this section, we outline two geometric operations on diagrams of the form
3.6
and
3.7
that significantly improve the efficiency of the shape optimization
Algorithm

2
.

4.3.1.
Resampling of a diagram

As will be exemplified in the numerical examples of
Section

7
, the optimization of the shape
\Omega^{n}
generally entails significant changes in its volume through the iterations
n=0,\ldots
of the process; it is often relevant to dynamically adjust the number
N
of cells of the defining diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}^{n},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
according to these changes.

To achieve this, we periodically carry out a simple resampling procedure, at the end of every one iteration over, say,
3
or
4
of
Algorithm

1
: a desired average value
\overline{\nu}
for the measures of the cells of the diagram of the shape
\Omega^{n}
is given, and we infer the suitable number
N^{n}
of cells in the latter via the following relation:

N^{n}=\frac{\text{\rm Vol}(\Omega^{n})}{\overline{\nu}}.

We then add or delete seed points from the collection
\mathbf{s}^{n}
to attain this number, according to the following rules:

•

Seed points
\mathbf{s}_{i}
are added inside the regions of
\Omega^{n}
lying far from the boundary of
\Omega^{n}
(so that this entity is unchanged in the process), where the local cell measures are largest. The measures
\nu_{i}
of the corresponding added cells are set to
\overline{\nu}
, and the (larger) measures of the neighboring cells are decreased so that the volume of
\Omega^{n}
is unaltered by this operation.

•

The deleted seed points
\mathbf{s}_{i}
lie far from
\partial\Omega^{n}
, and they correspond to cells
V_{i}(\mathbf{s}^{n},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
with “small” measures. Their mass is redistributed to the neighboring cells, so that
\text{\rm Vol}(\Omega^{n})
is unchanged in the process.

4.3.2.
Elimination of material “islands” disconnected from the main structure

As described in
Section

4.1.2
, some of the edges of the cell in the diagrams
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}^{n},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
produced by
Algorithm

1
may bear particular labels, inherited from the boundary
\partial D
of the computational domain. These notably serve to identify the regions of
\partial\Omega^{n}
bearing particular boundary conditions in the physical problem at play. For instance, in the settings of
Sections

2.2.1
and
2.2.2
, they characterize the regions
\Gamma_{D}
and
\Gamma_{N}
supporting homogeneous Dirichlet and inhomogeneous Neumann boundary conditions.
In practice, it may be desirable to remove the components of
\Omega^{n}
that are not connected to any cell bearing such a label.
For instance, in the situation of
Section

2.2.2
,
the regions of
\Omega^{n}
that are not connected to
\Gamma_{D}
cause the problem
Elas
to be ill-posed,
since the elastic displacement
\mathbf{u}_{\Omega^{n}}
is only characterized by this problem up to a rigid-body motion inside each such material “islands”.

The elimination of these regions is realized thanks to a simple algorithmic procedure, based on the connectivity of
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}^{n},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
. We start by storing into a pile all the indices
i\in\left\{1,\ldots,N\right\}
belonging to cells having one edge bearing the desired label. We then travel the cells of the diagram by propagating through the neighbors of the elements in the pile, and we thus obtain all the indices of the connected component of
\Omega^{n}
attached to the label at stake.
We eventually discard all the cells (i.e. the associated seed points) in
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
that have not been visited in this process.

5.
Mechanical computations on
\Omega
via the Virtual Element Method

In this section, we describe – in 2d for simplicity – the resolution of physical boundary value problems of the form
Cond
and
Elas
on a polygonal mesh
{\mathcal{T}}
of the shape
\Omega
.
Different numerical methods are available to achieve this goal.
Although they are slightly unusual in the physical contexts at stake in this article, let us notably mention finite volume methods – see
[
32
]
for the treatment of heat conduction problems, or
[
69
]
for linear elasticity –, discontinuous Galerkin methods
[
31
]
, or the recent Network Element Method
[
37
,
38
]
.
Here, we rely on the Virtual Element Method, which is an elegant variant of the well-known Finite Element Method adapted to the solution of boundary value problems on arbitrary polygonal meshes, see e.g.
[
12
,
15
,
39
,
57
,
120
]
, or
[
41
]
for a recent open-source implementation.

After setting notations in
Section

5.1
, we present this method in
Section

5.2
in the context of the 2d conductivity equation
Cond
, where its salient features can be exposed in a relatively non technical manner.
This presentation is not intended to be minimal; rather, it prepares the ground for the treatment of the linear elasticity system which is discussed next in
Section

5.3
.
Practical implementation details are deferred to
Appendix

D
.

5.1.
Notations

Throughout this section,
\Omega
is a two-dimensional shape equipped with a polygonal mesh
{\mathcal{T}}
.
The latter is composed of
N
(closed) elements
E_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,N
, and

M
vertices, denoted by
\mathbf{q}_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,M
.
Each polygon
E\in{\mathcal{T}}
is characterized by its
n^{E}
vertices, which form a subset
\left\{\mathbf{q}^{E}_{j}\right\}_{j=1,\ldots,n^{E}}
of
\left\{\mathbf{q}_{j}\right\}_{j=1,\ldots,M}
;
these are numbered in a counterclockwise fashion and we set
\mathbf{q}^{E}_{0}:=\mathbf{q}^{E}_{n^{E}}
and
\mathbf{q}_{n^{E}+1}^{E}:=\mathbf{q}_{1}^{E}
, see
Fig.

8
(a). We also introduce the following notation:

•

The diameter of
E
is denoted by
h^{E}:=\sup_{x,y\in E}\lvert x-y\lvert
.

•

For
i=1,\ldots,n^{E}-1
,
\mathbf{e}_{i}
is the edge with endpoints
\mathbf{q}^{E}_{i}
and
\mathbf{q}^{E}_{i+1}
;
\mathbf{e}_{0}=\mathbf{e}_{n^{E}}
stands for the edge between
\mathbf{q}^{E}_{n^{E}}
and
\mathbf{q}^{E}_{1}
.

•

For
i=0,\ldots,n^{E}
, we denote by
\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
the unit normal vector to
\mathbf{e}_{i}
, pointing outward
E
.

•

For
i=1,\ldots,n^{E}
, we denote by
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}
the edge between
\mathbf{q}^{E}_{i-1}
and
\mathbf{q}^{E}_{i+1}
,
and by
\mathbf{n}_{\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}}
the unit normal vector to
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}
, oriented so that
\langle\mathbf{n}_{\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}}\,\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{e}_{j}}\rangle\geq 0
for
j\in\left\{i-1,i\right\}
.
A simple calculation shows that

(5.1)

\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i-1}\lvert\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{e}_{i-1}}+\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i}\lvert\mathbf{n}_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}=\lvert\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}\lvert\mathbf{n}_{\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}}.

•

For any continuous function
v:E\to{\mathbb{R}}
, we denote by

\bar{v}=\frac{1}{n^{E}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n^{E}}v(\mathbf{q}^{E}_{j})

the average of
v
over the vertices of
E
; the same notation is used when
v
is replaced by a vector field or a tensor field on
E
.

•

Accordingly,
\overline{\mathbf{q}^{E}}=(\overline{q^{E}_{1}},\overline{q^{E}_{2}})
is the average position of the vertices of
E
, i.e.

\overline{\mathbf{q}^{E}}=\frac{1}{n^{E}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n^{E}}\mathbf{q}^{E}_{j};

note that
\overline{\mathbf{q}^{E}}
differs from the centroid of
E
.

•

For any integrable function
v:E\to{\mathbb{R}}
, we denote the average of
v
over
E
by

\langle v\rangle=\frac{1}{|E|}\int_{E}v(\mathbf{x})\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x};

the same notation is used when
v
is replaced by a vector field or a tensor field on
E
.

[formula]

[formula]

Figure 8
.
(a) One polygonal element
E\in{\mathcal{T}}
; (b) Graph of the basis function
\zeta_{1}
, attached to vertex
\mathbf{q}^{E}_{1}
(see
Section

5.2.2
).

5.2.
The virtual element method for the 2d conductivity equation

This section deals with the implementation of the Virtual Element Method for the solution of the conductivity equation equipped with homogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions:

(
Cond
)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}-\text{\rm div}(\gamma\nabla u)=f&\text{in } \Omega,\\
u=0&\text{on }\partial\Omega,\end{array}\right.

where the sought function
u\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
is denoted without reference to the (fixed) domain
\Omega
in this section,
the right-hand side
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
stands for a source term, and the conductivity
\gamma
is assumed to be a positive constant.
Our presentation relies on the pedagogic article
[
120
]
, see also
[
15
]
. We provide a general overview of the method in
Section

5.2.1
before focusing on the definition of the local space of discrete functions in
Section

5.2.2
.

5.2.1.
General presentation of the method

Like the Finite Element Method (see e.g.
[
36
]
),
the Virtual Element Method leverages the variational formulation
2.2
of
Cond
, which reads:

(5.2)

\text{Search for }u\in V\text{ s.t. }\forall v\in V,\quad a(u,v)=\ell(v).

Here, the functional space
V
is
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
, and we have posed:

(5.3)

\forall u,v\in V,\quad a(u,v)=\gamma\int_{\Omega}\nabla u\cdot\nabla v\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}\>\text{ and }\>\ell(v)=\int_{\Omega}fv\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}.

The discretization of
5.2
relies on a finite-dimensional subspace
{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
of
V
;
the numerical approximation
u_{{\mathcal{T}}}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
of
u
is sought as the solution to the problem:

(5.4)

\text{Search for }u_{{\mathcal{T}}}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}\text{ s.t. }\forall v\in{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{T}}},\quad a(u_{{\mathcal{T}}},v)=\ell(v).

The space
{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
is defined by:

(5.5)

{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}=\left\{u\in{\mathcal{C}}(\overline{\Omega})\text{ and }u\lvert_{E}\>\in\>{\mathcal{W}}(E)\text{ for all }E\in{\mathcal{T}}\right\},

where for each element
E\in{\mathcal{T}}
,
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
is a local space of functions on
E
whose definition is the topic of the next
Section

5.2.2
.
For the moment, let us solely mention that the dimension of
{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
coincides with the number
M
of vertices in the mesh
{\mathcal{T}}
,
and that the values of functions
u\in{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
at the vertices
\mathbf{q}_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,M
are the degrees of freedom of the discretization.
We introduce a basis
\left\{\varphi_{k}\right\}_{k=1,\ldots,M}
of
{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
as follows:
for
k=1,\ldots,M
, the function
\varphi_{k}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
is attached to the vertex
\mathbf{q}_{k}
in the sense that:

(5.6)

\forall l=1,\ldots,M,\quad\varphi_{k}(\mathbf{q}_{l})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}1&\text{if }k=l,\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

By writing the decomposition
u_{{\mathcal{T}}}=\sum_{l=1}^{M}u_{l}\varphi_{l}
of the sought function
u_{{\mathcal{T}}}
on this basis, and using
v=\varphi_{k}
as test function in
5.4
for
k=1,\ldots,M
,
the problem
5.2
and
5.3
boils down to the following
M\times M
linear system:

(5.7)

K_{{\mathcal{T}}}U_{{\mathcal{T}}}=F_{{\mathcal{T}}},

where
U_{{\mathcal{T}}}:=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{M})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
, and the entries of the stiffness matrix
K_{{\mathcal{T}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{M\times M}
and force vector
F_{{\mathcal{T}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
are given by:

(5.8)

K_{{\mathcal{T}},kl}=a(\varphi_{l},\varphi_{k}),\text{ and }F_{{\mathcal{T}},k}=\ell(\varphi_{k}),\quad k,l=1,\ldots,M.

The integrals defining these quantities are sums of contributions from each element
E\in{\mathcal{T}}
, i.e.

(5.9)

a(\varphi_{l},\varphi_{k})=\sum\limits_{E\in{\mathcal{T}}}a^{E}(\varphi_{l},\varphi_{k}),\text{ where }a^{E}(u,v):=\gamma\int_{E}\nabla u\cdot\nabla v\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x},\text{ and }\\
F_{{\mathcal{T}},k}=\sum\limits_{E\in{\mathcal{T}}}\ell^{E}(\varphi_{k}),\text{ where }\ell^{E}(v):=\int_{E}fv\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}.

Hence, the assembly of
5.7
is realized by calculating the local, element-wise contributions
a^{E}(\varphi_{l},\varphi_{k})
,
\ell^{E}(\varphi_{k})
to the entries
K_{{\mathcal{T}},kl}
of the global stiffness matrix
and
F_{{\mathcal{T}},k}
of the global force vector, respectively.
These computations are discussed in the next
Sections

5.2.2
and
D
.

5.2.2.
The local space
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
attached to an element
E\in{\mathcal{T}}

Throughout this section,
E
is a given polygon in
{\mathcal{T}}
;
we define
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
as the space of functions
u:E\to{\mathbb{R}}
satisfying the following properties:

•

The restriction
u|_{\mathbf{e}}:\mathbf{e}\to{\mathbb{R}}
of
u
to any edge
\mathbf{e}
of
E
is affine;

•

The differential operator of
Cond
cancels
u
, i.e.
-\text{\rm div}(\gamma\nabla u)=-\gamma\Delta u=0
in
E
.

Obviously, the functions
u\in{\mathcal{W}}(E)
are uniquely determined by their values at the vertices
\mathbf{q}_{j}^{E}
of
E
,
j=1,\ldots,n^{E}
;
in particular,
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
has dimension
n^{E}
and it contains the space
{\mathcal{P}}(E)
of affine functions on
E
:

{\mathcal{P}}(E):=\left\{u(\mathbf{x})=a+\langle\textbf{b},\mathbf{x}\rangle,\>\>a\in{\mathbb{R}},\>\>\textbf{b}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\right\}.

We then define a basis
\left\{\zeta_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,n^{E}}
of
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
as follows: for
i=1,\ldots,n^{E}
,
\zeta_{i}
is the unique function in
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
such that:

(5.10)

\zeta_{i}(\mathbf{q}_{j}^{E})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}1&\text{if }i=j,\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Observe that the dependency of the functions
\zeta_{i}
on the actual element
E
is omitted for notational simplicity.

Remark 5.1
.

•

Several definitions of
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
are actually possible, all of them leading to discrete functions
u\in{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
whose degrees of freedom are their values at the vertices of
{\mathcal{T}}
.
In fact, a careful selection of
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
may significantly ease the numerical implementation,
see for instance
Section

D.3
for an alternative definition of the behavior of functions
u\in{\mathcal{W}}(E)
inside
E
which lends itself to an easier evaluation of integrals of the form
\int_{E}uv\>\text{\rm d}x
,
u,v\in{\mathcal{W}}(E)
, or the appendix in
[
57
]
for another definition which is better adapted to the evaluation of quadrature formulas in the 3d elasticity setting.

•

Higher-order versions of
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
, featuring higher degree polynomials, could also be considered, leading to higher-order approximations
u_{{\mathcal{T}}}
of
u
.

With these notations, the global basis functions
\varphi_{k}
defined in
5.6
can be expressed in terms of the local functions
\zeta_{i}
; more precisely:

\forall k=1,\ldots,M,\>\> E\in{\mathcal{T}},\quad\varphi_{k}\lvert_{E}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\zeta_{i}&\text{if there exists }i\in\left\{1,\ldots,n^{E}\right\}\text{ s.t. }\mathbf{q}_{i}^{E}=\mathbf{q}_{k},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\\
\end{array}\right.

As a result, the computation of the entries of the stiffness matrix
K_{{\mathcal{T}}}
and force vector
F_{{\mathcal{T}}}
in
5.9
boils down to that of the local building blocks

(5.11)

K^{E}_{ij}:=a^{E}(\zeta_{i},\zeta_{j})\text{ and }F^{E}_{i}:=\ell^{E}(\zeta_{i}),\quad i,j=1,\ldots,n^{E}.

Unfortunately, no explicit expression of the
\zeta_{i}
is available to realize these computations; the key idea of the Virtual Element Method is to take advantage of the definition of
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
to approximate them with a computation which uses the sole property
5.10
.
To achieve this, let us introduce:

•

The subspace
{\mathcal{W}}_{R}(E)\subset{\mathcal{P}}(E)
of constant functions on
E
:

{\mathcal{W}}_{R}(E):=\text{\rm span}\left\{m_{1}\right\},\text{ where }m_{1}(\mathbf{x})=1.

•

The subspace
{\mathcal{W}}_{C}(E)\subset{\mathcal{P}}(E)
of functions with zero mean value over the vertices of
E
:

{\mathcal{W}}_{C}(E):=\left\{u\in{\mathcal{P}}(E),\>\>\overline{u}=0\right\},

which is spanned by the functions

m_{2}(\mathbf{x})=x_{1}-\overline{q_{1}^{E}},\text{ and }m_{3}(\mathbf{x})=x_{2}-\overline{q^{E}_{2}}.

It is easily seen that
{\mathcal{P}}(E)
is the direct sum of
{\mathcal{W}}_{R}(E)
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{C}(E)
:

{\mathcal{P}}(E)={\mathcal{W}}_{R}(E)\oplus{\mathcal{W}}_{C}(E).

We next introduce projection operators
\pi_{R}
and
\pi_{C}
onto
{\mathcal{W}}_{R}(E)
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{C}(E)
, respectively:
for any (smooth enough) function
v:E\to{\mathbb{R}}
,
\pi_{R}v\in{\mathcal{W}}_{R}(E)
and
\pi_{C}v\in{\mathcal{W}}_{C}(E)
are respectively defined by:

\pi_{R}v(\mathbf{x})=\overline{v},\text{ and }\pi_{C}v(\mathbf{x})=\langle\nabla v\rangle\cdot(\mathbf{x}-\overline{\mathbf{q}^{E}}).

With these notations, the following relations are easily verified:

\forall c\in{\mathcal{W}}_{C}(E),\>\>\pi_{R}c=0,\>\>\pi_{C}c=c\text{ and }\forall r\in{\mathcal{W}}_{R}(E),\>\>\pi_{C}r=0,\>\>\pi_{R}r=r.

We finally define the projection
\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}:{\mathcal{W}}(E)\to{\mathcal{P}}(E)
onto affine functions by:

(5.12)

\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}v=\pi_{C}v+\pi_{R}v.

The following lemma draws elementary, albeit crucial properties of these objects.

Lemma 5.1
.

The following facts hold true:

(i)

The operators
\pi_{R}
and
\pi_{C}
are the “orthogonal projections” from
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
onto
{\mathcal{W}}_{R}(E)
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{C}(E)
, respectively, in the sense of the bilinear form
a^{E}(\cdot,\cdot)
:

\forall r\in{\mathcal{W}}_{R}(E),\>\>c\in{\mathcal{W}}_{C}(E),\>\>v\in{\mathcal{W}}(E),\quad a^{E}(r,v)=0\text{ and }a^{E}(c,v-\pi_{C}v)=0.

(ii)

The following decomposition of
a^{E}(\cdot,\cdot)
holds true:

(5.13)

\forall u,v\in{\mathcal{W}}(E),\quad a^{E}(u,v)=a^{E}(\pi_{C}u,\pi_{C}v)+a^{E}(u-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}u,v-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}v).

Proof.

(i)
The first relation is obvious since functions in
{\mathcal{W}}_{R}(E)
are constant on
E
.
The second one arises from the fact that the gradient
\nabla c
of any function
c\in{\mathcal{W}}_{C}(E)
is constant over
E
:

a^{E}(c,v-\pi_{C}v)=\gamma\nabla c\cdot\left(\int_{E}(\nabla v-\nabla(\pi_{C}v))\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}\right)=\gamma\nabla c\cdot\Big(|E|\langle\nabla v\rangle-|E|\langle\nabla v\rangle\Big)=0.

(ii)
A straightforward calculation based on the identities in
(i)
yields:

\begin{array}[]{>{\displaystyle}cc>{\displaystyle}l}a^{E}(u,v)&=&a^{E}(\pi_{R}u+\pi_{C}u+(u-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}v),\pi_{R}v+\pi_{C}v+(v-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}v))\\
&=&a^{E}(\pi_{C}u+(u-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}v),\pi_{C}v+(v-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}v))\\
&=&a^{E}(\pi_{C}u,\pi_{C}v)+a^{E}(u-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}v,v-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}v).\end{array}

∎

Let us now note that
5.13
gives rise to a decomposition of the local stiffness matrix
K^{E}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n^{E}\times n^{E}}
in
5.11
into two terms:

K^{E}=P^{E}+S^{E}.

Here, the matrices
P^{E}
and
S^{E}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n^{E}\times n^{E}}
are defined by:

\forall i,j=1,\ldots,n^{E},\quad P^{E}_{ij}=a^{E}(\pi_{C}\zeta_{i},\pi_{C}\zeta_{j})\text{ and }S^{E}_{ij}=a^{E}(\zeta_{i}-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\zeta_{i},\zeta_{j}-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\zeta_{j}).

The entries of the matrix
P^{E}
can be calculated exactly; indeed, the projection
\pi_{C}u
of a function
u\in{\mathcal{W}}(E)
has coefficients over the basis
\left\{m_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha=1,2,3}

that can be calculated solely from the values of
u
at the vertices of
E
, see
Section

D.1
about this fact.

On the other hand, the second term
a^{E}(u-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}u,v-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}v)
in the right-hand side of
5.13
(associated to
S^{E}
) appraises the behavior of
a^{E}(\cdot,\cdot)

on those functions in
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
that are not affine. It is not easily computed, and it is therefore replaced by another bilinear form
\widetilde{s}^{E}
(and matrix
\widetilde{S}^{E}
) which can be calculated
just from the values of
u
and
v
at the vertices of
E
(see
Section

D.1
about this calculation):

\widetilde{s}^{E}(u-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}u,v-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}v),\text{ where }\widetilde{s}^{E}(w,z):=\alpha^{E}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n^{E}}w(\mathbf{q}_{i}^{E})z(\mathbf{q}_{i}^{E}),

and
\alpha^{E}
is a constant which is chosen so that
\widetilde{s}^{E}
scale as
a^{E}
upon refinement of the mesh
{\mathcal{T}}
;
in this simple case, we take
\alpha^{E}=1
.

This replacement gives rise to the modified bilinear form
\widetilde{a}^{E}(\cdot,\cdot)
given by:

\forall u,v\in{\mathcal{W}}(E),\quad\widetilde{a}^{E}(u,v)=a^{E}(\pi_{{\mathcal{C}}}u,\pi_{{\mathcal{C}}}v)+\widetilde{s}^{E}(u-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}u,v-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}v).

It guarantees the so-called polynomial consistency of
\widetilde{a}^{E}
with
a^{E}
(or “patch test” in engineering):

\widetilde{a}^{E}(u,v)
and
a^{E}(u,v)
coincide when at least one of the functions
u
or
v
belongs to
{\mathcal{P}}(E)
. Indeed,

\begin{array}[]{>{\displaystyle}cc>{\displaystyle}l}\forall p\in{\mathcal{P}}(E),\>\>v\in{\mathcal{W}}(E),\quad\widetilde{a}^{E}(p,v)&=&a^{E}(\pi_{C}p,\pi_{C}v)\\
&=&a^{E}(\pi_{C}p,v),\\
&=&a^{E}(p,v).\end{array}

This method can be proved to be convergent, see
[
39
]
. Intuitively, it behaves at least as well as if only linear polynomials were the only basis functions on each element.

Remark 5.2
.

The strategy illustrated in this section can be adapted to the solution of eigenvalue problems of the form
2.4
, see

[
59
]
. The main additional operation required to achieve this purpose is the assembly of the mass matrix, which is described in
Section

D.3
.

5.3.
Resolution of the linear elasticity system with the Virtual Element Method

In this section, whose presentation is based on
[
18
,
57
]
,
we briefly describe how the strategy presented in the previous
Section

5.2
can by adapted to deal with the solution of the 2d linear elasticity system. To emphasize the similarities between the treatments of both situations, we use the same notations for corresponding quantities when no confusion is possible.

Let us recall the setting of
Section

2.2.2
: the boundary of the shape
\Omega
is decomposed into three disjoint regions
\Gamma_{D}
,
\Gamma_{N}
,
\Gamma
,
where
\Gamma_{D}
is a clamping area,
\Gamma_{N}
is the support of external loads
\mathbf{g}:\Gamma_{N}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and
\Gamma
is effort-free.
Assuming the presence of body forces
\mathbf{f}:\Omega\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, the elastic displacement
\mathbf{u}:\Omega\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is the unique solution in
H^{1}_{\Gamma_{D}}(\Omega)^{2}
to the boundary value problem:

(
Elas
)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}-\text{\rm div}(Ae(\mathbf{u}))=\mathbf{f}&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\mathbf{u}=\bm{0}&\text{on }\Gamma_{D},\\
Ae(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{g}&\text{on }\Gamma_{N},\\
Ae(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{n}=\bm{0}&\text{on }\Gamma.\end{array}\right.

The associated variational formulation reads, see
2.6
:

\text{Search for }\mathbf{u}\in V\text{ s.t. }\forall\mathbf{v}\in V,\quad a(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=\ell(\mathbf{v}),\text{ where }V=H^{1}_{\Gamma_{D}}(\Omega)^{2},\\
a(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=\int_{\Omega}Ae(\mathbf{u}):e(\mathbf{v})\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x},\text{ and }\ell(\mathbf{v})=\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathbf{v}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}+\int_{\Gamma_{N}} \mathbf{g}\cdot\mathbf{v}\>\text{\rm d}s.

As described in
Section

5.2.1
, the cornerstone of the development of the Virtual Element Method
lies in the definition of the local space of discrete functions
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
and the assembly of the local stiffness matrix
K^{E}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n^{E}\times 2n^{E}}
attached to a given polygon
E
in
{\mathcal{T}}
. The local space
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
reads:

{\mathcal{W}}(E)=\Big\{\mathbf{u}:E\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2},\>\>\mathbf{u}\text{ is affine on each edge of }\partial E\text{ and }-\text{\rm div}(Ae(\mathbf{u}))=0\Big\}.

Again, an element
\mathbf{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}(E)
is uniquely determined by its values at the vertices
\mathbf{q}_{j}^{E}
,
j=1,\ldots,n^{E}
of
E
and

{\mathcal{W}}(E)
has dimension
2n^{E}
.
Besides,
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
contains the space
{\mathcal{P}}(E)
of affine functions, given by:

{\mathcal{P}}(E):=\left\{\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{a}+B\mathbf{x},\>\>\mathbf{a}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2},\>\>B\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2\times 2}\right\}.

Let us introduce the natural basis
\left\{{\bm{\zeta}}_{j}\right\}_{j=1,\ldots,2n^{E}}
of
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
, defined by, for
i=1,\ldots,n^{E}
:

(5.14)

{\bm{\zeta}}_{2i-1}(\mathbf{q}_{i}^{E})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
0\end{array}\right),\text{ and }{\bm{\zeta}}_{2i-1}(\mathbf{q}_{j}^{E})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
0\end{array}\right)\text{ for }j\neq i;

(5.15)

{\bm{\zeta}}_{2i}(\mathbf{q}_{i}^{E})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
1\end{array}\right),\text{ and }{\bm{\zeta}}_{2i}(\mathbf{q}_{j}^{E})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
0\end{array}\right)\text{ for }j\neq i.

In a similar state of mind as in
Section

5.2
, among the elements in
{\mathcal{P}}(E)
, we shall distinguish the rigid-body motions
\mathbf{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{R}(E)
, whose strain
e(\mathbf{u})
equals
0
,
and the fields
\mathbf{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{C}(E)
with constant strain
e(\mathbf{u})
; precisely:

•

The subspace
{\mathcal{W}}_{R}(E)\subset{\mathcal{P}}(E)
of rigid-body motions is defined by

{\mathcal{W}}_{R}(E):=\left\{\mathbf{a}+M(\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{q}}^{E}),\>\>\mathbf{a}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2},\>\>M\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2\times 2},\>\>M^{T}=-M\right\};

it is spanned by the three vector fields:

(5.16)

\mathbf{r}_{1}(\mathbf{x})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
0\end{array}\right),\quad\mathbf{r}_{2}(\mathbf{x})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
1\end{array}\right)\text{ and }\mathbf{r}_{3}(\mathbf{x})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-(x_{2}-\overline{q_{2}^{E}})\\
x_{1}-\overline{q_{1}^{E}}\end{array}\right).

•

The subspace
{\mathcal{W}}_{C}(E)\subset{\mathcal{P}}(E)
of vector fields with constant strain is defined by

{\mathcal{W}}_{C}(E):=\left\{S(\mathbf{x}-\overline{\mathbf{q}^{E}}),\>\>S\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2\times 2},\>\>S^{T}=S\right\};

it is spanned by the three vector fields:

(5.17)

\mathbf{c}_{1}(\mathbf{x})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x_{1}-\overline{q^{E}_{1}}\\
0\end{array}\right),\quad\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mathbf{x})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
x_{2}-\overline{q^{E}_{2}}\end{array}\right)\text{ and }\mathbf{c}_{3}(\mathbf{x})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x_{2}-\overline{q_{2}^{E}}\\
x_{1}-\overline{q_{1}^{E}}\end{array}\right).

Again, it is easily verified that
{\mathcal{P}}(E)
is the direct sum of
{\mathcal{W}}_{R}(E)
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{C}(E)
:

{\mathcal{P}}(E)={\mathcal{W}}_{R}(E)\oplus{\mathcal{W}}_{C}(E).

We next define the projections
\pi_{R}
and
\pi_{C}
from
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
onto
{\mathcal{W}}_{R}(E)
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{C}(E)
, respectively.

•

For any (smooth enough) vector field
\mathbf{u}:E\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
\pi_{R}\mathbf{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{R}(E)
is defined by:

\pi_{R}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x})=\bar{\mathbf{u}}+\langle\omega(\mathbf{u})\rangle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-(x_{2}-\overline{q^{E}_{2}})\\
x_{1}-\overline{q^{E}_{1}}\end{array}\right),\quad\mathbf{x}\in E;

where
\omega(\mathbf{u}):=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial u_{2}}{\partial x_{1}}-\frac{\partial u_{1}}{\partial x_{2}}\right)
is (half) the scalar curl of
\mathbf{u}
.

•

For any (smooth enough) vector field
\mathbf{u}:E\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
\pi_{C}\mathbf{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{C}(E)
is defined by:

\pi_{C}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{x})=\langle e(\mathbf{u})\rangle(\mathbf{x}-\overline{\mathbf{q}^{E}}).

The following relations are then easily verified:

\forall\mathbf{r}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{R}(E),\>\>\pi_{C}\mathbf{r}=\bm{0},\>\>\pi_{R}\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{r},\text{ and }\forall\mathbf{c}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{C}(E),\>\>\pi_{R}\mathbf{c}=\bm{0},\>\>\pi_{C}\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{c}.

From these data, we define the projection operator
\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}
onto affine functions in
{\mathcal{P}}(E)
by:

(5.18)

\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathbf{u}=\pi_{C}\mathbf{u}+\pi_{R}\mathbf{u}.

The next lemma is the counterpart of
Lemma

5.1
in the present linear elasticity setting; its very similar proof is omitted for brevity.

Lemma 5.2
.

The following facts hold true:

(i)

The operators
\pi_{R}
and
\pi_{C}
are the “orthogonal projections” from
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
onto
{\mathcal{W}}_{R}(E)
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{C}(E)
, respectively, in the sense of the bilinear form
a^{E}(\cdot,\cdot)
:

\forall\mathbf{r}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{R}(E),\>\>\mathbf{c}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{C}(E),\>\>\mathbf{v}\in{\mathcal{W}}(E),\quad a^{E}(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{v})=0\text{ and }a^{E}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{v}-\pi_{C}\mathbf{v})=0.

(ii)

The following decomposition of
a^{E}(\cdot,\cdot)
holds:

(5.19)

\forall\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\in{\mathcal{W}}(E),\quad a^{E}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=a^{E}(\pi_{C}\mathbf{u},\pi_{C}\mathbf{v})+a^{E}(\mathbf{u}-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathbf{v}).

Let us now discuss the calculation of the local stiffness matrix

K^{E}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n^{E}\times 2n^{E}},\quad K^{E}_{ij}=a({\bm{\zeta}}_{i},{\bm{\zeta}}_{j}),\>\>i,j=1,\ldots,2n^{E}.

Like in
Section

5.2.2
,
5.19
induces a convenient decomposition
K^{E}
into two blocks:

K^{E}=P^{E}+S^{E},\quad P^{E},S^{E}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n^{E}\times 2n^{E}}.

Here,

•

The first term in the right-hand side of
5.19
gives rise to the matrix
P^{E}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n^{E}\times 2n^{E}}
defined by:

\forall i,j=1,\ldots,2n^{E},\quad P^{E}_{ij}=a^{E}(\pi_{C}{\bm{\zeta}}_{i},\pi_{C}{\bm{\zeta}}_{j}),

which roughly speaking corresponds to the block of
K^{E}
over
{\mathcal{P}}(E)
.
The entries of
P^{E}
can be calculated exactly, since the coefficients of the projection
\pi_{C}\mathbf{u}
of a vector field
\mathbf{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}(E)
in the basis
\left\{\mathbf{c}_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha=1,2,3}
can be calculated from the values of
\mathbf{u}
at the vertices of
E
, see
Section

D.2
.

•

The second term in the right-hand side of
5.19
appraises the behavior of the bilinear form
a^{E}(\cdot,\cdot)

over those functions in
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
that are not affine. Again, this second block is replaced by a suitable quantity which can be calculated
just from the values of
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}
at the vertices of
E
.

As the result of these considerations, we replaced the local bilinear form
a^{E}(\cdot,\cdot)
with

\widetilde{a}^{E}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=a^{E}(\pi_{C}\mathbf{u},\pi_{C}\mathbf{v})+\widetilde{s}^{E}(\mathbf{u}-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathbf{v}),\text{ where }\widetilde{s}^{E}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=\alpha^{E}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{u}(\mathbf{q}_{i})\cdot\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{q}_{i}),

and the coefficient
\alpha^{E}
depends on the element
E
so that
\widetilde{s}^{E}
scale as
a^{E}
upon refinement of the mesh
{\mathcal{T}}
.
The implementation details of this methodology are presented in
Section

D.2
.

6.
Sensitivity of a shape functional with respect to seed points and volume fractions

In this section, we return to our main purpose of solving the shape and topology optimization problem

(
P
)

\min\limits_{\Omega}J(\Omega)\>\>\text{ s.t. }\mathbf{G}(\Omega)=0,

where
J(\Omega)
and
\mathbf{G}(\Omega)
depend on
\Omega
via the solution
u_{\Omega}
to a boundary value problem such as
Cond
or
Elas
.
We describe how the shape derivative of a general objective function
J(\Omega)
(or similarly, that of a general constraint function
\mathbf{G}(\Omega)
) can be expressed in terms of the defining variables of a diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
representing
\Omega
in the sense that
3.6
holds.
In this perspective, in
Section

6.1
, we first deal with the discretization of
J(\Omega)
into a function
J(\mathbf{q})
of the vertices
\mathbf{q}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dM}
of the diagram,
and we expose how the theoretical expression of the shape derivative
J^{\prime}(\Omega)({\bm{\theta}})
allows to calculate the gradient
\nabla_{\mathbf{q}}J(\mathbf{q})
of this discrete function.
As we have mentioned,
J(\mathbf{q})
(resp.
\mathbf{G}(q)
) in turn induces a function
\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
(resp.
\widetilde{G}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
) of the seed points
\mathbf{s}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
and cell measures
{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
of the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
.
This gives rise to our discrete shape and topology optimization problem:

(
P-disc
)

\min\limits_{(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})\>\>\text{ s.t. } \widetilde{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})=0.

We explain in
Section

6.2
how the sensitivities
\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
and
\nabla_{{\bm{\nu}}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
of
J(\Omega)
with respect to the parameters
(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
of the diagram for
\Omega
can be inferred from the gradient
\nabla_{\mathbf{q}}J(\mathbf{q})
.
In
Section

6.3
, we recall the Hilbertian trick, which allows among other things to smooth these sensitivities in a consistent manner. This procedure is also a key algorithmic component of the constrained optimization algorithm used to tackle
P
, that we finally describe in
Section

6.4
.

Notation.
In this section, the shape
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is described by a modified diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
of the form
3.6
and
3.7
. We consider a generic function
J(\Omega)
of
\Omega
, standing for either the objective or a constraint in
P
.
To simplify the presentation and without loss of generality, we shall sometimes assume that the space dimension
d
equals
2
,
and that
J(\Omega)
is of the form

(6.1)

J(\Omega)=\int_{\Omega}j(u_{\Omega})\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x},

where
j:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a smooth function, satisfying adequate growth conditions, and
u_{\Omega}
is the solution to the conductivity equation
Cond
.
As we have hinted at,
J(\Omega)
induces several discrete versions, one depending on the vertices
\mathbf{q}
of the diagram, that we shall denote by
J(\mathbf{q})
with a small abuse of notation, and in turn, another function
\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
, depending on the seed points
\mathbf{s}
and the cell measures
{\bm{\nu}}
.

6.1.
Discretization and differentiation of
J(\Omega)
in terms of the vertices of a diagram for
\Omega

In this section, we discuss the discretization of the shape functional
J(\Omega)
as a function
J(\mathbf{q})
of the vertices
\mathbf{q}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dM}
of the diagram

\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
representing
\Omega
as
3.6
and the calculation of the gradient
\nabla_{\mathbf{q}}J(\mathbf{q})
.

As we have mentioned, in our applications,
J(\Omega)
depends on
\Omega
via the solution
u_{\Omega}

to a boundary value problem posed on
\Omega
, see e.g.
6.1
.
The latter is solved along the lines of
Section

5
by applying the Virtual Element Method on the polygonal mesh
{\mathcal{T}}
resulting from the discretization of the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
, see
Section

4
. The discrete solution is a function of the vertices
\mathbf{q}
of the mesh
{\mathcal{T}}
, and it gives rise in turn to a discrete objective function
J(\mathbf{q})
once the formula
6.1
is discretized by suitable quadrature formulas.
In the present work, we rely on very basic expressions, which are exemplified in the forthcoming discussion.
More elaborate rules are available in the context of general polygonal elements
[
34
,
115
]
;
for brevity, we do not emphasize on this classical, albeit technical issue.

The calculation of the gradient
\nabla_{\mathbf{q}}J(\mathbf{q})
is a slightly more delicate issue. It falls under the “discretize-then-optimize” setting, in which
the derivatives of the discrete quantities of interest are used
[
100
]
; several methods can be used to achieve this goal.
One convenient practice relies on automatic differentiation, see e.g.
[
85
]
for an introduction in the shape optimization context. In a nutshell, the gradient of the mapping
\mathbf{q}\mapsto J(\mathbf{q})
can be calculated concurrently with its evaluation by leveraging adequate data structures and by implementing all the constituent operations of the evaluation pipeline (including the assembly and inversion of the stiffness matrix involved in calculations by the Virtual Element Method) together with their derivatives.
This elegant framework is unfortunately very stringent, and it is not always compatible with realistic applications where the mechanical solver is used in a black-box fashion.

When automatic differentiation of
J(\mathbf{q})
is not available, the computation of
\nabla_{\mathbf{q}}J(\mathbf{q})
can be realized “by hand”.
In fact, the derivatives of the aforementioned building blocks of the evaluation of
\mathbf{q}\mapsto J(\mathbf{q})
can be calculated explicitly, in principle.
Even though this strategy could be adopted in our context, it is still very intrusive as it requires a complete knowledge of the mechanical solver, notably.

For this reason, we consider yet another option, borrowing from the “optimize-then-discretize” paradigm in optimal design, which mainly relies on the continuous formula for the shape derivative
J^{\prime}(\Omega)({\bm{\theta}})
.
More precisely, we leverage an elegant remark from
[
19
]
: the sensitivity
\frac{\partial J}{\partial\mathbf{q}_{i}}(\mathbf{q})(\mathbf{h}_{i})

of
J(\mathbf{q})
to a perturbation of the
i^{\text{th}}
vertex
\mathbf{q}_{i}
in the direction
\mathbf{h}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
can be approximated by evaluating (a discretization of) the volume form of the continuous shape derivative
{\bm{\theta}}\mapsto J^{\prime}(\Omega)({\bm{\theta}})
with the particular deformation field
{\bm{\theta}}=\varphi_{i}\mathbf{h}_{i}
, where
\varphi_{i}
is the unique function in the discrete space
{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
given by
5.5
taking the value
1
at vertex
i
and
0
elsewhere, see
5.6
:

\frac{\partial J}{\partial\mathbf{q}_{i}}(\mathbf{q})(\mathbf{h}_{i})=J^{\prime}(\Omega)(\varphi_{i}\mathbf{h}_{i}),\text{ where }\varphi_{i}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}\text{ is defined by: }\varphi_{i}(\mathbf{q}_{j})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}1&\text{if }i=j,\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

In a nutshell, this practice is legitimated by the fact that the theoretical formula for the volume form of
J^{\prime}(\Omega)({\bm{\theta}})
can be obtained by the exact same adjoint-based trail as that for the discrete derivative
\frac{\partial J}{\partial\mathbf{q}_{i}}(\mathbf{q})(\mathbf{h}_{i})
.
We refer to e.g.
[
62
]
and
\lx@sectionsign 2.9
in
[
64
]
for related discussions about the (non) commutation of discretization and differentiation in optimal design.

In order to describe this possibility more precisely, let us assume that the shape derivative
J^{\prime}(\Omega)({\bm{\theta}})
is available under the following volume form
2.9
:

J^{\prime}(\Omega)({\bm{\theta}})=\int_{\Omega}(\mathbf{t}_{\Omega}\cdot{\bm{\theta}}+S_{\Omega}:\nabla{\bm{\theta}})\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x},\text{ for some }\mathbf{t}_{\Omega}:\Omega\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\text{ and }S_{\Omega}:\Omega\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d},

and let us suppose for a moment that the fields
\mathbf{t}_{\Omega}
and
S_{\Omega}
are given approximate, discrete counterparts
\mathbf{t}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
and
S_{{\mathcal{T}}}
that are constant in restriction to each element
E\in{\mathcal{T}}
:

(6.2)

J^{\prime}(\Omega)({\bm{\theta}})\approx J^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{T}}}({\bm{\theta}}):=\int_{\Omega}(\mathbf{t}_{{\mathcal{T}}}\cdot{\bm{\theta}}+S_{{\mathcal{T}}}:\nabla{\bm{\theta}})\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}.

The gradient of
J(\mathbf{q})
with respect to the coordinates of the
i^{\text{th}}
vertex of the mesh is then approximated as:

\nabla_{\mathbf{q}_{i}}J(\mathbf{q})\approx\left(\begin{array}[]{c}J^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{T}}}({\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{1})\\
\vdots\\
J^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{T}}}({\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{d})\end{array}\right),

where for
k=1,\ldots,d
,
{\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{k}\in{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}^{d}
is the unique vector-valued discrete function such that:

(6.3)

{\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{k}(\mathbf{q}_{j})=\mathbf{e}_{k}\text{ if }i=j\text{ and }\mathbf{0}\text{ otherwise}.

The aforementioned approximations of
\mathbf{t}_{\Omega}
,
S_{\Omega}
by
\mathbf{t}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
and
S_{{\mathcal{T}}}
can be realized in various ways. For instance one could use the simple formulas:

\mathbf{t}_{{\mathcal{T}}}\lvert_{E}=\frac{1}{n^{E}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n^{E}}\mathbf{t}_{\Omega}(\mathbf{q}_{j}^{E}),\text{ and }S_{{\mathcal{T}}}\lvert_{E}=\frac{1}{n^{E}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n^{E}}S_{\Omega}(\mathbf{q}_{j}^{E}),

where we have used the notations of
Section

5.1
.

When
S_{\Omega}
involves the gradient of the state function
u_{\Omega}
, say for instance

S_{\Omega}=\gamma\nabla u_{\Omega}\otimes\nabla u_{\Omega}
, one could also make the approximation:

S_{{\mathcal{T}}}\lvert_{E}=\gamma_{E}\nabla(\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}u)\otimes\nabla(\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}u),\text{ where }\gamma_{E}:=\frac{1}{n^{E}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n^{E}}\gamma(\mathbf{q}_{j}^{E}),

and
\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}
is the projector over first-order polynomial functions in
{\mathcal{P}}(E)
, defined in
5.18
.

In any event, the integrals featured in the quantities
J^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{T}}}({\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{k})
can be naturally decomposed as sums of integrals running over the elements
E
of the mesh
{\mathcal{T}}
:

J_{{\mathcal{T}}}^{\prime}({\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{k})=\sum\limits_{E\in{\mathcal{T}}}\Big(\mathbf{t}_{{\mathcal{T}}}\lvert_{E}\cdot\mathbf{t}_{E}({\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{k})+S_{{\mathcal{T}}}\lvert_{E}:S_{E}({\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{k})\Big),

where the vectors
\mathbf{t}_{E}({\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{k})
and matrices
S_{E}({\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{k})
are defined by:

\mathbf{t}_{E}({\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{k})=\int_{E}{\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{k}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}\text{ and of the matrices }S_{E}({\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{k})=\int_{E}\nabla{\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{k}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}.

These basic ingredients can be calculated or approximated in explicit terms of the physical characteristics of the element
E
.
For instance, in the case where the space dimension
d
equals
2
, we approximate
\mathbf{t}_{E}(\theta_{i}^{1})
and
\mathbf{t}_{E}(\theta_{i}^{2})
as:

\mathbf{t}_{E}({\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{1})\approx\frac{1}{n^{E}}\lvert E\lvert\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
0\end{array}\right)\text{ and }\mathbf{t}_{E}({\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{2})\approx\frac{1}{n^{E}}\lvert E\lvert\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
1\end{array}\right).

As far as the matrices
S_{E}({\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{1})
,
S_{E}({\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{2})
are concerned, similar integrations by parts to those used in
Section

D.1
yield:

S_{E}({\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{1})=\frac{1}{2}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\lvert\widehat{\mathbf{e}_{j}}\lvert(\mathbf{n}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}_{j}}})_{1}&\lvert\widehat{\mathbf{e}_{j}}\lvert(\mathbf{n}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}_{j}}})_{2}\\
0&0\end{array}\right),\text{ and }S_{E}({\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{2})=\frac{1}{2}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
\lvert\widehat{\mathbf{e}_{j}}\lvert(\mathbf{n}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}_{j}}})_{1}&\lvert\widehat{\mathbf{e}_{j}}\lvert(\mathbf{n}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}_{j}}})_{2}\end{array}\right).

Remark 6.1
.

In principle, the continuous shape derivative
J^{\prime}(\Omega)({\bm{\theta}})
depends only
on the values of the deformation
{\bm{\theta}}
on the boundary
\partial\Omega
, see
Section

2.3
about this general feature.
On the contrary, it is not exactly true that the approximation
J^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{T}}}({\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{k})
in
6.2
vanishes when the deformation
{\bm{\theta}}_{i}^{k}
is associated to an internal vertex
\mathbf{q}_{i}
. This is a manifestation of the lack of commutation between the operations of discretization and differentiation in optimal design, see again
[
62
]
and
\lx@sectionsign 2.9
in
[
64
]
.
However, we expect (and indeed verify) that if the approximation
6.2
is “good enough”, the entries of
\nabla_{\mathbf{q}}J(\mathbf{q})
attached to internal vertices should be “very small” when compared to those related to boundary vertices. For this reason, it is enough to compute the quantities
J^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{T}}}({\bm{\theta}}_{j}^{k})
for the indices
j=1,\ldots,M
of the boundary vertices of
\Omega
.

6.2.
Calculation of the derivative of a functional with respect to the positions of the seeds and the cell measures

We have seen in
Section

6.1
how a general function of the domain
J(\Omega)

can be discretized into a function
J(\mathbf{q})
depending on the positions
\mathbf{q}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dM}
of the vertices of the mesh
{\mathcal{T}}
, and how to calculate the gradient
\nabla_{\mathbf{q}}J(\mathbf{q})
.
In turn,
\mathbf{q}
depends on the seed points
\mathbf{s}
and the weights
{\bm{\psi}}
of the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
of
\Omega
, and, again, on the seed points
\mathbf{s}
and the cell measures
{\bm{\nu}}
, which are the design variables of our discrete version of the shape and topology optimization problem
P
, as explained in
Section

3.3
.
This raises the need to express the derivative of a general function
J(\mathbf{q})
of the vertices
\mathbf{q}
of the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
in terms of
\mathbf{s}
and
{\bm{\nu}}
.

To formulate this issue rigorously, let us write the relation between the seeds points
\mathbf{s}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
, the weights
{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
and the vertices
\mathbf{q}
of the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
under the abstract form:

(6.4)

\mathbf{q}=\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})),

where
\mathbf{Q}:{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}_{\mathbf{s}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}_{{\bm{\psi}}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{dM}
produces the collection of vertices of the mesh
{\mathcal{T}}

induced by
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
, and, as in
Section

3.2
,
{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
is the unique weight vector such that each cell
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))
has measure
\nu_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,N
. We then wish to calculate the derivatives of the functional
\widetilde{J}:{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}_{\mathbf{s}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}_{\bm{\nu}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
defined by:

(6.5)

\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}):=J(\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})).

This is the purpose of the next result.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
J:{\mathbb{R}}^{dM}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a differentiable function.
Then the composite function
\widetilde{J}:{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}_{\mathbf{s}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}_{\bm{\nu}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
in
6.5
is differentiable almost everywhere, and its derivatives read:

(6.6)

\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})=\Big[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{Q}(s,{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))\Big]^{T}\nabla_{\mathbf{q}}J(\mathbf{q})+\Big[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))\Big]^{T}\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}),

and

(6.7)

\nabla_{\bm{\nu}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})=-\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}).

Here, the function
\mathbf{F}:{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}_{\mathbf{s}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}_{\bm{\nu}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}_{\bm{\psi}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
is the derivative
\nabla_{\bm{\psi}}K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})
, see
3.19
;
the adjoint vector
\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
is defined as the solution to the
N\times N
system:

(6.8)

\Big[\nabla_{{\bm{\psi}}}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))\Big]^{T}\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})=-\Big[\nabla_{\bm{\psi}}\mathbf{Q}(s,{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))\Big]^{T}\nabla_{\mathbf{q}}J(\mathbf{q}).

In these formulas,

•

The gradient
\nabla_{\mathbf{q}}J(\mathbf{q})
is a vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{dM}
, whose expression is assumed to be known.

•

The matrices
\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))\right]
and
\left[\nabla_{\bm{\psi}}\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))\right]
have respective sizes
dM\times dN
and
dM\times N
;
their explicit expressions (in 2d) are the subject of
Appendix

A
.

•

The matrix
\left[\nabla_{{\bm{\psi}}}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))\right]^{T}
has size
N\times N
, and its calculation (in 2d) is provided in
Appendix

B
.

Proof.

At first,
Proposition

A.1
below states that the mapping
\mathbf{Q}
is differentiable at any point
(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
satisfying the assumptions of
Theorem

3.1
. These points form an open subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
whose complement has null Lebesgue measure.
Moreover, for any such point
(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, we have seen that
{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
is the unique solution to the equation
3.19
. By the implicit function theorem and the regularity of
\mathbf{F}
expressed in
Theorem

3.1
, it follows that
{\bm{\psi}}^{*}
is differentiable at such a point
(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
, and
by composition,
\widetilde{J}
is differentiable at a.e. point
(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
.

Let us now prove
6.6
.
At any point
(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
where
\widetilde{J}
is differentiable, the chain rule shows that:

\forall\mathbf{h}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN},\quad\frac{\partial\widetilde{J}}{\partial\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})(\mathbf{h})=\frac{\partial J}{\partial\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{q})\left(\frac{\partial\mathbf{Q}}{\partial\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))(\mathbf{h})+\frac{\partial\mathbf{Q}}{\partial{\bm{\psi}}}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))\left(\frac{\partial{\bm{\psi}}^{*}}{\partial\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})(\mathbf{h})\right)\right),

where we have set
\mathbf{q}:=\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))
. This rewrites, in terms of gradients and matrices:

(6.9)

\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})=\Big(\Big[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))\Big]^{T}+\Big[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})\Big]^{T}\Big[\nabla_{\bm{\psi}}\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))\Big]^{T}\Big)\nabla_{\mathbf{q}}J(\mathbf{q}).

All the terms are explicit in this formula, except for the matrix
\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})\right]
.
The calculation of the latter is difficult,
since the function
\mathbf{s}\mapsto{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
is only known implicitly, through the relation
3.19
.
To overcome this difficulty, we use the classical adjoint technique for the differentiation of a quantity depending on the solution to a parametrized implicit equation,
see again
[
78
,
101
]
.
Differentiation with respect to
\mathbf{s}
in
3.19
yields:

(6.10)

\underbrace{\Big[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))\Big]}_{N\times dN\text{ matrix}}+\underbrace{\Big[\nabla_{{\bm{\psi}}}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))\Big]}_{N\times N\text{ matrix}}\underbrace{\Big[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})\Big]}_{N\times dN\text{ matrix}}=0.

We then introduce the adjoint state
\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, defined as the solution to the
N\times N
linear system:

\Big[\nabla_{{\bm{\psi}}}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))\Big]^{T}\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})=-\Big[\nabla_{\bm{\psi}}\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))\Big]^{T}\nabla J_{\mathbf{q}}(\mathbf{q}).

Injecting this identity into
6.9
, we obtain:

\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})=\Big[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))\Big]^{T}\nabla_{\mathbf{q}}J(\mathbf{q})-\Big[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})\Big]^{T}\Big[\nabla_{{\bm{\psi}}}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))\Big]^{T}\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}).

Eventually, using
6.10
to transform the last expression in the above right-hand side, we obtain:

\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})=\Big[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))\Big]^{T}\nabla_{\mathbf{q}}J(\mathbf{q})+\Big[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))\Big]^{T}\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}),

which is the desired formula.

Let us then consider the second formula
6.7
.

Again, a simple use of the chain rule yields:

(6.11)

\begin{array}[]{ccl}\nabla_{{\bm{\nu}}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})&=&\nabla_{{\bm{\nu}}}\Big(J(\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})))\Big)\\
&=&\Big[\nabla_{\bm{\nu}}{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})\Big]^{T}\Big[\nabla_{\bm{\psi}}\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))\Big]^{T}\nabla_{\mathbf{q}}J(\mathbf{q}),\end{array}

where
\mathbf{q}:=\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))
.
As in the proof of
6.6
, we rely on the adjoint method to eliminate the difficult term
\left[\nabla_{\bm{\nu}}{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})\right]^{T}
from this expression.
Recalling the defining system
6.8
for
\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, we obtain immediately:

\begin{array}[]{>{\displaystyle}cc>{\displaystyle}l}\nabla_{{\bm{\nu}}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})&=&-\Big[\nabla_{\bm{\nu}}{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})\Big]^{T}\Big[\nabla_{\bm{\psi}}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}))\Big]^{T}\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})\\
&=&-\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}),\end{array}

which is the desired expression.
∎

Remark 6.2
.

When the optimized shape
\Omega
is only one phase within a large computational domain
D

and is represented as a subcollection of the cells of a “true” Laguerre diagram
\mathbf{Lag}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
of
D
, of the form
3.3
and
3.4
,
similar considerations show that formula
6.7
reads instead:

\nabla_{\bm{\nu}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})=-\left(\mathbf{p}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})-\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})\right).

Remark 6.3
.

The geometric calculations needed in the present section for the expressions of the matrices
\Big[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})\Big]
,
\Big[\nabla_{{\bm{\psi}}}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})\Big]
,
\Big[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})\Big]
and
\Big[\nabla_{{\bm{\psi}}}\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})\Big]
are provided in
Appendices

A
and
B
. They need only be implemented once and for all.
In particular, this implementation is independent of the particular objective function.

6.3.
The Hilbertian method

We have described in the previous
Sections

6.1
and
6.2
how to calculate the sensitivities
\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
and
\nabla_{\bm{\nu}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
of a function
J
,
depending on the domain
\Omega
via the seeds
\mathbf{s}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
and the cell measures
{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
of a diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
of the form
3.6
.
Unfortunately, a direct use of these derivatives in the solution of our discrete shape and topology optimization problem
P-disc
often proves awkward in practice:
on the one hand, they may show very irregular variations in space (i.e. they may have very different components along the indices
i=1,\ldots,N
of neighboring cells), thus causing numerical instabilities. On the other hand, in multiple applications,
it is desirable to handle sensitivities that take into account certain requirements, for instance that some of their components associated to cells located in non optimizable regions of space should vanish.
In this section, we explain how the so-called Hilbertian method allows to achieve both purposes with rigorous guarantees that the resulting modified sensitivities retain descent properties. The application in shape optimization of this classical procedure in the context of gradient flows is exposed in e.g.
[
7
,
30
,
44
,
85
]
.

6.3.1.
The general strategy

Let us describe the use of the Hilbertian method in the illustrative perspective of regularizing the gradient
\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
of a discrete objective function
\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
at a given point
(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
.

To this end, let us recall that
\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
is obtained by identifying the derivative
\mathbf{h}\mapsto\frac{\partial\widetilde{J}}{\partial\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})(\mathbf{h})

with an element in the space
{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
via the following relation:

\forall\mathbf{h}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN},\quad\frac{\partial\widetilde{J}}{\partial\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})(\mathbf{h})=\left\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}),\mathbf{h}\right\rangle.

The main idea of the Hilbertian method is to introduce a subspace
V
of the space
{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
of seed points,
equipped with an inner product
a_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,\cdot)
different from the canonical one
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
,
and to search for the gradient
\mathbf{h}_{J}\in V
of
\widetilde{J}(\cdot,{\bm{\nu}})
with respect to this inner product. In other terms, we solve the following variational problem:

(6.12)

\text{Search for }\mathbf{h}_{J}\in V\text{ s.t. for all }\mathbf{h}\in V,\quad a_{\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{h}_{J},\mathbf{h})=\frac{\partial\widetilde{J}}{\partial\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})(\mathbf{h}).

Depending on the nature of the space
V
and its inner product
a_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,\cdot)
, the gradient
\mathbf{h}_{J}
may for instance be more regular than its counterpart
\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
associated to the canonical inner product, some of its components may vanish, etc.

Our choices of
V
and
a_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,\cdot)
in the present context rely on the smoothing effect of the discrete Laplace operator acting on mass–spring networks,
see for instance
[
106
]
or
[
87
]
for reviews about the role of such discrete Laplace operators in the field of computer graphics.
We take
V={\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
and search for the regularized gradient of
\widetilde{J}(\cdot,{\bm{\nu}})
at
\mathbf{s}
as the solution
\mathbf{h}_{J}:=\left\{\mathbf{h}_{J,i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
to the following minimization problem:

(6.13)

\min\limits_{\mathbf{h}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}}E(\mathbf{h}),\text{ where }E(\mathbf{h}):=\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}\sum\limits_{(i,j)\in{\mathcal{E}}\atop j>i}\lvert\mathbf{h}_{i}-\mathbf{h}_{j}\lvert^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\lvert\mathbf{h}_{i}\lvert^{2}-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\left\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{s}_{i}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}),\mathbf{h}_{i}\right\rangle,

where
\alpha
is a user-defined parameter encoding a regularization length scale. Intuitively, the solution
\mathbf{h}_{J}
to
6.13
is a version of
\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
whose spatial variations are averaged over a region with radius
\alpha
.
The strictly convex optimization problem
6.13
has a unique solution, which is characterized by the following first-order optimality condition:

\text{For }\>i=1,\ldots,N,\quad\alpha^{2}\sum\limits_{j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{i}}(\mathbf{h}_{i}-\mathbf{h}_{j})+\mathbf{h}_{i}=\nabla_{\mathbf{s}_{i}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}).

This rewrites as a
2N\times 2N
matrix system:

(\alpha^{2}A_{\mathbf{s}}+\text{\rm I})\mathbf{h}=\nabla_{\mathbf{s}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}}),

where the matrix
A_{\mathbf{s}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2N\times 2N}
is given by the entries:

(6.14)

A_{\mathbf{s},2(i-1)+1,2(j-1)+1}=A_{\mathbf{s},2(i-1)+2,2(j-1)+2}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\#{\mathcal{N}}_{i}&\text{if }j=i,\\
-1&\text{if }j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{i},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{array}\right.\text{ and } \\
A_{\mathbf{s},2(i-1)+1,2(j-1)+2}=A_{\mathbf{s},2(i-1)+2,2(j-1)+1}=0,\quad i,j=1,\ldots,N.

The inner product
a_{\mathbf{s}}(\cdot,\cdot)
on
V={\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
associated to this strategy is:

(6.15)

\forall\>\mathbf{h}^{1},\mathbf{h}^{2}\in V,\quad a_{\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{h}^{1},\mathbf{h}^{2})=\Big\langle(\alpha^{2}A_{\mathbf{s}}+\text{\rm I})\mathbf{h}^{1},\mathbf{h}^{2}\Big\rangle.

Remark 6.4
.

A similar strategy can be applied to impose smooth spatial variations of the gradient
\nabla_{{\bm{\nu}}}\widetilde{J}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
of
\widetilde{J}
with respect to the measures of cells. One then relies on the choice
V={\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, equipped with the inner product:

(6.16)

a_{{\bm{\nu}}}({\bm{\nu}}^{1},{\bm{\nu}}^{2})=\Big\langle A_{\nu}{\bm{\nu}}^{1},{\bm{\nu}}^{2}\Big\rangle,\text{ where }A_{{\bm{\nu}},ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\#{\mathcal{N}}_{i}&\text{if }j=i,\\
-1&\text{if }j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{i},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{array}\right.

6.3.2.
Imposing that seeds stay inside the computational domain

The Hilbertian method naturally allows to enforce certain constraints on the regularized sensitivity
\mathbf{h}_{J}
of
J
with respect to the seed points
\mathbf{s}
in
6.12
. For instance
\mathbf{h}_{J}
can be imposed to have null components over the indices
i
inside a given subset
I\subset\left\{1,\ldots,N\right\}
of the seed points by simply taking the following Hilbert space
V
in
6.12
:

V:=\left\{\mathbf{h}=(\mathbf{h}_{1},\ldots,h_{N})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}\text{ s.t. }\mathbf{h}_{i}=\mathbf{0}\text{ for }i\in I\right\}.

In this section, we exemplify how the seed points
\mathbf{s}
of the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
featured in
Algorithm

1
can be constrained to stay inside the computational domain
D
during the optimization process thanks to this practice, see also
Remark

3.1
about this point.

Let
(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
be a given stage of the process of
Algorithm

1
, and let
{\bm{\psi}}={\bm{\psi}}^{*}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}})
.
To simplify the presentation, we assume that the seed points of the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
are numbered in such a way that the first
B\leq N
ones are those adjacent to the external boundary
\partial D
.
