This work was completed while C.D. was visiting the Laboratoire Jacques-Louis Lions from Sorbonne Universités, whose hospitality is gratefully acknowledged.
E.O. was also supported by the “Institut Universitaire de France”.
The work of B.L. is supported by the COSMOGRAM-launchpad Inria Action Exploratoire grant.

Appendix A
Calculation and differentiation of the coordinates of the vertices of a diagram with respect to its seed points and weights

In this appendix, we detail the calculation of the positions of the vertices
\mathbf{q}=\left\{\mathbf{q}_{j}\right\}_{j=1,\ldots,M}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dM}
of the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
in
3.6

from the datum of its seed points
\mathbf{s}
and weights
{\bm{\psi}}
. We also investigate the calculation of their derivatives with respect to
\mathbf{s}
and
{\bm{\psi}}
,
which is a central ingredient in the procedure of
Section

6.2
for expressing differentiation of functions with respect to the positions
\mathbf{q}
of the vertices of the diagram in terms of the generating seed points
\mathbf{s}
and weights
{\bm{\psi}}
.
These formulas are admittedly not new, but their rigorous exposition and establishment are not so easily found in the literature.
Our presentation focuses on the case
d=2
, but similar analyses could be conducted in three space dimensions.

Let us start with the following general result.

Proposition A.1
.

Let
\mathbf{s}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
and
{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
be sets of seed points and weights satisfying the genericity assumptions
G1
,
G2
,
3.10
,
3.11
,
3.12
,
3.13
and
3.14
and
let
\mathbf{q}=\left\{\mathbf{q}_{j}\right\}_{j=1,\ldots,M}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dM}
denote the vertices of the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
.
Then for
\widehat{\mathbf{s}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
and
\widehat{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
small enough, the perturbed diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}+\widehat{\mathbf{s}},{\bm{\psi}}+\widehat{\bm{\psi}})
has the same neighbour relations as
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
.

Moreover, there exists a mapping
\mathbf{m}:{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}_{\mathbf{s}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}_{{\bm{\psi}}}\to W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
which is differentiable in a neighborhood of
(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0})
such that:

\forall i=1,\ldots,N,\quad\mathbf{m}(\widehat{\mathbf{s}},\widehat{\bm{\psi}})\Big(V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})\Big)=V_{i}(\mathbf{s}+\widehat{\mathbf{s}},{\bm{\psi}}+\widehat{\bm{\psi}}),

and

\text{The vertices of the diagram } \mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}+\widehat{\mathbf{s}},{\bm{\psi}}+\widehat{\bm{\psi}})\text{ are exactly the points }\mathbf{m}(\widehat{\mathbf{s}},\widehat{\bm{\psi}})(\mathbf{q}_{j}),\>\>j=1,\ldots,M.

Proof.

At first, we prove that
i\neq j\in\left\{1,\ldots,N\right\}
are the indices of neighbor cells in the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
if and only if
the cells
i
and
j
are neighbors in
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}+\widehat{\mathbf{s}},{\bm{\psi}}+\widehat{\bm{\psi}})
for small enough perturbations
\widehat{\mathbf{s}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
and
\widehat{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
.
For simplicity, we only deal with the case where the edge
\mathbf{e}_{ij}
between the cells
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
and
V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
in
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
lies entirely in the open set
D
, the treatment of the situation where it intersects
\partial D
being similar.

To achieve our purpose, let us first observe that
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
and
V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
are neighbors in the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
if and only if the following property holds true:

\exists\mathbf{x}\in D\text{ s.t. }\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert^{2}-\psi_{i}=\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{j}\lvert^{2}-\psi_{j},&\\
\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert^{2}-\psi_{i}<\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{k}\lvert^{2}-\psi_{k},&\text{for each }k\notin\left\{i,j\right\}.\end{array}\right.

In turn, this is equivalent to the positivity of the optimal value

\max\limits_{\mathbf{x}}\left\{\min\limits_{k\notin\left\{i,j\right\}}\left\{\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{k}\lvert^{2}-\psi_{k}-\Big(\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert^{2}-\psi_{i}\Big)\right\},\>\mathbf{x}\in D,\>\>\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert^{2}-\psi_{i}=\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{j}\lvert^{2}-\psi_{j}\right\}.

We now invoke the continuity of the optimal value of a constrained optimization program with respect to perturbations of its parameters, see e.g. Prop. 4.4 in
[
23
]
, whose assumptions are satisfied in the present case.
Hence, if the cells indexed by
i\neq j
are neighbors in the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
, then they are also neighbors in the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}+\widehat{\mathbf{s}},{\bm{\psi}}+\widehat{\bm{\psi}})
for small enough
\widehat{\mathbf{s}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
,
\widehat{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
.
Note that a similar argument shows that if
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
satisfies
G1
,
G2
,
3.10
,
3.11
,
3.12
,
3.13
and
3.14
, then so does
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}+\widehat{\mathbf{s}},{\bm{\psi}}+\widehat{\bm{\psi}})
.

Conversely, let us now prove that there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that, for any perturbations
\widehat{\mathbf{s}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
,
\widehat{{\bm{\psi}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
with
\lvert\widehat{\mathbf{s}}\lvert+\lvert\widehat{{\bm{\psi}}}\lvert<\varepsilon
, the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}+\widehat{\mathbf{s}},{\bm{\psi}}+\widehat{{\bm{\psi}})})
cannot have neighbor cells that are not already neighbors in
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
.
To achieve this, we proceed by contradiction, assuming that there exist sequences
\widehat{\mathbf{s}}^{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
,
\widehat{{\bm{\psi}}}^{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
converging to
\bm{0}
and index sequences
i^{n}\neq j^{n}\in\left\{1,\ldots,N\right\}
such that the cells with indices
i^{n}
,
j^{n}
are neighbors in
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}+{\widehat{\mathbf{s}}}^{n},{\bm{\psi}}+\widehat{{\bm{\psi}}}^{n})
but not in
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
. Actually, since the number
N
of seed points is finite, we may extract a subsequence (still labeled by
n
) along which these indices
i
,
j
are independent of
n
.
Let then
\mathbf{a}^{n}
and
\mathbf{b}^{n}
denote the vertices of the edge between the cells
i
and
j
in
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}+\widehat{\mathbf{s}}^{n},{\bm{\psi}}+\widehat{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
,
and let
k^{n},l^{n}\notin\left\{i,j\right\}
denote the (distinct) indices such that
\mathbf{a}^{n}
(resp.
\mathbf{b}^{n}
) is at the intersection between the cells
i
,
j
and
k^{n}
(resp.
l^{n}
) in
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}+\widehat{\mathbf{s}}^{n},{\bm{\psi}}+\widehat{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
.
Up to extraction of another subsequence, we may assume that these indices do not depend on
n
, and we denote them by
k\neq l
.
We may also assume that
\mathbf{a}^{n}
and
\mathbf{b}^{n}
converge to limiting positions
\mathbf{a}^{*}
and
\mathbf{b}^{*}
, respectively. Both points belong to the boundaries
\partial V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
and
\partial V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
. Since
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
and
V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
are not neighbors in
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
, one must then have
\mathbf{a}^{*}=\mathbf{b}^{*}
and this single vertex is at the intersection between the four cells
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
,
V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
,
V_{k}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
and
V_{l}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
. This contradicts Assumption
G2
, and the statement that
i
and
j
are not neighbors in
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
is absurd.

At this point, we have proved that for
\widehat{\mathbf{s}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
,
\widehat{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
small enough, the perturbed diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}+\widehat{\mathbf{s}},{\bm{\psi}}+\widehat{\bm{\psi}})
has the same neighbor relations as
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
.
Now, each cell
V_{i}(\mathbf{s}+\widehat{\mathbf{s}},{\bm{\psi}}+\widehat{\bm{\psi}})
is a closed, convex subset of
\overline{D}
whose non degenerate edges are completely characterized by their endpoints.
The calculations below show that all the vertices of this diagram behave as differentiable functions in a neighborhood of
(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
.
We may then define
\mathbf{m}(\widehat{\mathbf{s}},\widehat{\bm{\psi}})
as an affine function in restriction to every edge of the cells
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
,
and then extend the latter into
D
as a whole by barycentric coordinates extension. This induces a
W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
mapping
\mathbf{m}(\widehat{\mathbf{s}},\widehat{\bm{\psi}})
which is a differentiable of the variables
(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
in the neighborhood of
(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{0})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
.
∎

We provide the missing ingredients of the above proof by showing that the vertices of the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
are smooth functions of the seed points
\mathbf{s}
and weights
{\bm{\psi}}
when they are slight perturbations of reference values satisfying
G1
,
G2
,
3.10
,
3.11
,
3.12
,
3.13
and
3.14
. Meanwhile, we provide explicit characterizations of their derivatives, which are useful in particular in the numerical implementation of the methods presented in
Section

6
.
We proceed in the case
d=2
for simplicity, but a similar analysis could be conducted when
d=3
, up to an increased level of tediousness. We distinguish between several types of vertices, which are illustrated on
Fig.

23
.

[formula]

[formula]

[formula]

[formula]

[formula]

Figure 23
.
Different categories of vertices in a diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
: (a) Case of a vertex
\mathbf{q}
at the intersection between
3
cells; (b) Case of a vertex between
2
cells and the void phase; (c) Case of a vertex resulting from the discretization of an exterior circle; (d) Case of a vertex at the intersection between
2
cells and the boundary
\partial D
of the computational domain
D
; (e) Case of a vertex at the intersection between one cell, the void phase, and
\partial D
.

Case 1: Vertices at the intersection of three cells.

Let
\mathbf{q}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be the vertex at the intersection between three given cells
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
,
V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
and
V_{k}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
, for some distinct indices
i,j,k\in\left\{1,\ldots,N\right\}
.
This vertex is characterized by the following relations:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|^{2}-\psi_{i}&=&|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{j}|^{2}-\psi_{j}\\[3.99994pt]
|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|^{2}-\psi_{i}&=&|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{k}|^{2}-\psi_{k},\end{array}\right.

which rewrites:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}\langle\mathbf{q},\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\rangle&=&\frac{1}{2}(|\mathbf{s}_{j}|^{2}-|\mathbf{s}_{i}|^{2}-(\psi_{j}-\psi_{i}))\\[3.99994pt]
\langle\mathbf{q},\mathbf{s}_{k}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\rangle&=&\frac{1}{2}(|\mathbf{s}_{k}|^{2}-|\mathbf{s}_{i}|^{2}-(\psi_{k}-\psi_{i})).\end{array}\right.

Invoking Assumptions
G2
and
3.10
, this system uniquely determines
\mathbf{q}
as a smooth function of
\mathbf{s}_{i}
,
\mathbf{s}_{j}
,
\mathbf{s}_{k}
and
\psi_{i}
,
\psi_{j}
,
\psi_{k}
,
a dependence which is not made explicit for notational simplicity.
Taking derivatives in the previous system then leads to the following
2\times 2
linear systems for the sensitivities
\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}_{i}}\mathbf{q}\right]\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2\times 2}
and
\frac{\partial\mathbf{q}}{\partial\psi_{i}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
:

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{s}_{k}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\end{array}\right)\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}_{i}}\mathbf{q}\right]=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\end{array}\right),\text{ and }\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{s}_{k}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\end{array}\right)\frac{\partial\mathbf{q}}{\partial\psi_{i}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{1}{2}\\[1.99997pt]
\frac{1}{2}\end{array}\right),

which are invertible on account of Assumption
G2
. Similar expressions hold true for the derivatives
\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}_{j}}\mathbf{q}\right]
,
\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}_{k}}\mathbf{q}\right]
and
\frac{\partial\mathbf{q}}{\partial\psi_{j}}
,
\frac{\partial\mathbf{q}}{\partial\psi_{k}}
.

Case 2: Vertices at the intersection of two cells and void.

Let now
\mathbf{q}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be one vertex at the intersection between the cells
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
,
V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
(
i\neq j
) and the void phase
V_{0}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
.
Then,
\mathbf{q}
satisfies the following system of equations:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}\langle\mathbf{q},\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\rangle&=&\frac{1}{2}(|\mathbf{s}_{j}|^{2}-|\mathbf{s}_{i}|^{2}-(\psi_{j}-\psi_{i}))\\
|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|^{2}&=&\psi_{i}.\end{array}\right.

Note that there may be several such vertices, and so the above system may accordingly have multiple solutions.
Assumption
3.10
and the implicit function theorem together imply that
\mathbf{q}
behaves as a smooth function of
\mathbf{s}_{i}
,
\mathbf{s}_{j}
,
\psi_{i}
and
\psi_{j}
– actually, any solution to this system induces a smooth branch of solutions for small perturbations of these parameters.
Taking derivatives, it follows:

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\end{array}\right)\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}_{i}}\mathbf{q}\right]=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\end{array}\right),\text{ and }\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\end{array}\right)\frac{\partial\mathbf{q}}{\partial\psi_{i}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{1}{2}\\[1.99997pt]
\frac{1}{2}\end{array}\right).

These systems are invertible on account of Assumption
3.10
. Again, similar expressions hold for the derivatives
\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}_{j}}\mathbf{q}\right]
and
\frac{\partial\mathbf{q}}{\partial\psi_{j}}
.

Case 3: Vertices corresponding to the discretization of a circular arc.

Consider a circular arc pertaining to the boundary of the cell
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
, and let
\mathbf{q}_{r}
,
r=0,\ldots,n_{\text{\rm arc}}
be the vertices corresponding to its discretization, enumerated counterclockwise, see
Section

4
.
Let
\alpha\in(0,2\pi)
be the angle from
\mathbf{s}_{i}\mathbf{q}_{0}
to
\mathbf{s}_{i}\mathbf{q}_{n_{\text{\rm arc}}}
, which is a smooth function of
\mathbf{s}_{i}
,
\mathbf{q}_{0}
and
\mathbf{q}_{n_{\text{\rm arc}}}
,
and, in turn, as revealed by the previous item, a smooth function of the seed points
\mathbf{s}
and weights
{\bm{\psi}}
.
More precisely, it holds:

\cos\alpha=\left\langle\frac{\mathbf{s}_{i}\mathbf{q}_{0}}{\lvert\mathbf{s}_{i}\mathbf{q}_{0}\lvert},\frac{\mathbf{s}_{i}\mathbf{q}_{n_{\text{\rm arc}}}}{\lvert\mathbf{s}_{i}\mathbf{q}_{n_{\text{\rm arc}}}\lvert}\right\rangle,\>\>\sin\alpha=\det\left(\frac{\mathbf{s}_{i}\mathbf{q}_{0}}{\lvert\mathbf{s}_{i}\mathbf{q}_{0}\lvert},\frac{\mathbf{s}_{i}\mathbf{q}_{n_{\text{\rm arc}}}}{\lvert\mathbf{s}_{i}\mathbf{q}_{n_{\text{\rm arc}}}\lvert}\right),

whence we infer
\alpha\in(0,2\pi)
via the formula

\alpha=2\text{acot}\left(\frac{1+\cos\alpha}{\sin\alpha}\right)=\pi-2\text{atan}\left(\frac{1+\cos\alpha}{\sin\alpha}\right).

Then, let us introduce the vector
\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{s}_{i}\mathbf{q}_{0}^{\perp}:=(-(s_{i,2}-q_{0,2}),s_{i,1}-q_{0,1})
,
where
\mathbf{u}^{\perp}:=(-u_{2},u_{1})
denotes the
90^{\circ}
counterclockwise rotate of a vector
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},u_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Each vertex
\mathbf{q}_{r}
(
r=1,\ldots,n_{\text{\rm arc}}-1
) is determined by the following relation:

\mathbf{s}_{i}\mathbf{q}_{r}=\cos(t_{r}\alpha)\mathbf{s}_{i}\mathbf{q}_{0}+\sin(t_{r}\alpha)\mathbf{b},\text{ where }t_{r}=\frac{r}{n_{\text{\rm arc}}}.

The differentiability of
\mathbf{q}_{r}
and the closed form expressions of its derivatives with respect to
\mathbf{s}
and
{\bm{\psi}}
are straightforward consequences of this formula.

Since the cells of the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
are restricted to the bounded computational domain
D\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, two additional situations should be considered, which are depicted on
Fig.

23
(e) (f).
To address them, it is convenient to introduce a level set function
\phi:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
for
D
, that is

\forall\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2},\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\phi(\mathbf{x})<0&\text{if }\mathbf{x}\in D,\\
\phi(\mathbf{x})=0&\text{if }\mathbf{x}\in\partial D,\\
\phi(\mathbf{x})>0&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Then, the unit normal vector
\mathbf{n}(\mathbf{x})
to
\partial D
pointing outward
D
is given by
\mathbf{n}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{\nabla\phi(\mathbf{x})}{\lvert\nabla\phi(\mathbf{x})|}
for
\text{\rm d}s
a.e.
\mathbf{x}\in\partial D
,
see e.g.
[
95
,
110
]
about general features of such a level set representation of domains.

Case 2b: Vertices corresponding to the intersection between two cells and
\partial D
.

Let
\mathbf{q}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be one vertex at the intersection between the cells
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
,
V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
and
\partial D
.
Then the following system of equations holds:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}\lvert\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert^{2}-\psi_{i}&=&\lvert\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{j}\lvert^{2}-\psi_{j}\\
\phi(\mathbf{q})&=&0.\end{array}\right.

As in the previous items, the combination of Assumptions
3.12
and
3.13
with the implicit function theorem shows that this
\mathbf{q}
behaves as a smooth function of the parameters
\mathbf{s}
and
{\bm{\psi}}
when the latter are slightly perturbed from reference values satisfying
G1
,
G2
,
3.10
,
3.11
,
3.12
,
3.13
and
3.14
.
In the meantime, taking derivatives in the above equations yields immediately the following systems:

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{n}(\mathbf{q})\end{array}\right)\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}_{i}}\mathbf{q}\right]=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{0}\end{array}\right),\text{ and }\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{n}(\mathbf{q})\end{array}\right)\frac{\partial\mathbf{q}}{\partial\psi_{i}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{1}{2}\\
0\end{array}\right),

which are invertible on account of
3.12
and
3.13
. Similar relations allow to characterize the derivatives
\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}_{j}}\mathbf{q}\right]
and
\frac{\partial\mathbf{q}}{\partial\psi_{j}}
.

Case 3b: Vertices at the intersection between one exterior circle and
\partial D
.

Let
\mathbf{q}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be one vertex lying at the intersection between the cell
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
(
i=1,\ldots,N
), the void phase
V_{0}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
and
\partial D
.
Then,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccl}|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|^{2}-\psi_{i}&=&0\\
\phi(\mathbf{q})&=&0,\\
\end{array}\right.

Using
G1
,
G2
,
3.10
,
3.11
,
3.12
,
3.13
and
3.14
and the implicit function theorem, we see that this system characterizes a smooth function of
\mathbf{s}
and
{\bm{\psi}}
. In the meantime,
taking derivatives into these equations, we obtain the following invertible systems:

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{n}(\mathbf{q})\end{array}\right)\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{s}_{i}}\mathbf{q}\right]=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{0}\end{array}\right),\text{ and }\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\\
\hline\cr\mathbf{n}(\mathbf{q})\end{array}\right)\frac{\partial\mathbf{q}_{r}}{\partial\psi_{i}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{1}{2}\\
0\end{array}\right).

Appendix B
Second order derivatives of the Kantorovic functional with respect to seeds and weights

Let
\mathbf{s}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
,
{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
be generic seed points and weights, in the sense that
G1
,
G2
,
3.10
,
3.11
,
3.12
,
3.13
and
3.14
hold true. Let also
{\bm{\nu}}=\left\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{N}\right\}
be a vector of prescribed measures. Restricting to the case of two space dimensions
d=2
for simplicity, this section details the calculation of the partial
derivatives of the derivative
\mathbf{F}:{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}_{\mathbf{s}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}_{{\bm{\nu}}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}_{{\bm{\psi}}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
of the Kantorovic functional introduced in
Theorem

3.1
:

\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})=\Big(\nu_{i}-|V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})|\Big)_{i=1,\ldots,N}.

Let us recall that this result is useful for a variety of purposes in our framework:
on the one hand, it is the key ingredient of the Newton-Raphson algorithm implemented for the resolution of
3.16
, see
Section

4.1
.
On the other hand, it is needed in the procedure of
Section

6
for expressing the derivative of a quantity with respect to the vertices
\mathbf{q}
of the diagram in terms of the defining seed points
\mathbf{s}
and weights
{\bm{\psi}}
.
Again, the results of this appendix are not completely new, see
e.g.
[
42
,
84
,
93
]
where fairly similar calculations are conducted in a formal fashion.
The calculation of the derivatives of
\mathbf{F}
with respect to the weights
{\bm{\psi}}
is also achieved in
[
84
]
, and in
[
45
]
in a more general context. Here, we present an elementary mixture of arguments used in the aforementioned references.

Let us introduce a few additional notations, which are illustrated on
Fig.

24
:

•

For any index
i=1,\ldots,N
and any neighbor
j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{i}
of
i
,
we recall that
\mathbf{e}_{ij}
is the line segment
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})\cap V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
, and we denote by
\mathbf{m}_{ij}
the midpoint of
\mathbf{e}_{ij}
.

•

For
i=1,\ldots,N
, we denote by
{\mathcal{C}}_{i,r}
,
r=1,\ldots,n_{c,i}
the circular arcs composing the boundary of the cell
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
; the value
n_{c,i}=0
indicates that the cell is not on the boundary of the associated shape
\Omega
. We also let
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}:=\bigcup_{r=1}^{n_{c,i}}{\mathcal{C}}_{i,r}
.

•

For
i=1,\ldots,N
and
r=1,\ldots,n_{c,i}
, the endpoints of the circular arc
{\mathcal{C}}_{i,r}
are denoted by
\mathbf{q}_{i,r}^{0}
,
\mathbf{q}_{i,r}^{1}
when the latter is oriented counterclockwise, and
\theta_{i,r}\in(0,2\pi)
is its angular aperture.

•

For
i=1,\ldots,N
, we denote by
\mathbf{n}_{i}
the unit normal vector field to
\partial V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
, pointing outward
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
. This latter is given for
\text{\rm d}s
-almost every point
\mathbf{x}\in\partial V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
by the following expressions:

\mathbf{n}(\mathbf{x})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}}{\lvert\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert}&\text{if }\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{e}_{ij}\text{ for some }j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{i},\\
\frac{\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}}{\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert}&\text{if }\mathbf{x}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{i}.\end{array}\right.

The result of interest in this appendix is the following.

Proposition B.1
.

Let
\mathbf{s}=\left\{\mathbf{s}_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
and
{\bm{\psi}}=\left\{\psi_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
be sets of seed points and weights satisfying the genericity Assumptions
G1
,
G2
,
3.10
,
3.11
,
3.12
,
3.13
and
3.14
.
Then, for all
i=1,\ldots,N
, the function
\mathbf{F}
is differentiable at
(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})
.
It holds, for
i=1,\ldots,N
:

\nabla_{\mathbf{s}_{i}}F_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})=-\sum\limits_{j\in N_{i}}\frac{|\mathbf{e}_{ij}|}{|\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|}(\mathbf{m}_{ij}-\mathbf{s}_{i})-\sum\limits_{r=1}^{n_{c,i}}(\mathbf{q}_{i,r}^{0}-\mathbf{q}_{i,r}^{1})^{\perp},\>\>\text{ and for }j\neq i\>\>\> \nabla_{\mathbf{s}_{j}}F_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})=\frac{|\mathbf{e}_{ij}|}{|\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|}(\mathbf{m}_{ij}-\mathbf{s}_{i}),

where
\mathbf{u}^{\perp}:=(-u_{2},u_{1})
is the 90
∘
counterclockwise rotate of a vector
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},u_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Besides,

\frac{\partial F_{i}}{\partial\psi_{i}}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum\limits_{j\in N_{i}}\frac{\lvert\mathbf{e}_{ij}\lvert}{|\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|}\right)-\sum\limits_{r=1}^{n_{c,i}}\theta_{i,r},\>\>\text{ and for }j\neq i\>\>\> \frac{\partial F_{i}}{\partial\psi_{j}}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\lvert\mathbf{e}_{ij}\lvert}{|\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|}.

[formula]

[formula]

Figure 24
.
Deformation of (a) An interior cell; (b) A cell containing at least one circular arc in the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
, induced by a small perturbation of the corresponding seed point, as considered in
Appendix

B
.

Proof.

Judging from the definition
3.19
of the function
\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},\cdot)
, the proof boils down to the calculation of the derivatives of the measures

A_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}):=\lvert V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})\lvert,\quad i=1,\ldots,N

with respect to the seed points
\mathbf{s}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
and weights
{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
of the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
.
The main idea builds on the conclusion of
Proposition

A.1
, whereby their exists
\varepsilon>0
such that for any perturbation
(\widehat{\mathbf{s}},\widehat{{\bm{\psi}}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}_{\mathbf{s}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N}_{{\bm{\psi}}}
with
\lvert\widehat{\mathbf{s}}\lvert+\lvert\widehat{{\bm{\psi}}}\lvert<\varepsilon
, the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s}+\widehat{\mathbf{s}},{\bm{\psi}}+\widehat{{\bm{\psi}}})
is obtained from the reference diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
by application of a “smooth” deformation

\text{\rm Id}+\mathbf{m}(\widehat{\mathbf{s}},\widehat{{\bm{\psi}}}),\text{ where }\mathbf{m}(\widehat{\mathbf{s}},\widehat{{\bm{\psi}}})\in W^{1,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2},{\mathbb{R}}^{2}).

The derivative of
(\widehat{\mathbf{s}},\widehat{{\bm{\psi}}})\mapsto A_{i}(\mathbf{s}+\widehat{\mathbf{s}},{\bm{\psi}}+\widehat{{\bm{\psi}}})
thus results from the combination of the chain rule with the formula
2.8
for the derivative of the volume of a domain with respect to arbitrary perturbations of its boundary.

We first apply this strategy to calculate the derivative of
A_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
with respect to
\mathbf{s}_{i}
, for a given index
i=1,\ldots,N
. For any vector
\mathbf{h}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, let
\mathbf{s}(\mathbf{h}):=(\mathbf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{i-1},\mathbf{s}_{i}+\mathbf{h},\mathbf{s}_{i+1},\ldots,\mathbf{s}_{N})
be the collection of seed points resulting from a translation
\mathbf{h}
of the
i^{\text{th}}
element of
\mathbf{s}
.
As we have recalled, when
\lvert\mathbf{h}\lvert<\varepsilon
, the cell
V_{i}(\mathbf{s}(\mathbf{h}),{\bm{\psi}})
is of the form

(B.1)

V_{i}(\mathbf{s}(\mathbf{h}),{\bm{\psi}})=(\text{\rm Id}+\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{h}))\Big(V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})\Big),

for a certain differentiable mapping
B(\bm{0},\varepsilon)\ni\mathbf{h}\mapsto\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{h})\in W^{1,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2},{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
.
The combination of the chain rule with formula
2.8
yields:

(B.2)

\begin{array}[]{>{\displaystyle}cc>{\displaystyle}l}\frac{\partial A_{i}}{\partial\mathbf{s}_{i}}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})(\mathbf{h})&=&\int_{\partial V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})}\left\langle\frac{\partial\mathbf{m}}{\partial\mathbf{h}}(\bm{0})(\mathbf{h})(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{n}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rangle\>\text{\rm d}s(\mathbf{x})\\[10.00002pt]
&=&\sum\limits_{j\in N_{i}}\int_{\mathbf{e}_{ij}}\left\langle\frac{\partial\mathbf{m}}{\partial\mathbf{h}}(\bm{0})(\mathbf{h})(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{n}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rangle\>\text{\rm d}s(\mathbf{x})+\sum\limits_{r=1}^{n_{c,i}}\int_{{\mathcal{C}}_{i,r}}\left\langle\frac{\partial\mathbf{m}}{\partial\mathbf{h}}(\bm{0})(\mathbf{h})(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{n}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rangle\>\text{\rm d}s(\mathbf{x}),\\[10.00002pt]
\end{array}

and we now proceed to glean information about the derivative
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\ni\mathbf{h}\mapsto\frac{\partial\mathbf{m}}{\partial\mathbf{h}}(\bm{0})(\mathbf{h})\in W^{1,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2},{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
.

Let
j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{i}
, and
\mathbf{x}
be an arbitrary point on the edge
\mathbf{e}_{ij}
. For any perturbation
\mathbf{h}\in B(\bm{0},\varepsilon)
, the point
\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{h})(\mathbf{x})
belongs to the intersection of the cells
V_{i}(\mathbf{s}(\mathbf{h}),{\bm{\psi}})
and
V_{j}(\mathbf{s}(\mathbf{h}),{\bm{\psi}})
, which implies that:

\lvert\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{h})(\mathbf{x})-\mathbf{s}_{i}-\mathbf{h}\lvert^{2}-\psi_{i}=\lvert\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{h})(\mathbf{x})-\mathbf{s}_{j}\lvert^{2}-\psi_{j}.

A simple calculation then yields:

2\langle\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{h})(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\rangle=2\langle\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i},\mathbf{h}\rangle+\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{j}\lvert^{2}-\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert^{2}-(\psi_{j}-\psi_{i})+\text{\rm o}(\mathbf{h}),

and so:

\left\langle\frac{\partial\mathbf{m}}{\partial\mathbf{h}}(\mathbf{0})(\mathbf{h})(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\right\rangle=\langle\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i},\mathbf{h}\rangle.

As a result, we have proved the expression

(B.3)

\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{e}_{ij},\quad\left\langle\frac{\partial\mathbf{m}}{\partial\mathbf{h}}(\mathbf{0})(\mathbf{h})(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{n}_{i}\right\rangle=\left\langle\frac{\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}}{|\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert},\mathbf{h}\right\rangle.

Let now
\mathbf{x}
be a point on one of the circular arcs
{\mathcal{C}}_{i,r}
,
r=1,\ldots,n_{c,i}
. Again, the definition of
{\mathcal{C}}_{i,r}
readily implies that, for any perturbation vector
\lvert\mathbf{h}\lvert<\varepsilon
, we have:

\lvert\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{h})(\mathbf{x})-\mathbf{s}_{i}-\mathbf{h}\lvert^{2}=\psi_{i}.

Hence, we obtain

\left\langle\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{h})(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i},\mathbf{h}\right\rangle-\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\psi_{i}+\text{\rm o}(\mathbf{h}),

and so:

(B.4)

\forall\mathbf{x}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{i,r},\quad\left\langle\frac{\partial\mathbf{m}}{\partial\mathbf{h}}(\mathbf{0})(\mathbf{h})(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{n}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rangle=\left\langle\frac{\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}}{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|},\mathbf{h}\right\rangle.

Eventually, combining
B.2
with
B.3
and
B.4
, we obtain:

\begin{array}[]{>{\displaystyle}cc>{\displaystyle}l}\frac{\partial A_{i}}{\partial\mathbf{s}_{i}}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})(\mathbf{h})&=&\left\langle\sum\limits_{j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{i}}\int_{\mathbf{e}_{ij}}\frac{\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}}{|\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|}\>\text{\rm d}s(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{h}\right\rangle+\left\langle\sum\limits_{r=1}^{n_{c,i}}\int_{{\mathcal{C}}_{i,r}}\frac{\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}}{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|}\>\text{\rm d}s(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{h}\right\rangle\\[10.00002pt]
&=&\left\langle\sum\limits_{j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{i}}\frac{\lvert\mathbf{e}_{ij}\lvert}{|\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|}(\mathbf{m}_{ij}-\mathbf{s}_{i}),\mathbf{h}\right\rangle+\left\langle\sum\limits_{r=1}^{n_{c,i}}(\mathbf{q}_{i,r}^{0}-\mathbf{s}_{i})^{\perp}-(\mathbf{q}_{i,r}^{1}-\mathbf{s}_{i})^{\perp},\mathbf{h}\right\rangle,\end{array}

where the second line follows from an elementary calculation. This yields the desired formula.

Let us now apply the same strategy to calculate the derivative of
A_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
with respect to the weight
\psi_{i}
. A perturbation of
\psi_{i}
of the form
\psi_{i}+h
for
\lvert h\lvert<\varepsilon
incurs a deformation of the
i^{\text{th}}
cell of the diagram of the form:

V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}+h\mathbf{e}_{i})=(\text{\rm Id}+\mathbf{m}(h))\Big(V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})\Big),

for some differentiable mapping
B(0,\varepsilon)\ni h\mapsto\mathbf{m}(h)\in W^{1,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2},{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
. Again, the combination of
2.8
with the chain rule yields:

(B.5)

\begin{array}[]{>{\displaystyle}cc>{\displaystyle}l}\frac{\partial A_{i}}{\partial\psi_{i}}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})(h)&=&\int_{\partial V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})}\left\langle\frac{\partial\mathbf{m}}{\partial h}(0)(h)(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{n}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rangle\>\text{\rm d}s(\mathbf{x})\\[10.00002pt]
&=&\sum\limits_{j\in N_{i}}\int_{\mathbf{e}_{ij}}\left\langle\frac{\partial\mathbf{m}}{\partial h}(0)(h)(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{n}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rangle\>\text{\rm d}s(\mathbf{x})+\sum\limits_{r=1}^{n_{c,i}}\int_{{\mathcal{C}}_{i,r}}\left\langle\frac{\partial\mathbf{m}}{\partial h}(0)(h)(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{n}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rangle\>\text{\rm d}s(\mathbf{x}),\\[10.00002pt]
\end{array}

and we now aim to characterize the derivative
{\mathbb{R}}\ni h\mapsto\frac{\partial\mathbf{m}}{\partial h}(0)(h)\in W^{1,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2},{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
.

To this end, let us first consider an index
j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{i}
, and let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{e}_{ij}
be given. For any
h\in B(0,\varepsilon)
, it holds:

|\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{m}(h)(\mathbf{x})-\mathbf{s}_{i}|^{2}-\psi_{i}-h=|\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{m}(h)(\mathbf{x})-\mathbf{s}_{j}|^{2}-\psi_{j},

and so

\langle\mathbf{m}(h)(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\rangle=\frac{h}{2}+|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{j}|^{2}-|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|^{2}-(\psi_{j}-\psi_{i})+\text{\rm o}(h).

We then obtain the following formula involving the derivative of the mapping
m
:

\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{e}_{ij},\quad\left\langle\frac{\partial\mathbf{m}}{\partial h}(0)(h)(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\right\rangle=\frac{h}{2},

which rewrites:

(B.6)

\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{e}_{ij},\quad\left\langle\frac{\partial\mathbf{m}}{\partial h}(0)(h)(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{n}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rangle=\frac{h}{2}\frac{1}{\lvert\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert}.

Let us now consider a point
\mathbf{x}
located on one of the circular arcs
{\mathcal{C}}_{i,r}
,
r=1,\ldots,n_{c,i}
. It holds, for all
h\in B(0,\varepsilon)
:

|\mathbf{x}+m(h)(\mathbf{x})-\mathbf{s}_{i}|^{2}=\psi_{i}+h,

and so:

(B.7)

\left\langle\frac{\partial\mathbf{m}}{\partial h}(0)(h),\mathbf{n}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rangle=\frac{1}{2|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|}=\frac{1}{2}\psi_{i}^{-1/2}.

Eventually, combining
B.5
with
B.6
and
B.7
, we arrive at:

\begin{array}[]{>{\displaystyle}cc>{\displaystyle}l}\frac{\partial A_{i}}{\partial\psi_{i}}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})(h)&=&\left\langle\sum\limits_{j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{i}}\int_{\mathbf{e}_{ij}}\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{\lvert\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert}\>\text{\rm d}s(\mathbf{x}),h\right\rangle+\left\langle\sum\limits_{r=1}^{n_{c,i}}\int_{{\mathcal{C}}_{i,r}}\frac{1}{2}\psi_{i}^{-1/2}\>\text{\rm d}s(\mathbf{x}),h\right\rangle\\[10.00002pt]
&=&\left\langle\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{i}}\frac{\lvert\mathbf{e}_{ij}\lvert}{|\mathbf{s}_{j}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|},h\right\rangle+\left\langle\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{n_{c,i}}\theta_{i,r},h\right\rangle,\end{array}

which yields the desired formula.

The expressions of the partial derivatives of the mapping
(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})\mapsto A_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
with respect to the position
\mathbf{s}_{j}
and weight
\psi_{j}
of a neighboring index
j\in{\mathcal{N}}_{i}
are obtained in a completely similar fashion, and we omit the details for brevity. The proof of
Proposition

B.1
is therefore complete.
∎

Remark B.1
.

The differentiability of the mapping
{\bm{\psi}}\mapsto K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})
– which essentially involves the areas
\lvert V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})\lvert
of the cells of the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
– holds true under weaker assumptions than the collection
G1
,
G2
,
3.10
,
3.11
,
3.12
,
3.13
and
3.14
, which implies the differentiability of all the individual vertices of the diagram, see for instance
[
45
,
84
]
. To keep the presentation elementary and self-contained, we limit ourselves with the presented results, which are sufficient for our purpose.

Corollary B.1
.

Let
\mathbf{s}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{dN}
and
{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
be collections of seed points and weights
satisfying
G1
,
G2
,
3.10
,
3.11
,
3.12
,
3.13
and
3.14
.
Then,
\left[\nabla_{{\bm{\psi}}}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})\right]
is a negative definite
N\times N
matrix, which is therefore invertible.

Proof.

The above assumptions imply in particular that the diagram
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\bm{\psi})
contains no empty cell, so that the weights
\psi_{i}
are positive for
i=1,\ldots,N
(see Step 2 in the next
Appendix

C
where the same argument is used).
Then, the matrix
\left[\nabla_{{\bm{\psi}}}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})\right]
is strictly diagonal dominant, and by a classical argument, it is invertible.
Since its diagonal entries are negative, the claim follows.
∎

Remark B.2
.

In the case where the void phase
V_{0}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
is empty,
\left[\nabla_{{\bm{\psi}}}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})\right]
has a one-dimensional kernel,
which reflects the fact that the collection of weights
{\bm{\psi}}
realizing a given measure constraint for each cell is unique up to addition of a common real number to all the
\psi_{i}
.

Appendix C
Proof of
Theorem

3.1

The proof is decomposed into four steps.

Step 1: We prove that the function
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\ni{\bm{\psi}}\mapsto K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})\in{\mathbb{R}}
defined by
3.18
is concave, differentiable, and we calculate its derivative.

For
\mathbf{x}\in X
, we denote for convenience
\mathbf{s}_{0}\equiv\mathbf{s}_{0}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{x}
.
For a given weight vector
{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, let us define the measurable mappings
I_{{\bm{\psi}}}:X\to\left\{0,\ldots,N\right\}
and
T_{{\bm{\psi}}}:X\to X
by:

I_{{\bm{\psi}}}(\mathbf{x})=\argmin\limits_{i=0,\ldots,N}\Big(|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|^{2}-\psi_{i}\Big),\text{ and }T_{{\bm{\psi}}}(x)=\mathbf{s}_{I_{{\bm{\psi}}}(\mathbf{x})},

where we recall the convention
\psi_{0}=0
.
Note that the points
\mathbf{x}\in X
where the above minimum is not uniquely attained form a set of Lebesgue measure
0
, so that
I_{{\bm{\psi}}}
and
T_{{\bm{\psi}}}
are well-defined a.e. on
X
.
This definition immediately implies that, for any measurable mapping
I:X\to\left\{0,\ldots,N\right\}
, it holds:

|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{I_{{\bm{\psi}}}(\mathbf{x})}|^{2}-\psi_{I_{{\bm{\psi}}}(\mathbf{x})}\leq|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{I(\mathbf{x})}|^{2}-\psi_{I(\mathbf{x})}\text{ for a.e. }\mathbf{x}\in X.

It follows that, for any weight vectors
{\bm{\psi}},{\bm{\varphi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
:

(C.1)

\begin{array}[]{>{\displaystyle}cc>{\displaystyle}l}K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\varphi}})&=&\int_{D}\Big(|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{I_{\bm{\varphi}}(\mathbf{x})}(\mathbf{x})|-\varphi_{I_{\bm{\varphi}}(\mathbf{x})}\Big)\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\nu_{i}\varphi_{i}\\
&\leq&\int_{D}\Big(|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{I_{\bm{\psi}}(\mathbf{x})}(\mathbf{x})|-\varphi_{I_{{\bm{\psi}}(\mathbf{x})}}\Big)\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\nu_{i}\varphi_{i}\\
&=&\sum\limits_{i=0}^{N}\int_{V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})}\left(|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}|^{2}-\varphi_{i}\right)\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\nu_{i}\varphi_{i}\\[15.00002pt]
&=&K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})+\langle\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}),{\bm{\varphi}}-{\bm{\psi}}\rangle,\end{array}

where we recall that the components of
\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
are defined by:

(C.2)

F_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}):=\nu_{i}-|V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})|,\quad i=1,\ldots,N.

Hence, the superdifferential
\partial_{{\bm{\psi}}}K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})
of the mapping
{\bm{\varphi}}\mapsto K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\varphi}})
at an arbitrary weight vector
{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
contains
\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
,
and it is in particular non empty.
On the other hand, the inequality
C.1
expresses
K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},\cdot)
as the minimum of a collection of affine functions:

K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\varphi}})=\min\limits_{{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}}\Big(K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})+\langle\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}),{\bm{\varphi}}-{\bm{\psi}}\rangle\Big),\quad{\bm{\varphi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}.

This discussion shows that the mapping
{\bm{\psi}}\mapsto K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})
is concave, and thus differentiable at a.e.
{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
; besides, its gradient at any point
{\bm{\psi}}
where it is differentiable equals
\nabla_{\bm{\psi}}K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})=\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})
, see
[
105
]
, Th. 25.4.

The
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
character of
{\bm{\psi}}\mapsto K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})
follows from the same argument as in the proof of Th. 40 in
[
84
]
.
At first, a simple application of the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem shows that the mapping
{\bm{\psi}}\to\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})
is continuous on
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
.
On the other hand, according to Th. 25.6 in
[
105
]
the superdifferential
\partial_{\bm{\psi}}K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})
at any vector
{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
reads:

\partial_{\bm{\psi}}K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})=\left\{\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{g}^{n},\>\>\mathbf{g}^{n}=\nabla_{\bm{\psi}}K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n}),\>\>{\bm{\psi}}^{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}{\bm{\psi}}\text{ and }K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},\cdot)\text{ is differentiable at }{\bm{\psi}}^{n}\right\}.

Now, the continuity of
\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},\cdot)
implies that, for any sequence
{\bm{\psi}}^{n}\to{\bm{\psi}}
such that
K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},\cdot)
is differentiable at
{\bm{\psi}}^{n}
, the unique element
\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})=\nabla_{\bm{\psi}}K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
in
\partial_{{\bm{\psi}}}K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{n})
converges to
\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})
as
n\to\infty
.
The superdifferential
\partial_{\bm{\psi}}K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})
is thus reduced to a single point, and so
K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},\cdot)
is differentiable on
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, with continuous derivative
\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},\cdot)
.
This completes the first step in the proof of
Theorem

3.1
; in passing, we have proved that the maximizers
{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
in
3.17
are characterized by the equation

\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})=\bm{0}.

Step 2: We prove the existence of a weight vector
{\bm{\psi}}^{*}
satisfying
3.16
.

To this end, we adapt the argument of the proof of Corollary 39 in
[
84
]
.
This starts with a few remarks:

•

For any
{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
and
i=1,\ldots,N
, the mapping
{\mathbb{R}}\ni t\mapsto\lvert V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}+t\mathbf{e}_{i})\lvert
is non decreasing.

•

For any
{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, and
i,j=1,\ldots,N
,
i\neq j
, the mapping
{\mathbb{R}}\ni t\mapsto\lvert V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}+t\mathbf{e}_{i})\lvert
is non increasing.

•

If the cell
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
is non empty, then the weight
\psi_{i}
must satisfy

\psi_{i}\geq 0
, since any point
\mathbf{x}\in V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
satisfies in particular
\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert^{2}-\psi_{i}\leq 0
.

•

In the same spirit, if
V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
has positive Lebesgue measure, one has
\psi_{i}>0
.

•

If the cell
V_{0}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})
in
3.8
is non empty, it holds:

\forall i=1,\ldots,N,\quad\psi_{i}\leq\text{\rm diam}(D)^{2}.

Now, let
{\bm{\nu}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
be a vector satisfying
3.15
, and let
{\mathcal{K}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
be the set defined by

{\mathcal{K}}=\Big\{{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\text{ s.t. }|V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})|\leq\nu_{i}\text{ for all }i=1,\ldots,N\Big\}.

According to the previous observations, for all
{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathcal{K}}
, it holds
\lvert V_{0}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}})\lvert\>\geq\nu_{0}>0
, and so

{\mathcal{K}}\subset[0,\text{\rm diam}(D)^{2}]^{N}
. Moreover, since the mapping
\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},\cdot)
is continuous,
{\mathcal{K}}
is closed, and it is therefore compact.
Let us then consider one solution
{\bm{\psi}}^{*}
to the following maximization problem:

(C.3)

\max\limits_{{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathcal{K}}}\>W({\bm{\psi}}),\text{ where }W({\bm{\psi}})=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\psi_{i}.

Assume that there exists
i\in\left\{1,\ldots,N\right\}
such that
\lvert V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*})\lvert<\nu_{i}
. Then by the continuity of
F(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},\cdot)
, there exists
t>0
such that

\lvert V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}+t\mathbf{e}_{i})\lvert<\nu_{i}
, and by the foregoing observations, one also has:

\lvert V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*}+t\mathbf{e}_{i})\lvert\>\leq\>\lvert V_{j}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*})\lvert\>\leq\>\nu_{j}\text{ for }j=1,\ldots,N,\>\>j\neq i.

Hence, the vector
({\bm{\psi}}^{*}+t\mathbf{e}_{i})
also belongs to
{\mathcal{K}}
and
W({\bm{\psi}}^{*}+t\mathbf{e}_{i})>W({\bm{\psi}}^{*})
,
which contradicts the definition of
{\bm{\psi}}^{*}
. We have thus proved that any maximizer
{\bm{\psi}}^{*}
in
C.3
satisfies the desired property:

\lvert V_{i}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\psi}}^{*})\lvert=\nu_{i},\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,N.

Step 3: We prove the uniqueness of
{\bm{\psi}}^{*}
under the genericity assumptions
G1
,
G2
,
3.10
,
3.11
,
3.12
,
3.13
and
3.14
.

We have seen in
Corollary

B.1
that under the assumptions
G1
,
G2
,
3.10
,
3.11
,
3.12
,
3.13
and
3.14
,
the mapping
{\bm{\psi}}\mapsto\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}})
is differentiable at
{\bm{\psi}}^{*}
, and that the derivative
\left[\nabla_{\bm{\psi}}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{*})\right]
is an invertible
N\times N
matrix.

Let us assume that there exists another weight vector
{\bm{\varphi}}^{*}
satisfying
3.16
. Then
{\bm{\psi}}^{*}
and
{\bm{\varphi}}^{*}
are both maximizers of the concave Kantorovic functional
K(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},\cdot)
, and so, for any
t\in(0,1)
, it holds:

\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},(1-t){\bm{\psi}}^{*}+t{\bm{\varphi}}^{*})=\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{*})=\bm{0}.

Rearranging the above equality, dividing both sides by
t
, then letting
t
tend to
0
, we obtain:

\left[\nabla_{\bm{\psi}}\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{s},{\bm{\nu}},{\bm{\psi}}^{*})\right]({\bm{\varphi}}^{*}-{\bm{\psi}}^{*})=\bm{0},

which proves that
{\bm{\psi}}^{*}={\bm{\varphi}}^{*}
, as expected.

Step 4: We use duality to conclude that
3.20
is the optimal transport mapping between
\mu
and
\nu
.

Let us recall the well-known expression of the dual maximization problem attached to the Kantorovic minimization problem
OT-K
:

(
OT-D
)

\max\limits_{\psi\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)}\left(\int_{X}\min\limits_{\mathbf{y}\in X}\Big(c(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\psi(\mathbf{y})\Big)\>\text{\rm d}\mu(\mathbf{y})+\nu_{0}\frac{1}{|D|}\int_{X}\psi(\mathbf{y})\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{y}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\nu_{i}\psi_{i}\right),

see e.g.
[
107
]
.
The weak duality phenomenon reads, in the present situation:

(C.4)

\max\>\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{eq.OTD}\leq\min\>\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{eq.OTKanto}.

Note that actually, strong duality holds, i.e.
\max\>\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{eq.OTD}=\min\>\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{eq.OTKanto}
, see Th. 1.39 in
[
107
]
. We shall not need this fact, which will actually turn out to be a by-product of our analysis.

Let
{\bm{\psi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
be the weight vector supplied by the second step, guaranteeing the equality
3.16
,
and let
T_{\bm{\psi}}
be the mapping defined in
3.20
; we recall from Step 2 that
\psi_{i}>0
for all
i=1,\ldots,N
and we retain the convention
\psi_{0}=0
. By construction,
(T_{\bm{\psi}})_{\#}\mu=\nu
, so that
(\text{\rm Id},T_{\bm{\psi}})_{\#}\mu
is an admissible transport plan in the problem
OT-K
; hence:

\min\>\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{eq.OTKanto}\leq\int_{X}c(\mathbf{x},T_{\bm{\psi}}(\mathbf{x}))\>\text{\rm d}\mu(\mathbf{x}).

On the other hand, let us define the (discontinuous) function
\widetilde{\psi}:X\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

\forall\mathbf{y}\in X,\quad\widetilde{\psi}(\mathbf{y})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\psi_{i}&\text{if }\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{s}_{i},\>i=1,\ldots,N,\\
\psi_{0}=0&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

It follows from the definition of
T_{\bm{\psi}}
that:

\forall\mathbf{x}\in X,\quad c(\mathbf{x},T_{\bm{\psi}}(\mathbf{x}))-\widetilde{\psi}(T_{\bm{\psi}}(\mathbf{x}))=\min\limits_{\mathbf{y}\in X}\Big(c(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\widetilde{\psi}(\mathbf{y})\Big).

Integrating this identity, we obtain:

(C.5)

\int_{X}c(\mathbf{x},T_{\bm{\psi}}(\mathbf{x}))\>\text{\rm d}\mu(\mathbf{x})-\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\nu_{i}\psi_{i}=\int_{X}\min\limits_{\mathbf{y}\in X}\Big(c(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\widetilde{\psi}(\mathbf{y})\Big)\>\text{\rm d}\mu(\mathbf{x}).

We now approximate the discontinuous function
\widetilde{\psi}
in the last term in the above right-hand side.
To this end, let us first introduce the step function
\chi_{\varepsilon}
defined for
\varepsilon>0
small enough by

\forall\mathbf{y}\in X,\quad\chi_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{y})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\psi_{i}&\text{if }\lvert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert<\varepsilon\text{ for some } i=1,\ldots,N,\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

We also introduce one function
\zeta_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
satisfying the properties:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\zeta_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{y})=\psi_{i}&\text{if } \lvert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\text{ for } i=1,\ldots,N,\\
0\leq\zeta_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{y})\leq\psi_{i}&\text{if } \frac{\varepsilon}{2} \leq\lvert\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{s}_{i}\lvert<\varepsilon\text{ for } i=1,\ldots,N,\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{array}\right.

i.e.
\zeta_{\varepsilon}
is a smoothed version of
\widetilde{\psi}
, whose support is contained in a collection of balls of radius
\varepsilon
around the
\mathbf{s}_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,N
;
the latter can easily be constructed thanks to mollifiers.
Then, it holds:

\min\limits_{\mathbf{y}\in X}\Big(c(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\chi_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{y})\Big)\leq\min\limits_{\mathbf{y}\in X}\Big(c(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\zeta_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{y})\Big)\leq\min\limits_{\mathbf{y}\in X}\Big(c(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\widetilde{\psi}(\mathbf{y})\Big).

On the other hand, the first term in the above left-hand side can be estimated as:

\begin{array}[]{ccl}\min\limits_{\mathbf{y}\in X}\Big(c(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\chi_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{y})\Big)&\geq&\min\Big(0,\min\limits_{i=1,\ldots,N\atop\mathbf{h}\in B(0,\varepsilon)}\Big(c(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{s}_{i}+\mathbf{h})-\psi_{i}\Big)\Big)\\
&=&\min\Big(0,\min\limits_{i=1,\ldots,N\atop\mathbf{h}\in B(0,\varepsilon)}\Big(c(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{h},\mathbf{s}_{i})-\psi_{i}\Big)\Big)\\
&\geq&\min\Big(0,\min\limits_{i=1,\ldots,N}\Big(c(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{s}_{i})-\psi_{i}\Big)\Big)-L\varepsilon\\
&=&\min\limits_{\mathbf{y}\in X}\Big(c(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\widetilde{\psi}(\mathbf{y})\Big)-L\varepsilon,\end{array}

where
L
is a Lipschitz constant for the cost
c(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\lvert\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\lvert^{2}
on
X\times X
, and we have used the particular difference structure of the latter when passing from the first to the second line.
It follows that:

\min\limits_{\mathbf{y}\in X}\Big(c(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\widetilde{\psi}(\mathbf{y})\Big)\leq\min\limits_{\mathbf{y}\in X}\Big(c(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\zeta_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{y})\Big)+L\varepsilon,

and so, returning to
C.5
:

\begin{array}[]{>{\displaystyle}cc>{\displaystyle}l}\int_{X}c(\mathbf{x},T_{\bm{\psi}}(\mathbf{x}))\>\text{\rm d}\mu(\mathbf{x})&\leq&\int_{X}\min\limits_{\mathbf{y}\in X}\Big(c(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\zeta_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{y})\Big)\>\text{\rm d}\mu(\mathbf{x})+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\nu_{i}\psi_{i}+L\varepsilon\\
&\leq&\int_{X}\min\limits_{\mathbf{y}\in X}\Big(c(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\zeta_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{y})\Big)\>\text{\rm d}\mu(\mathbf{x})+\nu_{0}\frac{1}{|D|}\int_{X}\zeta_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{y})\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{y}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\nu_{i}\psi_{i}+L\varepsilon\\
&\leq&\max\>\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{eq.OTD}+L\varepsilon.\end{array}

Since
\varepsilon
is arbitrary, we may let
\varepsilon
tend to
0
in the above inequality; recalling the weak duality inequality
C.4
, we have thus proved that
T_{\bm{\psi}}
is indeed an optimal transport mapping.

The uniqueness of such an optimal transport mapping is a classical fact in optimal transport theory, see e.g. Th. 1.17 in
[
107
]
.
This concludes the proof of
Theorem

3.1
.

Appendix D
Implementation details of the Virtual Element Method

In this section, we describe the practical implementation of the Virtual Element Method for the solution of the conductivity equation
Cond
and of the linear elasticity system
Elas
.

D.1.
The case of the conductivity equation

Slipping into the notation of
Section

5.2
and closely following
[
120
]
, we now provide a little details about the computation of the entries of the local stiffness matrix
K^{E}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n^{E}\times n^{E}}
defined by

\forall i,j=1,\ldots,n,\quad K^{E}_{ij}=\widetilde{a}^{E}(\zeta_{i},\zeta_{j}),

where the discrete bilinear form
\widetilde{a}^{E}(u,v)
reads

\forall u,v\in{\mathcal{W}}(E),\quad\widetilde{a}^{E}(u,v)=a^{E}(\pi_{C}u,\pi_{C}v)+\widetilde{s}^{E}(u-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}u,v-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}v),

and the basis
\left\{\zeta_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,n^{E}}
of
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
is characterized by
5.10
.
As discussed in
Section

5.2
, this formula induces a decomposition of
K^{E}
as the sum of two contributions:

K^{E}=P^{E}+\widetilde{S}^{E},\text{ where }P^{E}_{ij}:=a^{E}(\pi_{C}\zeta_{i},\pi_{C}\zeta_{j})\text{ and }\widetilde{S}^{E}_{ij}=\widetilde{s}^{E}(\zeta_{i}-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\zeta_{i},\zeta_{j}-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\zeta_{j}).

The cornerstone of the calculation of both matrices is therefore the calculation of the matrix
\Pi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3\times n^{E}}
of the projection operator
\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}
onto affine functions, expressed in the bases
\left\{\zeta_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,n^{E}}
of
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
and
\left\{m_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha=1,2,3}
of
{\mathcal{P}}(E)
, i.e.
the
i^{\text{th}}
column of
\Pi
gathers the entries of
\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\zeta_{i}
in the basis
m_{\alpha}
:

\pi_{\mathcal{P}}\zeta_{i}(\mathbf{x})=\sum\limits_{\alpha=1}^{3}\Pi_{\alpha i}m_{\alpha}(\mathbf{x}),\quad i=1,\ldots,n^{E},\quad\mathbf{x}\in E.

By using the following characterization of
\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}
,

\overline{\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\zeta_{i}}=\overline{\zeta_{i}}=\frac{1}{n^{E}}\text{ and }a^{E}(\pi_{\mathcal{P}}\zeta_{i},m_{\alpha})=a^{E}(\zeta_{i},m_{\alpha}),\>\>\alpha=2,3,

we obtain the identity:

(D.1)

\widetilde{G}\Pi=B,

where

•

The matrix
\widetilde{G}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3\times 3}
is defined by:

\widetilde{G}_{11}=1\text{ and }\widetilde{G}_{12}=\widetilde{G}_{13}=0,

as well as:

\widetilde{G}_{\alpha,\beta}=\int_{E}\nabla m_{\alpha}\cdot\nabla m_{\beta}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}\text{ for }\alpha=2,3\text{ and }\beta=1,2,3.

•

The matrix
B\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3\times n^{E}}
is given by:

B_{1i}=\frac{1}{n^{E}},\text{ and }B_{\alpha i}=\int_{E}\nabla\zeta_{i}\cdot\nabla m_{\alpha}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,n^{E}\text{ and }\alpha=2,3.

The entries of
B
can be calculated in closed form by using the facts that
\nabla m_{\alpha}
is constant on
E
and that the basis functions
\zeta_{i}
are affine on each edge of
\partial E
. Indeed, Green’s formula implies that, for
\alpha=2,3
:

B_{\alpha,i}=\left(\int_{E}\nabla\zeta_{i}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}\right)\cdot\nabla m_{\alpha}=\left(\int_{\partial E}\zeta_{i}\mathbf{n}\>\text{\rm d}\ell\right)\cdot\nabla m_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{2}\lvert\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}\lvert\mathbf{n}_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}}\cdot\nabla m_{\alpha}.

Hence, the system
D.1
features the explicit matrix
\widetilde{G}
and right-hand side
B
, and it can be solved for the matrix
\Pi
.
The practical implementation is further simplified thanks to the introduction of
the matrix
D\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n^{E}\times 3}
corresponding to the expression of the polynomials
m_{\alpha}
,
\alpha=1,2,3
in the basis
\left\{\zeta_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,n^{E}}
of
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
, that is:

m_{\alpha}(\mathbf{x})=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n^{E}}D_{i\alpha}\zeta_{i}(\mathbf{x}),\quad\mathbf{x}\in E.

The coefficients of this matrix read, by definition of the
\zeta_{i}
:

D_{i\alpha}=m_{\alpha}(\mathbf{q}_{i}^{E}),

and so

D_{i1}=1,\text{, }D_{i2}=q^{E}_{i,1}-\overline{q_{1}^{E}},\text{ and }D_{i3}=q^{E}_{i,2}-\overline{q_{2}^{E}}\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,n^{E}.

The definitions of the matrices
\widetilde{G}
,
B
and
D
yield the following relation:

\widetilde{G}=BD.

Indeed, elementary calculations lead to:

\widetilde{G}_{11}=1=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n^{E}}B_{1i}D_{i1},\>\>\widetilde{G}_{1\beta}=0=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n^{E}}B_{1i}D_{i\beta}\text{ for }\beta=2,3,

and likewise, for
\alpha=2,3
,

\widetilde{G}_{\alpha\beta}=\int_{E}\nabla m_{\alpha}\cdot\nabla m_{\beta}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n^{E}}D_{i\beta}\int_{E}\nabla m_{\alpha}\cdot\nabla\zeta_{i}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n^{E}}D_{i\beta}B_{\alpha i}.

Once the matrix
\Pi
is calculated,
P^{E}
is inferred from the formula:

(D.2)

P^{E}=\gamma\Pi^{T}G\Pi,\text{ where }G\text{ is the matrix with entries }G_{\alpha\beta}=\int_{E}\nabla m_{\alpha}\cdot\nabla m_{\beta}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x},\quad\alpha,\beta=1,2,3.

Likewise,
\widetilde{S}^{E}
is obtained as:

(D.3)

\widetilde{S}^{E}=\alpha^{E}\Big(\text{\rm I}-D\Pi\Big)^{T}\Big(\text{\rm I}-D\Pi\Big),\text{ where }\alpha^{E}=1,

and
I
is the identity matrix with size
n^{E}\times n^{E}
.

Summarizing, the computations of the matrices
P^{E}
and
S^{E}
(and thus
K^{E}
) proceed as follows:

(1)

Assemble the matrices
B
and
D
;

(2)

Calculate
\widetilde{G}=BD\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3\times 3}
;

(3)

Compute
\Pi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3\times n^{E}}
as the solution to the matrix system

\widetilde{G}\Pi=B.

(4)

The matrice
P^{E}
and
\widetilde{S}^{E}
are inferred from the formulas
D.2
and
D.3
.

Eventually, the entries
F^{E}_{i}
of the local force vector
F^{E}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n^{E}}
are simply approximated as:

F^{E}_{i}\>\>=\>\>\int_{E}f\zeta_{i}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}\>\>\approx\>\>f(\overline{\mathbf{q}^{E}})\int_{E}\zeta_{i}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}\>\>\approx\>\>f(\overline{\mathbf{q}^{E}})\frac{\lvert E\lvert}{n^{E}},\quad i=1,\ldots,n^{E}.

Remark D.1
.

An important consequence of the above analysis is that the projection
\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\zeta
of a function
\zeta\in{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
onto affine functions depends only on the values
\zeta(\mathbf{q}_{i}^{E})
(
i=1,\ldots,n^{E}
) of
\zeta
at the vertices of the considered element
E

D.2.
The case of the linear elasticity system

The present section deals with the linear elasticity system
Elas
.
Relying on
[
18
,
57
]
, we briefly describe its numerical solution by the Virtual Element Method.

As emphasized in
Sections

5
and
D.1
, the most critical operation in this perspective is the calculation of the local stiffness matrix
K^{E}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n^{E}\times 2n^{E}}
defined by

\forall i,j=1,\ldots,2n^{E},\quad K^{E}_{ij}=\widetilde{a}^{E}({\bm{\zeta}}_{i},{\bm{\zeta}}_{j}),

where
\widetilde{a}^{E}
is given by:

\forall u,v\in{\mathcal{W}}(E),\quad\widetilde{a}^{E}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=a^{E}(\pi_{C}\mathbf{u},\pi_{C}\mathbf{v})+\widetilde{s}^{E}(\mathbf{u}-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\mathbf{v}),

and the basis functions
{\bm{\zeta}}_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,2n^{E}
are characterized by
5.14
and
5.15
.
Again, we decompose
K^{E}
as:

K^{E}=P^{E}+\widetilde{S}^{E},\text{ where }P^{E},\widetilde{S}^{E}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n^{E}\times 2n^{E}}\text{ are the matrices }\\
P^{E}_{ij}:=a^{E}(\pi_{C}{\bm{\zeta}}_{i},\pi_{C}{\bm{\zeta}}_{j})\text{ and }\widetilde{S}^{E}_{ij}:=\widetilde{s}^{E}({\bm{\zeta}}_{i}-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}{\bm{\zeta}}_{i},{\bm{\zeta}}_{j}-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}{\bm{\zeta}}_{j}).

Let
W_{C}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n^{E}\times 3}
be the transpose of the matrix of
\pi_{C}
in the bases
\left\{{\bm{\zeta}}_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,2n^{E}}
and
\left\{\mathbf{c}_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha=1,2,3}
, i.e. the
i^{\text{th}}
line of
W_{C}
gathers the coordinates of the projection
\pi_{C}{\bm{\zeta}}_{i}
over the functions

\mathbf{c}_{1},\mathbf{c}_{2},\mathbf{c}_{3}
in
5.17
:

\forall i=1,\ldots,2n^{E},\quad\pi_{C}{\bm{\zeta}}_{i}(\mathbf{x})=\sum\limits_{\beta=1}^{3}({W_{C}})_{i\beta}\mathbf{c}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x}),\quad\mathbf{x}\in E.

Likewise, let
W_{R}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n^{E}\times 3}
be the transpose of the matrix of
\pi_{R}
in the bases
\left\{{\bm{\zeta}}_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,2n}
and
\left\{\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha=1,2,3}
:

\forall i=1,\ldots,2n^{E},\quad\pi_{R}{\bm{\zeta}}_{i}(\mathbf{x})=\sum\limits_{\beta=1}^{3}({W_{R}})_{i\beta}\mathbf{r}_{\beta}(\mathbf{x}).

With these notations, the first block
P^{E}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n^{E}\times 2n^{E}}
of
K^{E}
reads,

P^{E}_{ij}=a^{E}(\pi_{C}{\bm{\zeta}}_{i},\pi_{C}{\bm{\zeta}}_{j})=\sum\limits_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{3}(W_{C})_{i\alpha}(W_{C})_{j\beta}\>a^{E}(\mathbf{c}_{\alpha},\mathbf{c}_{\beta}),

and so, in matrix form:

(D.4)

P^{E}=W_{C}DW_{C}^{T},\text{ where }D\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3\times 3}\text{ is defined by }D_{\alpha\beta}=a^{E}(\mathbf{c}_{\alpha},\mathbf{c}_{\beta})=\int_{E}Ae(\mathbf{c}_{\alpha}):e(\mathbf{c}_{\beta})\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}.

An elementary calculation yields:

D=|E|\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}2\mu+\lambda&\lambda&0\\
\lambda&2\mu+\lambda&0\\
0&0&4\mu\end{array}\right),

and we shall detail below how to calculate the matrices
W_{C}
and
W_{R}
in practice.

As far as the stabilizing block
\widetilde{S}^{E}
of
K^{E}
is concerned, we need to calculate the matrices
P_{C}
and
P_{R}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n^{E}\times 2n^{E}}
of the projections
\pi_{C}:{\mathcal{W}}(E)\to{\mathcal{W}}(E)
and
\pi_{R}:{\mathcal{W}}(E)\to{\mathcal{W}}(E)

in the basis
\left\{{\bm{\zeta}}_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,2n^{E}}
.
To this end, let us introduce the matrix
N_{C}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n^{E}\times 3}
, whose
\alpha^{\text{th}}
column (
\alpha=1,2,3
) contains the coordinates of
\mathbf{c}_{\alpha}
over the basis
\left\{{\bm{\zeta}}_{i}\right\}
, that is:

\forall\alpha=1,2,3,\quad\mathbf{c}_{\alpha}(\mathbf{x})=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{2n^{E}}(N_{C})_{i\alpha}{\bm{\zeta}}_{i}(\mathbf{x}),\quad\mathbf{x}\in E.

With these notations, it holds, for
j=1,\ldots,2n^{E}
:

\begin{array}[]{>{\displaystyle}cc>{\displaystyle}l}\pi_{C}{\bm{\zeta}}_{j}&=&\sum\limits_{\alpha=1}^{3}(W_{C})_{j\alpha}\mathbf{c}_{\alpha}\\
&=&\sum\limits_{i=1}^{2n^{E}}\left(\sum\limits_{\alpha=1}^{3}(N_{C})_{i\alpha}(W_{C})_{j\alpha}\right){\bm{\zeta}}_{i}\end{array}

and so,
P_{C}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n^{E}\times 2n^{E}}
reads:

P_{C}=N_{C}W_{C}^{T}.

Likewise, let
N_{R}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n^{E}\times 3}
be the matrix whose
\alpha^{\text{th}}
column (
\alpha=1,2,3
) contains the coordinates of
\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}
in the basis
{\bm{\zeta}}_{i}
, that is:

\forall\alpha=1,2,3,\quad\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}(\mathbf{x})=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{2n^{E}}(N_{R})_{i\alpha}{\bm{\zeta}}_{i}(\mathbf{x}),\quad\mathbf{x}\in E;

it holds:

(D.5)

P_{R}=N_{R}W_{R}^{T}.

Finally, the matrix
P_{P}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n^{E}\times 2n^{E}}
of the projection
\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}
over the space
{\mathcal{P}}(E)
of affine functions reads:

(D.6)

P_{P}=P_{R}+P_{C}.

With these notations,
\widetilde{S}^{E}
can be computed via the following formula:

(D.7)

S^{E}=\alpha^{E}(\text{\rm I}-P_{P})^{T}(\text{\rm I}-P_{P}),

where
I
is the
2n^{E}\times 2n^{E}
identity matrix.

It follows from the formulas
D.4
,
D.5
,
D.6
and
D.7
that the basic ingredients of the practical implementation of the Virtual Element Method dedicated to the solution of the linear elasticity system
Elas
are the assembly of the matrices
N_{C},N_{R},W_{C},W_{R}
, and the calculation of the coefficient
\alpha^{E}
; we now detail these operations.

Assembly of the matrices
W_{C},W_{R}

For
i=1,\ldots,n^{E}
, let us introduce the vector
\mathbf{a}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
defined by

\mathbf{a}_{i}=\frac{|\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}|}{2|E|}\mathbf{n}_{\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}},

where we recall the notation
\mathbf{n}_{\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}}
in
5.1
.

Let us first consider the matrix
W_{R}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n^{E}\times 3}
; for
i=1,\ldots,n^{E}
and
\mathbf{x}\in E
, we have

\pi_{R}{\bm{\zeta}}_{2i-1}(\mathbf{x})=\overline{{\bm{\zeta}}_{2i-1}}+\langle\omega({\bm{\zeta}}_{2i-1})\rangle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-(x_{2}-\overline{q_{2}^{E}})\\
x_{1}-\overline{q_{1}^{E}}\end{array}\right),

where obviously,
\overline{{\bm{\zeta}}_{2i-1}}=\frac{1}{n^{E}}\mathbf{r}_{1}
and a simple calculation shows that:

\begin{array}[]{>{\displaystyle}cc>{\displaystyle}l}\langle\omega({\bm{\zeta}}_{2i-1})\rangle&=&-\frac{1}{2|E|}\int_{E}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}({\bm{\zeta}}_{2i-1})_{1}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}\\[10.00002pt]
&=&-\frac{1}{2|E|}\int_{\partial E}({\bm{\zeta}}_{2i-1})_{1}n_{2}\>\text{\rm d}\ell\\[10.00002pt]
&=&-\frac{1}{4|E|}(|\mathbf{e}_{i-1}|n_{\mathbf{e}_{i-1}}+|\mathbf{e}_{i}|n_{\mathbf{e}_{i}})_{2}\\[10.00002pt]
&=&-\frac{1}{|E|}\frac{|\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}|}{4}(n_{\widehat{\mathbf{e}_{i}}})_{2}\\[10.00002pt]
&=&-\frac{1}{2}(a_{i})_{2}.\end{array}

Hence, it follows:

\pi_{R}{\bm{\zeta}}_{2i-1}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{n^{E}}\mathbf{r}_{1}(\mathbf{x})-\frac{1}{2}(a_{i})_{2}\>\mathbf{r}_{3}(\mathbf{x}).

A similar calculation yields:

\pi_{R}{\bm{\zeta}}_{2i}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{n^{E}}\mathbf{r}_{2}(\mathbf{x})+\frac{1}{2}(a_{i})_{1}\>\mathbf{r}_{3}(\mathbf{x}).

Likewise, considering the entries of
W_{C}
, we obtain:

\pi_{C}{\bm{\zeta}}_{2i-1}(\mathbf{x})=(a_{i})_{1}\mathbf{c}_{1}(\mathbf{x})+\frac{1}{2}(a_{i})_{2}\>\mathbf{c}_{3}(\mathbf{x}),\text{ and }\pi_{C}{\bm{\zeta}}_{2i}(\mathbf{x})=(a_{i})_{2}\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mathbf{x})+\frac{1}{2}(a_{i})_{1}\>\mathbf{c}_{3}(\mathbf{x}).

Summarizing, the matrices
W_{R}
and
W_{C}
read, in the respective bases
\left\{\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha=1,2,3}
,
\left\{{\bm{\zeta}}_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,2n^{E}}
and
\left\{\mathbf{c}_{\alpha}\right\}_{j=1,2,3}
,
\left\{{\bm{\zeta}}_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,2n^{E}}
:

W_{R}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\\
\frac{1}{n^{E}}&\>\>\>0&\>\>\>-\frac{1}{2}(a_{i})_{2}\\[5.69054pt]
0&\>\>\>\frac{1}{n^{E}}&\>\>\>\frac{1}{2}(a_{i})_{1}\\
\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\end{array}\right),\text{ and }W_{C}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\\
(a_{i})_{1}& \>\>\>0& \>\>\>\frac{1}{2}(a_{i})_{2}\\[5.69054pt]
0& \>\>\>(a_{i})_{2}& \>\>\>\frac{1}{2}(a_{i})_{1}\\
\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\end{array}\right).

Assembly of the matrices
N_{C},N_{R}

It follows from the definition
5.14
and
5.15
of the functions
{\bm{\zeta}}_{i}
that:

\mathbf{c}_{\alpha}(\mathbf{x})=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n^{E}}(c_{\alpha}(\mathbf{q}_{i}^{E}))_{1}\>{\bm{\zeta}}_{2i-1}(\mathbf{x})+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n^{E}}(c_{\alpha}(\mathbf{q}_{i}^{E}))_{2}\>{\bm{\zeta}}_{2i}(\mathbf{x}),\quad\mathbf{x}\in E.

Hence, a simple calculation based on the explicit form
5.17
of the basis functions
\mathbf{c}_{j}
reveals that:

N_{C}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\\
(c_{1}(\mathbf{q}_{i}^{E}))_{1}&\>\>\>(c_{2}(\mathbf{q}_{i}^{E}))_{1}&\>\>\>(c_{3}(\mathbf{q}_{i}^{E}))_{1}\\[5.69054pt]
(c_{1}(\mathbf{q}_{i}^{E}))_{2}&\>\>\>(c_{2}(\mathbf{q}_{i}^{E}))_{2}&\>\>\>(c_{3}(\mathbf{q}_{i}^{E}))_{2}\\
\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\\
q_{i,1}^{E}-\overline{q_{1}^{E}}&\>\>\>0&\>\>\>q_{i,2}^{E}-\overline{q_{2}^{E}}\\[5.69054pt]
0&\>\>\>q_{i,2}^{E}-\overline{q_{2}^{E}}&\>\>\>q_{i,1}^{E}-\overline{q_{1}^{E}}\\
\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\end{array}\right).

Similarly, the following expression of
N_{R}
is derived:

N_{R}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\\
1&\>\>\>0&\>\>\>-q_{i,2}^{E}-\overline{q_{2}^{E}}\\[5.69054pt]
0&\>\>\>1&\>\>\>q_{i,1}^{E}-\overline{q_{1}^{E}}\\
\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\end{array}\right).

Calculation of
\alpha^{E}

As we have mentioned, the coefficient
\alpha^{E}
is chosen so as to ensure the stability of the method.
The idea consists in taking
\alpha^{E}
such that the block
\left\{s^{E}(\mathbf{c}_{k},\mathbf{c}_{l})\right\}_{k,l=1,2,3}
,
corresponding to the application of the stabilizing form
\widetilde{s}^{E}
to constant strain fields,
scale like
\left\{a^{E}(\mathbf{c}_{k},\mathbf{c}_{l})\right\}_{k,l=1,2,3}
, which is exactly the matrix
D
, when the mesh
{\mathcal{T}}
is refined.
Hence, we require that

\widetilde{s}^{E}(\mathbf{c}_{k},\mathbf{c}_{l})=\alpha^{E}(N_{C}^{T}N_{C})_{kl}
be comparable with
D_{kl}
,
which motivates the choice

\alpha^{E}=\frac{\text{\rm tr}(D)}{\text{\rm tr}(N_{C}^{T}N_{C})}.

Eventually, let us mention that the approximation of the local force vector
F^{E}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n^{E}}
whose entries equal

F^{E}_{i}=\int_{E}\mathbf{f}\cdot{\bm{\zeta}}_{i}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}+\int_{\partial E\cap\Gamma_{N}}\mathbf{g}\cdot{\bm{\zeta}}_{i}\>\text{\rm d}\ell,\quad i=1,\ldots,2n^{E}

is realized in a completely similar fashion as in the case of the conductivity equation, see
Section

D.1
.

D.3.
Addition of a term of order
0

For the sake of simplicity and without loss of generality, let us return to the setting of the conductivity equation
Cond
of
Section

2.2.1
.
In multiple applications, starting from the solution of eigenvalue problems of the form
2.5
(see
Section

7.2
for a numerical example), the numerical implementation requires to calculate the mass matrix
M_{{\mathcal{T}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{M\times M}
in addition to the stiffness matrix
K_{{\mathcal{T}}}
in
5.8
. This matrix
M_{{\mathcal{T}}}
is defined by:

\forall k,l=1,\ldots,M,\quad M_{{\mathcal{T}},kl}=\int_{\Omega}\varphi_{k}\varphi_{l}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x},

where the basis
\left\{\varphi_{k}\right\}_{k=1,\ldots,M}
of the virtual element space
{\mathcal{W}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
is that characterized by
5.6
.
Arguing as in
Section

5.2.1
,
M_{{\mathcal{T}}}
is assembled from the local contributions
M^{E}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n^{E}\times n^{E}}
attached to the individual elements
E\in{\mathcal{T}}
, defined by:

(D.8)

M^{E}_{ij}=\int_{E}\zeta_{i}\zeta_{j}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}.

To achieve this computation, we introduce the
L^{2}
projector
\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}^{0}:{\mathcal{W}}(E)\to{\mathcal{P}}(E)
over
{\mathcal{P}}(E)
, defined by

\forall m\in{\mathcal{P}}(E),\quad\int_{E}(\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}^{0}\zeta)m\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}=\int_{E}\zeta m\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}.

Unfortunately, this operator cannot be expressed in closed form, as the last term in the above right-hand side cannot be readily computed from the degrees of freedom of the function
\zeta
.

To overcome this issue, we leverage an elegant observation from
[
1
]
whereby a slight modification of the local space
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
in
Section

5.2.2
, which is completely transparent in the numerical implementation, enables this computation at no additional cost.
Let us first define the larger space
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}(E)
of local functions attached to
E
by:

\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}(E)=\Big\{\zeta:E\to{\mathbb{R}},\>\>\zeta\text{ is affine on each edge of }\partial E,\>\>-\Delta\zeta\text{ is an affine function on }E\Big\}.

We note that the projection operator
\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}:{\mathcal{W}}(E)\to{\mathcal{P}}(E)
introduced in
5.12
can actually be extended as
\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}:\widetilde{{\mathcal{W}}}(E)\to{\mathcal{P}}(E)
;
actually, for a given function
\zeta\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{W}}}(E)
, the same calculations as in the previous
Section

D.1
reveal that
\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\zeta
can still be calculated from the sole values of
\zeta
at the vertices of
E
.
We now introduce the subspace
{\mathcal{Z}}(E)\subset\widetilde{{\mathcal{W}}}(E)
defined by:

(D.9)

{\mathcal{Z}}(E)=\Big\{\zeta:E\to{\mathbb{R}},\>\>\zeta\text{ is affine on each edge of }\partial E,\>\>-\Delta\zeta\text{ is affine on }E,\text{ and }\\
\forall m\in{\mathcal{P}}(E),\>\>\int_{E}\zeta m\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}=\int_{E}(\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\zeta)m\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}\Big\}.

The key remark about
{\mathcal{Z}}(E)
is that its elements can be characterized by the exact same degrees of freedom as those of the space
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
. Before proceeding, let us provide a simple technical fact:

Lemma D.1
.

The mapping
L:=\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}^{0}\circ\Delta^{-1}:{\mathcal{P}}(E)\to{\mathcal{P}}(E)
is an isomorphism,
where
\Delta
stands for the Laplace operator on
E
with homogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions.

Proof.

Since
{\mathcal{P}}(E)
is a finite-dimensional space, it is enough to prove that
L
is injective.
Let then
m\in{\mathcal{P}}(E)
be such that
Lm=0
, and let
q=\Delta^{-1}m\in H^{1}_{0}(E)
be the function defined by

\forall w\in H^{1}_{0}(E),\quad\int_{E}\nabla q\cdot\nabla w\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}=\int_{E}mw\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}.

It follows:

0=\int_{E}Lm\>m\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}=\int_{E}q\>m\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}=\int_{E}\lvert\nabla q\lvert^{2}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}.

Hence,
q=0
, and so
m=0
, as expected.
∎

The result of interest is now the following.

Lemma D.2
.

For all
i=1,\ldots,n^{E}
, there exists a unique function
\zeta_{i}\in{\mathcal{Z}}(E)
such that

(D.10)

\zeta_{i}(\mathbf{q}_{j}^{E})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}1&\text{if }i=j,\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

Let
i\in\left\{1,\ldots,n^{E}\right\}
be an arbitrary index;
we first assume that there exists one function
\zeta\in{\mathcal{Z}}(E)

satisfying
D.10
, and we prove that it is necessarily unique. To this end, let
m:=\Delta\zeta\in{\mathcal{P}}(E)
, and let us introduce the unique function
\widetilde{\zeta}_{i}\in{\mathcal{W}}(E)
such that
\widetilde{\zeta}_{i}(\mathbf{q}_{j}^{E})=1
if
i=j
and
0
otherwise, see
Section

5.2.2
.
As noted in
Remark

D.1
, the functions
\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\zeta
and
\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\widetilde{\zeta}_{i}
coincide.
Then, introducing the basis
\left\{m_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha=1,2,3}
of
{\mathcal{P}}(E)
defined in
Section

5.2.2
, the difference
r=\zeta-\widetilde{\zeta}_{i}
satisfies:

\begin{array}[]{>{\displaystyle}cc>{\displaystyle}l}r=0\text{ on }\partial E,\>\>\Delta r=m,\text{ and for }\alpha=1,2,3,\quad\int_{E}rm_{\alpha}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}&=&\int_{E}\zeta m_{\alpha}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}-\int_{E}\widetilde{\zeta_{i}}m_{\alpha}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x},\\[10.00002pt]
&=&\int_{E}(\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\zeta-\pi^{0}_{{\mathcal{P}}}\widetilde{\zeta_{i}})m_{\alpha}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}\\[10.00002pt]
&=&\int_{E}(\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\widetilde{\zeta_{i}}-\pi^{0}_{{\mathcal{P}}}\widetilde{\zeta_{i}})m_{\alpha}\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x},\end{array}

where the second line follows from the definition
D.9
of
{\mathcal{Z}}(E)
and the final line is a consequence of the equality between
\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\zeta
and
\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\widetilde{\zeta}_{i}
.
It follows from this discussion and the previous
Lemma

D.1
that:

\Delta\zeta=L^{-1}\Big(\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\widetilde{\zeta_{i}}-\pi^{0}_{{\mathcal{P}}}\widetilde{\zeta_{i}}\Big).

Hence,
\zeta
is the solution to the boundary value problem:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}-\Delta\zeta=-L^{-1}(\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\widetilde{\zeta_{i}}-\pi^{0}_{{\mathcal{P}}}\widetilde{\zeta_{i}})&\text{in }E,\\
\zeta=\widetilde{\zeta}^{i}&\text{on }\partial E,\end{array}\right.

so that the function
\zeta
satisfying
D.10
is necessarily unique.

Conversely, defining
\zeta
by the above formula immediately yields one function
\zeta\in{\mathcal{Z}}(E)
satisfying
D.10
, which terminates the proof.
∎

Let us summarize the foregoing discussion:

•

The functions pertaining to
{\mathcal{W}}(E)
and in
{\mathcal{Z}}(E)
can both be characterized by their values at the vertices of
E
:
for any collection of values
\left\{v_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,n^{E}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n^{E}}
, there exist unique functions
w\in{\mathcal{W}}(E)
and
z\in{\mathcal{Z}}(E)
such that:

w(\mathbf{q}_{i}^{E})=v_{i}\text{ and }z(\mathbf{q}_{i}^{E})=v_{i}.

Both functions
w
and
z
are different, but, as noted in
Remark

D.1
, their projections onto affine functions coincide:

\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}z=\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}w.

•

By definition, the projectors
\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}
and
\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}^{0}
coincide on the space
{\mathcal{Z}}(E)
:

\forall\zeta\in{\mathcal{Z}}(E),\quad\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}^{0}\zeta=\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}\zeta.

Introducing the basis
\left\{\zeta_{i}\right\}_{i=1,\ldots,n^{E}}
of the local space
{\mathcal{Z}}(E)
defined in
Lemma

D.2
, the local mass matrix
M^{E}
expressed in terms of this basis reads as in
D.8
.
Like in the previous sections, we may approximate this matrix as:

\forall i,j=1,\ldots,n^{E}\quad M^{E}_{ij}=\int_{E}(\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}^{0}\zeta_{i})(\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}^{0}\zeta_{j})\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}+\int_{E}(\zeta_{i}-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}^{0}\zeta_{i})(\zeta_{j}-\pi_{{\mathcal{P}}}^{0}\zeta_{j})\>\text{\rm d}\mathbf{x}.

Here, the first contribution can be calculated exactly on account of the previous observations;
as for the second one, it can be replaced by a stabilization term, in the same spirit as in
Section

5.2.2
.
